Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. If the game lasts for 106 turns the draw is announced.

Vasya was performing big laboratory work in "Spelling and parts of speech" at night before the game, so he fell asleep at the very beginning of the game. Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves.

Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

Examples

5 6

Win

1 3
1 1
0

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge to a neighboring vertex
5. The player who cannot make a move loses
6. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

1. This is a variant of impartial games (like Nim) where we need to analyze winning and losing positions
2. A position is a "winning" position if the current player can force a win
3. A position is a "losing" position if any move leads to a winning position for the opponent
4. A position is a "draw" if the game can go on indefinitely (cycle detection)

## Solution Strategy

1. **Determine if Petya can win**: We need to find if there's a path from start vertex to a terminal vertex (where no moves are possible) such that after an odd number of moves (since Petya goes first), we reach a dead end.

2. **Detect cycles**: If there's a cycle that we can enter and stay in forever, then the game will draw.

3. **Analysis approach**:
   - A vertex is terminal if it has no outgoing edges
   - A terminal vertex is a losing position for the player whose turn it is
   - We can use DFS with state tracking to find winning paths
   - We need to detect cycles to determine if a draw is possible

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Build the graph from input
2. Find all terminal vertices (vertices with no outgoing edges)
3. Use DFS to determine if we can reach a terminal vertex in an odd number of steps (Petya wins)
4. Detect if there's a cycle reachable from start vertex (draw)
5. If Petya cannot win and there's a cycle, output "Draw"
6. If Petya cannot win and no cycle, output "Lose"

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            for edge in edges:
                graph[i].append(edge)
    
    start = int(input())
    
    # Find terminal vertices (vertices with no outgoing edges)
    terminal = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal.add(i)
    
    # Check if Petya can win (reach a terminal vertex in odd number of moves)
    # Use BFS to find shortest paths to terminal vertices
    # We need to track parity (odd/even) of moves
    
    # BFS with state (vertex, moves_count, path_so_far)
    queue = deque()
    visited = set()
    
    # We'll do BFS to find if we can reach a terminal in odd moves
    # Also detect cycles to determine if draw is possible
    
    # First, find all vertices reachable from start
    reachable = set()
    queue = deque([start])
    reachable.add(start)
    
    # BFS to find all reachable vertices
    while queue:
        v = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in reachable:
                reachable.add(neighbor)
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Check if any terminal vertex is reachable in odd number of moves
    # Use BFS with distance tracking
    distance = {}
    distance[start] = 0
    
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        v = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in distance:
                distance[neighbor] = distance[v] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Check if any terminal vertex is reachable in odd distance from start
    can_win = False
    winning_path = []
    
    # Find a specific winning path
    parent = {}
    queue = deque([start])
    parent[start] = -1
    
    while queue:
        v = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in parent:
                parent[neighbor] = v
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Try to find a path to a terminal vertex with odd length
    for terminal_vertex in terminal:
        if terminal_vertex in distance and distance[terminal_vertex] % 2 == 1:
            # We can win - reconstruct path
            path = []
            current = terminal_vertex
            while current != -1:
                path.append(current)
                current = parent.get(current, -1)
            path.reverse()
            
            # Check if this path is valid (each step has edge)
            valid = True
            for i in range(len(path) - 1):
                if path[i + 1] not in graph[path[i]]:
                    valid = False
                    break
            
            if valid:
                can_win = True
                winning_path = path
    
    # Check if there's a cycle (draw possible)
    # A cycle exists if we can reach a vertex that's already in our reach from start
    # We need to detect a cycle in the path from start to some vertex
    
    # Check if we can stay in a cycle forever
    # If there's a vertex with in-degree or out-degree that creates a cycle
    # and we can reach it from start, then draw is possible
    
    # Detect if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    def has_cycle():
        visited = set()
        rec_stack = set()
        
        def dfs(v):
            if v in rec_stack:
                return True
            if v in visited:
                return False
                
            visited.add(v)
            rec_stack.add(v)
            
            for neighbor in graph[v]:
                if dfs(neighbor):
                    return True
                    
            rec_stack.remove(v)
            return False
        
        for v in reachable:
            if v not in visited:
                if dfs(v):
                    return True
        return False
    
    has_cycle_from_start = has_cycle()
    
    # If Petya can win, output winning path
    if can_win:
        print("Win")
        print(*winning_path)
    # If Petya cannot win but can create a draw (cycle)
    elif has_cycle_from_start:
        print("Draw")
    else:
        print("Lose")

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())

        v = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in parent:
                parent[neighbor] = v
                queue.append(neighbor)

        if terminal_vertex in distance and distance[terminal_vertex] % 2 == 1:
            # Found a winning path - reconstruct it
            path = []
            current = terminal_vertex

                path.append(current)

                    valid = False
                    break
            if valid:
                can_win = True
                winning_path = path

    # A cycle exists if from start, we can reach a vertex that has a path back to itself
    # We need to detect if there's a cycle in the reachable part of the graph
    
    def has_cycle():
        # Use DFS to detect cycle
        visited = set()
        rec_stack = set()
        
        def dfs(v):
            if v in rec_stack:
                return True
            if v in visited:
                return False

            rec_stack.add(v)
            
            for neighbor in graph[v]:
                if dfs(neighbor):
                    return True
                    
            rec_stack.remove(v)
            return False
        
        # Only check vertices that are reachable from start
        for v in distance.keys():
            if v not in visited:
                if dfs(v):
                    return True
        return False
    
    has_cycle_from_start = has_cycle()
    
    if can_win:
        print("Win")

        print("Draw")
    else:
        print("Lose")
```

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)

            edges = line[1:]
            for edge in edges:
                graph[i].append(edge)

    terminal = set()

        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal.add(i)

    # Distance is number of moves from start
    distance = {}
    queue = deque([start])
    distance[start] = 0

        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in distance:
                distance[neighbor] = distance[v] + 1

    can_win = False
    winning_path = []

    queue = deque([start])

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Players take turns moving the chip along edges (Petya first)
4. Player who can't move loses
5. If game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
6. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

This is essentially a game theory problem on a directed graph. The key observations:

1. **Winning condition**: Petya wins if he can force Vasya into a position where Vasya has no moves
2. **Losing condition**: Petya loses if no matter what he does, Vasya can always respond and eventually force Petya into a position with no moves
3. **Draw condition**: Petya can create a cycle that continues indefinitely (game lasts ≥ 10^6 turns)

## Analysis

The game outcome depends on the game state. We can think of this as a variant of Nim or impartial games.

Key insights:
- If from some vertex, Petya can move to a vertex where Vasya has no moves, then Petya wins
- If from every vertex, no matter where Petya moves, Vasya can always respond to reach a winning position for Vasya, then Petya loses
- If there's a cycle that Petya can enter and stay in forever, then it's a draw

We need to determine:
1. Can Petya reach a terminal state (where Vasya has no moves)?
2. Can Petya create a cycle that avoids termination?

## Algorithm Approach

1. **Find terminal vertices**: Vertices with no outgoing edges
2. **Work backwards**: Determine if any vertex leads directly to a terminal vertex (Petya wins)
3. **Check for cycles**: Use DFS to detect cycles. If Petya can enter a cycle, it's a draw
4. **Otherwise**: If Petya can force a win, output the path

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Build the graph from input
2. Identify vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal vertices)
3. Use dynamic programming or game theory state analysis to determine winning/losing positions
4. Check if there's a path from start to terminal vertex that Petya can take
5. Check if there's a cycle reachable from start

Let me implement this:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:]
            for v in vertices:
                graph[i].append(v)
    
    start = int(input())
    
    # Find all terminal vertices (vertices with no outgoing edges)
    terminal = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal.add(i)
    
    # Check if Petya can directly win from start
    # Use BFS to find path to terminal
    
    # If start is terminal, Petya loses immediately (since it's Petya's turn and he can't move)
    if len(graph[start]) == 0:
        print("Lose")
        return
    
    # Try to find a winning path from start to terminal
    # We need to check if there's a path from start to any terminal vertex
    # where Petya makes the final move
    
    # Use BFS to find shortest path to any terminal vertex
    queue = deque([(start, [start])])
    visited = {start}
    
    winning_path = None
    
    while queue:
        curr, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # Try all outgoing edges from current vertex
        for next_vertex in graph[curr]:
            new_path = path + [next_vertex]
            
            # If next_vertex is terminal, Petya just made the last move and Vasya loses
            if next_vertex in terminal:
                winning_path = new_path
                break
                
            # If we haven't visited this vertex before, add it to queue
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, new_path))
        
        if winning_path is not None:
            break
    
    # If we found a winning path
    if winning_path is not None:
        print("Win")
        print(*winning_path)
        return
    
    # Check if there's a cycle reachable from start
    # If Petya can enter a cycle, then the game will go on forever (draw)
    
    # Use DFS to detect if there's a cycle reachable from start
    visited = set()
    rec_stack = set()
    
    def has_cycle(v, parent):
        visited.add(v)
        rec_stack.add(v)
        
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                if has_cycle(neighbor, v):
                    return True
            elif neighbor in rec_stack:
                return True
        
        rec_stack.remove(v)
        return False
    
    # Check if any vertex reachable from start has a cycle
    has_cycle_from_start = False
    
    def dfs_cycle(v):
        nonlocal has_cycle_from_start
        visited.add(v)
        rec_stack.add(v)
        
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                if dfs_cycle(neighbor):
                    has_cycle_from_start = True
                    return True
            elif neighbor in rec_stack:
                has_cycle_from_start = True
                return True
        
        rec_stack.remove(v)
        return False
    
    # First, find all vertices reachable from start
    reachable = set()
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        v = queue.popleft()
        reachable.add(v)
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in reachable:
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Now check if any reachable vertex has a cycle
    visited = set()
    rec_stack = set()
    
    for v in reachable:
        if v not in visited:
            if dfs_cycle(v):
                has_cycle_from_start = True
                break
    
    if has_cycle_from_start:
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    # If we reach here, Petya cannot win and cannot force a draw
    # This means Petya will eventually lose
    print("Lose")

solve()
```

- If from every possible move, Vasya can make a move that eventually leads to Petya losing, then Petya loses
- If Petya can enter a cycle (loop back to a previous vertex), then the game continues indefinitely (draw)

```python

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:]
            for v in vertices:
                graph[i].append(v)
    
    start = int(input())
    
    # Find all terminal vertices (vertices with no outgoing edges)
    terminal = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal.add(i)
    
    # First, check if Petya can win immediately
    # Use BFS to find path from start to any terminal vertex
    queue = deque([(start, [start])])
    visited = {start}
    
    winning_path = None
    
    while queue:
        curr, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # Try all outgoing edges from current vertex
        for next_vertex in graph[curr]:
            new_path = path + [next_vertex]
            
            # If next_vertex is terminal, Petya just made the move and Vasya has no moves
            if next_vertex in terminal:
                winning_path = new_path
                break
                
            # If we haven't visited this vertex before, add it to queue
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, new_path))
        
        if winning_path is not None:
            break
    
    # If we found a winning path
    if winning_path is not None:
        print("Win")
        print(*winning_path)
        return
    
    # Check if there's a cycle reachable from start
    # If Petya can enter a cycle (loop back), then the game goes on forever
    # Use DFS to detect if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    
    # First, find all vertices reachable from start
    reachable = set()
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        v = queue.popleft()
        reachable.add(v)
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in reachable:
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Now check if there's a cycle in the subgraph of reachable vertices
    visited = set()
    rec_stack = set()
    
    def has_cycle(v):
        visited.add(v)
        rec_stack.add(v)
        
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor in rec_stack:
                return True
            if neighbor not in visited:
                if has_cycle(neighbor):
                    return True
        
        rec_stack.remove(v)
        return False
    
    # Check if any reachable vertex has a cycle
    has_cycle_from_start = False
    for v in reachable:
        if v not in visited:
            if has_cycle(v):
                has_cycle_from_start = True
                break
    
    if has_cycle_from_start:
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    # If Petya cannot win and cannot create a cycle, he will eventually lose
    print("Lose")

solve()
```

from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:]
            for v in vertices:
                graph[i].append(v)
    
    start = int(input())
    
    # Find all terminal vertices (vertices with no outgoing edges)
    terminal = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal.add(i)
    
    # First, check if Petya can win immediately
    # Use BFS to find path from start to any terminal vertex
    queue = deque([(start, [start])])
    visited = {start}
    
    winning_path = None
    
    while queue:
        curr, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # Try all outgoing edges from current vertex
        for next_vertex in graph[curr]:
            new_path = path + [next_vertex]
            
            # If next_vertex is terminal, Petya just made the move and Vasya has no moves
            if next_vertex in terminal:
                winning_path = new_path
                break
                
            # If we haven't visited this vertex before, add it to queue
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, new_path))
        
        if winning_path is not None:
            break
    
    # If we found a winning path
    if winning_path is not None:
        print("Win")
        print(*winning_path)
        return
    
    # Check if there's a cycle reachable from start
    # If Petya can enter a cycle (loop back), then the game goes on forever
    # Use DFS to detect if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    
    # First, find all vertices reachable from start
    reachable = set()
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        v = queue.popleft()
        reachable.add(v)
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in reachable:
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Now check if there's a cycle in the subgraph of reachable vertices
    visited = set()
    rec_stack = set()
    
    def has_cycle(v):
        visited.add(v)
        rec_stack.add(v)
        
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor in rec_stack:
                return True
            if neighbor not in visited:
                if has_cycle(neighbor):
                    return True
        
        rec_stack.remove(v)
        return False
    
    # Check if any reachable vertex has a cycle
    has_cycle_from_start = False
    for v in reachable:
        if v not in visited:
            if has_cycle(v):
                has_cycle_from_start = True
                break
    
    if has_cycle_from_start:
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    # If Petya cannot win and cannot create a cycle, he will eventually lose
    print("Lose")

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge to an adjacent vertex
5. The player who cannot move loses
6. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

This is a classic game theory problem on directed graphs. The outcome depends on the structure of the graph and the starting position.

1. **Win condition**: Petya can win if he can force Vasya into a position where Vasya has no moves (terminal state)

2. **Draw condition**: If Petya can create a cycle that will go on indefinitely (exceeding 10^6 moves), then it's a draw

3. **Lose condition**: If no matter what Petya does, Vasya can always respond and eventually win

## Strategy Analysis

1. First, I need to identify all vertices that are terminal (have no outgoing edges)
2. For each vertex, determine if it's a winning position for the player whose turn it is
3. Use dynamic programming or game theory analysis

In game theory for such problems:
- A vertex is a winning position if the current player can make a move to a losing position for the opponent
- A vertex is a losing position if all possible moves lead to winning positions for the opponent

However, since we have a limited number of moves (10^6), we need to be careful about cycles.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Identify all terminal vertices (out-degree = 0)
3. Use DFS/BFS to analyze game states from the starting position
4. For each vertex, determine if it's a winning or losing position
5. If Petya can reach a terminal vertex in an odd number of moves (since he goes first), he wins
6. If Petya can enter a cycle, it results in a draw
7. Otherwise, Petya loses

## Implementation Approach

I'll use the following approach:
1. Build adjacency list representation
2. Use DFS to detect if there's a path to a terminal vertex from start
3. Use cycle detection to see if Petya can create an infinite loop
4. Calculate the game outcome based on the parity of moves

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            graph[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Check if s is terminal (no outgoing edges)
    if len(graph[s]) == 0:
        print("Win")
        print(s)
        return
    
    # Use DFS to find if there's a winning path to a terminal vertex
    # Also detect cycles
    
    # First, find all terminal vertices (outdegree 0)
    terminals = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminals.add(i)
    
    # Check if we can reach a terminal vertex in an odd number of moves (Petya wins)
    # We need to find a path from s to a terminal vertex with odd length
    
    # Use BFS to find shortest path to any terminal
    from collections import deque
    
    # visited array to track state - 0 = unvisited, 1 = visited (in current path), 2 = processed
    # We also need to track path to avoid cycles
    path_found = False
    path = []
    
    # We'll do BFS to find the shortest path to terminal from s
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited = set()
    visited.add(s)
    
    while queue:
        curr, curr_path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we've reached a terminal vertex, Petya wins (odd moves from start)
        if curr in terminals:
            # Length of path is len(curr_path) - 1 (edges)
            # Since Petya starts first, he wins if total moves is odd
            # Number of moves = len(curr_path) - 1
            moves = len(curr_path) - 1
            if moves % 2 == 1:  # Petya makes the last move
                path_found = True
                path = curr_path
                break
        
        # Try all possible next moves
        for next_vertex in graph[curr]:
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, curr_path + [next_vertex]))
    
    # If we found a winning path
    if path_found:
        print("Win")
        print(*path)
        return
    
    # Check if there's a cycle that can be traversed infinitely
    # We need to check if Petya can enter a cycle and make the game last more than 10^6 moves
    
    # Use DFS to detect cycles
    def has_cycle_with_path(start, path, seen):
        # Check if we've visited this node before in current path
        if start in path:
            # Found a cycle - we can create an infinite loop
            return True
        
        if start in seen:
            return False
            
        seen.add(start)
        path.append(start)
        
        for neighbor in graph[start]:
            if has_cycle_with_path(neighbor, path, seen):
                return True
                
        path.pop()
        seen.remove(start)
        return False
    
    # Check if there's a cycle reachable from s
    seen = set()
    path = []
    has_cycle = False
    
    # We need to check if any path from s contains a cycle
    stack = [(s, [s])]
    visited = set()
    
    while stack:
        curr, curr_path = stack.pop()
        
        if curr in curr_path:
            # Found a cycle in this path
            has_cycle = True
            break
            
        if curr not in visited:
            visited.add(curr)
            for neighbor in graph[curr]:
                stack.append((neighbor, curr_path + [neighbor]))
    
    if has_cycle:
        # Petya can create a cycle and the game will last indefinitely
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    # If no winning path and no cycle, Petya loses
    print("Lose")

solve()
```

```python
def solve():

    terminals = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminals.add(i)
    
    # Use BFS to find if there's a path from s to a terminal vertex with odd length
    # (Petya makes the first move, so odd-length path means Petya wins)
    
    queue = deque([(s, [s])])
    visited = {s}
    
    while queue:
        curr, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # If current vertex is terminal, check if path length is odd (Petya wins)
        if curr in terminals:
            moves = len(path) - 1  # Number of edges
            if moves % 2 == 1:
                print("Win")
                print(*path)
                return
        
        # Expand to neighbors
        for next_vertex in graph[curr]:
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, path + [next_vertex]))

    # Check if there's a cycle that can be traversed infinitely
    
    # Use DFS with path tracking to detect cycles from s
    visited_dfs = set()
    path_dfs = []
    
    def dfs_find_cycle(node, path):
        if node in path:
            return True
        
        path.append(node)
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if dfs_find_cycle(neighbor, path):
                return True
        path.pop()
        return False
    
    # Check if there's a cycle reachable from s
    has_cycle = False

            has_cycle = True
            break

            for neighbor in graph[curr]:
                stack.append((neighbor, curr_path + [neighbor]))

        print("Draw")
    else:
        print("Lose")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge from current vertex to another vertex
5. The player who can't make a move loses
6. If the game lasts for 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

1. This is a combinatorial game theory problem - we need to analyze winning/losing positions
2. A position is a "winning" position if the current player can force a win
3. A position is a "losing" position if no matter what the current player does, the opponent can win
4. A position is a "draw" if the game can continue indefinitely (cycle detection)

## Approach

1. **Identify terminal positions**: Vertices with no outgoing edges are losing positions for the player whose turn it is
2. **Reverse analysis**: Work backwards from terminal positions
3. **Classification**: For each vertex, determine if it's a winning, losing, or draw position
4. **Path finding**: If we find a winning path, we need to reconstruct it
5. **Cycle detection**: If we find a cycle, it's a draw

## Algorithm

1. Build the graph from input
2. Identify all vertices with no outgoing edges (lose for current player)
3. Use DFS/BFS with game state tracking to classify positions
4. For each vertex, determine if it's winning, losing, or draw:
   - If all neighbors are losing positions → current position is winning
   - If there exists at least one neighbor that is winning → current position is losing (since opponent wins)
   - If we can reach a cycle → draw

Actually, let me reconsider. This is more complex than a simple impartial game. Let me think about it differently:

- A vertex is a "winning" position for the current player if there exists at least one move to a "losing" position for the opponent
- A vertex is a "losing" position if all moves lead to "winning" positions for the opponent
- A draw occurs when we can enter a cycle that can continue indefinitely

But with the constraint that if game lasts 10^6 turns it's a draw, we need to detect cycles.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Build adjacency list
2. Use DFS to detect cycles and classify positions
3. For each vertex, determine if it's winning, losing, or draw
4. If starting position is winning, find the path
5. If starting position leads to a cycle, return draw
6. Otherwise, return lose

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:]
            graph[i] = vertices
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # If starting vertex has no outgoing edges, Petya loses immediately
    if len(graph[s]) == 0:
        print("Lose")
        return
    
    # We'll use DFS with state to detect cycles and determine game outcome
    # state: 0 - unvisited, 1 - visiting (in current DFS path), 2 - finished (classified)
    state = [0] * (n + 1)
    result = [0] * (n + 1)  # 0: unclassified, 1: winning, 2: losing, 3: draw
    
    # We need to detect cycles and classify positions
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    path = []
    
    def dfs(v):
        if result[v] != 0:
            return result[v]
            
        # If we're already in a cycle or found a draw path
        if state[v] == 1:
            # Found a cycle - this will lead to a draw
            result[v] = 3
            return 3
            
        # Mark current vertex as visiting
        state[v] = 1
        
        # Check all neighbors
        next_states = []
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            next_state = dfs(neighbor)
            next_states.append(next_state)
        
        # If any neighbor leads to a losing position for opponent, then current is winning
        winning = False
        for next_state in next_states:
            if next_state == 2:  # opponent loses
                winning = True
                break
                
        if winning:
            result[v] = 1
        else:
            # All neighbors lead to winning positions for opponent
            # Check if we can reach a draw (cycle)
            # If all next states are 1 or 3, and we can't force a win, it might be losing or draw
            is_draw = False
            for next_state in next_states:
                if next_state == 3:  # draw found
                    is_draw = True
                    break
                    
            if is_draw:
                result[v] = 3
            else:
                result[v] = 2  # losing
                
        state[v] = 2
        return result[v]
    
    # Special case: if the start vertex leads to a cycle, return draw
    # First, detect if there's any cycle that can be reached from s
    # And if we have a winning path
    
    # Let's try a different approach: for each vertex, try to find if it's winning or draw
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    in_stack = [False] * (n + 1)
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    
    def has_cycle(v, parent_v):
        in_stack[v] = True
        visited[v] = True
        
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                parent[neighbor] = v
                if has_cycle(neighbor, v):
                    return True
            elif in_stack[neighbor]:
                # Found cycle
                return True
                
        in_stack[v] = False
        return False
    
    # Check if there's a cycle reachable from s
    # But we need to determine the outcome from s
    
    # Simpler approach: use iterative DFS with position classification
    # First, find all vertices that are losing (they have no moves or only lead to winning positions)
    
    # Actually, let's implement a clean solution:
    
    # Step 1: Detect cycles from starting position
    # Step 2: For each vertex, determine if it's winning, losing, or draw
    
    # We'll use DFS to detect cycles and classify positions
    result = [0] * (n + 1)
    state = [0] * (n + 1)  # 0: unvisited, 1: visiting, 2: finished
    
    def classify(v):
        if result[v] != 0:
            return result[v]
            
        if len(graph[v]) == 0:
            result[v] = 2  # losing position (no moves)
            return 2
            
        state[v] = 1
        next_states = []
        
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            next_state = classify(neighbor)
            next_states.append(next_state)
        
        # If any neighbor is losing for opponent, then v is winning
        winning = any(state_val == 2 for state_val in next_states)
        
        if winning:
            result[v] = 1
        else:
            # All neighbors lead to winning positions for opponent
            # Check if any neighbor leads to a draw (cycle)
            draw_possible = any(state_val == 3 for state_val in next_states)
            if draw_possible:
                result[v] = 3
            else:
                result[v] = 2
                
        state[v] = 2
        return result[v]
    
    # Classification
    classify(s)
    
    if result[s] == 1:
        # Find the winning path
        path = []
        current = s
        path.append(current)
        
        # Find actual path from s to terminal vertex
        # We need to find a path where each step leads to a losing position for opponent
        # Use DFS to reconstruct path
        def find_path(v, path_so_far):
            if len(graph[v]) == 0:
                return path_so_far + [v] if path_so_far else [v]
                
            for neighbor in graph[v]:
                if result[neighbor] == 2:  # opponent loses in this state
                    next_path = find_path(neighbor, path_so_far + [v])
                    if next_path:
                        return next_path
            return None
        
        path = find_path(s, [])
        if path:
            print("Win")
            print(*path)
        else:
            print("Lose")
            
    elif result[s] == 3:
        print("Draw")
    else:
        print("Lose")

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's a bug in my reasoning. Let me reconsider the game theory aspect.

In standard impartial games:
- A position is losing if all moves lead to winning positions for opponent
- A position is winning if there exists a move to a losing position for opponent
- A position leads to a draw if there's a cycle (infinite game)

```python
from collections import deque
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = {}
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            vertices = line[1:]
            graph[i] = vertices
        else:
            graph[i] = []
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # If starting vertex has no outgoing edges, Petya loses immediately
    if not graph[s]:
        print("Lose")
        return
    
    # BFS to find path to a terminal vertex (vertex with no outgoing edges)
    # Also detect cycles
    parents = {}
    visited = set()
    queue = deque([s])
    visited.add(s)
    parents[s] = None
    
    # First, see if we can reach any vertex with no outgoing edges
    found_terminal_path = False
    terminal_path = []
    
    while queue:
        v = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if this vertex is terminal
        if not graph[v]:
            # Found a terminal vertex
            # Reconstruct path from s to v
            path_to_terminal = []
            current = v
            while current is not None:
                path_to_terminal.append(current)
                current = parents[current]
            path_to_terminal.reverse()
            
            print("Win")
            print(*path_to_terminal)
            return
        
        # Explore neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                visited.add(neighbor)
                parents[neighbor] = v
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # If we reach here, no terminal vertex is reachable from s
    # Now check if there's a cycle from s
    
    # DFS to detect cycle
    path = []
    found_cycle = False
    
    def dfs_cycle(v):
        nonlocal found_cycle
        if found_cycle:
            return
            
        path.append(v)
        
        for neighbor in graph[v]:
            if neighbor in path:
                found_cycle = True
                return
            if neighbor not in path:
                dfs_cycle(neighbor)
                
        path.pop()
        
    dfs_cycle(s)
    
    if found_cycle:
        print("Draw")
    else:
        print("Lose")
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. If the game lasts for 106 turns the draw is announced.

Vasya was performing big laboratory work in "Spelling and parts of speech" at night before the game, so he fell asleep at the very beginning of the game. Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves.

Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Players take turns moving the chip along edges (Petya first)
4. A player who can't make a move loses
5. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
6. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

1. This is a game theory problem on a directed graph
2. We need to analyze the game states based on whether they lead to winning, losing, or drawing positions
3. A position is winning if there exists at least one move to a losing position for the opponent
4. A position is losing if all possible moves lead to winning positions for the opponent
5. A position can lead to a draw if there's a cycle that can be traversed indefinitely

## Strategy

1. First, I'll identify if there's a cycle reachable from the starting position
2. If there's a cycle, Petya can potentially draw by traversing it forever
3. If there's no cycle, we can use DFS with memoization to determine winning/losing states
4. A position is winning if there's a move to a losing position
5. A position is losing if all moves lead to winning positions
6. If the chip reaches a dead-end (no outgoing edges), the current player loses

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the adjacency list from input
2. Check if there are cycles reachable from the starting position
3. If cycles exist, check if Petya can use them to force a draw
4. If no cycles, use DFS to determine winning/losing states
5. If Petya can reach a dead-end on his turn, he wins
6. If Petya can only reach positions that result in his loss, he loses

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            adj[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Check if there's a cycle reachable from s
    # Use DFS with coloring to detect cycles
    color = {}  # 0: unvisited, 1: visiting, 2: finished
    path = []
    
    def has_cycle(node):
        if node in color:
            return color[node] == 1
        
        color[node] = 1
        path.append(node)
        
        for neighbor in adj[node]:
            if has_cycle(neighbor):
                return True
        
        path.pop()
        color[node] = 2
        return False
    
    # Check for cycles from start
    cycle_exists = has_cycle(s)
    
    # If there's a cycle, Petya can potentially draw by looping
    # But we need to check if he can actually force a draw or win
    # If he can win, he will (by going to a dead-end on his turn)
    
    # First, let's see if we can find a path to a dead-end on Petya's turn
    # We'll do BFS to find all reachable vertices and their depth
    
    # Check if Petya can win - i.e., if there's a path from s to a dead-end
    # where the number of moves (edges) is odd (since Petya goes first)
    
    # BFS to find distances from s to all reachable nodes
    dist = {}
    queue = deque([s])
    dist[s] = 0
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor in adj[node]:
            if neighbor not in dist:
                dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Now check if there's any dead-end (node with no outgoing edges)
    # that is reachable and at an odd distance from s
    # Because Petya goes first, odd distance means Petya made the last move
    
    dead_ends = []
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(adj[i]) == 0:  # no outgoing edges
            if i in dist:
                if dist[i] % 2 == 1:  # odd distance means Petya made the last move
                    dead_ends.append(i)

    if dead_ends:
        # Reconstruct the path to one of these dead-ends
        # We need to find a path from s to one of the dead-ends
        path_found = []
        
        # BFS to reconstruct path

        visited = set()
        queue = deque([s])
        visited.add(s)
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.popleft()
            if node in dead_ends:
                # Found a path to a dead-end
                current = node
                path_found = []
                while current in parent:
                    path_found.append(current)
                    current = parent[current]
                path_found.append(s)
                path_found.reverse()
                break

                if neighbor not in visited:
                    visited.add(neighbor)
                    parent[neighbor] = node
                    queue.append(neighbor)

            # We need to verify the path works
            path_reconstructed = []
            current = s
            path_reconstructed.append(s)

            temp_parent = {}
            queue = deque([s])
            visited = set([s])

                    # Found a path to dead-end
                    path_reconstructed = []
                    current = node
                    while current in temp_parent:
                        path_reconstructed.append(current)
                        current = temp_parent[current]
                    path_reconstructed.append(s)

            # We'll use the parent to reconstruct the path
            if path_reconstructed:
                # Verify the path exists
                temp_path = []
                current = s
                temp_path.append(s)
                
                # Find the correct path from s to a dead-end
                parent = {}
                queue = deque([s])
                visited = {s}
                
                while queue:
                    node = queue.popleft()
                    if len(adj[node]) == 0 and node not in visited:  # dead-end
                        # But we need reachable dead-ends
                        continue
                        
                    for neighbor in adj[node]:
                        if neighbor not in visited:
                            visited.add(neighbor)
                            parent[neighbor] = node
                            queue.append(neighbor)

                for dead_end in dead_ends:

                        # Reconstruct path from s to dead_end
                        path = []
                        current = dead_end
                        while current != s:
                            path.append(current)
                            current = parent.get(current, -1)
                            if current == -1:
                                break
                        path.append(s)
                        path.reverse()
                        
                        # Verify the path is valid (each step has an edge)
                        valid_path = True
                        for i in range(len(path) - 1):
                            if path[i+1] not in adj[path[i]]:
                                valid_path = False
                                break
                        
                        if valid_path:
                            print("Win")
                            print(*path)
                            return

    # If there's a cycle, and Petya can enter it, then he can draw
    # But we need to see if he can force a draw
    
    # Check if there's a reachable cycle
    # We already checked for cycles in has_cycle() above
    
    # If there's a cycle reachable from s, Petya can draw by looping
    # However, we need to ensure the cycle is reachable from s
    
    # Actually, let's reconsider: if there's a cycle that can be reached from s,
    # and Petya can enter it, he can keep playing forever (draw)
    
    # Check if any vertex in a cycle is reachable from s
    # Use DFS to find all vertices reachable from s and check for cycles
    
    # Since we already have a cycle detection function, if cycle_exists is True
    # and there's a cycle reachable from s, then Petya can draw

    if cycle_exists:
        # Petya can draw by entering the cycle
        print("Draw")
        return
    
    # If no cycle and no winning path, Petya loses
    print("Lose")
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
```

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())

            return color[node] == 1
        
        color[node] = 1

    if cycle_exists:

        return

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. If the game lasts for 106 turns the draw is announced.

Vasya was performing big laboratory work in "Spelling and parts of speech" at night before the game, so he fell asleep at the very beginning of the game. Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves.

Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Players move the chip along an edge to an adjacent vertex
5. The player who can't move loses
6. If the game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
7. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

1. This is a game theory problem that can be solved using impartial games (like Nim)
2. The outcome depends on the structure of the game tree from the starting position
3. We can use the concept of "winning" and "losing" positions:
   - A position is "winning" if the current player can force a win
   - A position is "losing" if no matter what move the current player makes, the opponent can force a win
4. For a position with no outgoing edges, the current player loses (since they can't move)
5. A position is winning if there exists at least one move to a losing position for the opponent
6. A position is losing if all moves lead to winning positions for the opponent

## Algorithm Steps

1. First, identify if there's a cycle in the graph from the starting position
2. If there's a cycle, then the game can continue indefinitely, resulting in a draw
3. If there's no cycle, then the game will eventually end, so we need to determine if Petya has a winning strategy
4. We can use DFS with memoization to determine winning/losing positions

However, there's a simpler approach:
- If from the starting position we can reach a dead end (no outgoing edges) in an odd number of moves (Petya's turn), then Petya wins
- If we can reach a dead end in an even number of moves (Vasya's turn), then Vasya wins
- If there's a cycle, we get a draw

But we need to be more careful. Actually:
- If there's a path from start to a dead end with odd length (Petya makes the last move), then Petya wins
- If there's a path with even length, then Vasya wins
- If there's a cycle (any cycle reachable from start), then we can loop forever → draw

## Implementation Strategy

1. Build the graph from input
2. Check if there's any cycle reachable from start position
3. If cycle exists → return "Draw"
4. If no cycle, check if there's a path to a dead end in odd number of steps → "Win"
5. If all paths to dead ends have even length → "Lose"

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually:

- If we can reach a dead end in an odd number of moves (Petya makes the last move), Petya wins
- If we can only reach dead ends in even number of moves (Vasya makes the last move), Petya loses
- If there's a cycle, we can loop forever → draw

But we need to find the shortest path to any dead end with odd length.

Actually, let me do it differently:

1. First, do a BFS/DFS from the start position to find all reachable vertices
2. Mark vertices that have no outgoing edges (dead ends)
3. Check if any dead end is reachable in an odd number of steps from start
4. If yes, Petya wins by going through that path
5. If there's a cycle (any vertex that can reach itself or another vertex that can reach it), then draw
6. Otherwise, if all dead ends are at even distance, then lose

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            graph[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Mark vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends)
    dead_ends = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            dead_ends.add(i)
    
    # Check if there's a cycle reachable from s
    # We'll use BFS to detect cycles
    visited = set()
    in_stack = set()
    cycle_found = False
    
    def has_cycle_from(start):
        # BFS to detect cycle
        queue = deque([(start, [start])])
        visited.add(start)
        in_stack.add(start)
        
        while queue:
            node, path = queue.popleft()
            
            # Check outgoing edges
            for neighbor in graph[node]:
                if neighbor in in_stack:
                    # Found a cycle
                    return True
                if neighbor not in visited:
                    visited.add(neighbor)
                    in_stack.add(neighbor)
                    queue.append((neighbor, path + [neighbor]))
            
            # Remove from in_stack after processing (just for safety)
            in_stack.discard(node)
        
        return False
    
    # Check for cycles
    cycle_path = []
    
    # Actually, we can use a simpler approach - if we can reach a dead end in odd steps, Petya wins
    # First, find all paths to dead ends with minimal steps
    # Use BFS to find shortest distances to dead ends
    
    # BFS from start to find shortest distance to any dead end
    distance_to_dead_end = {}
    queue = deque([(s, 0)])  # (node, steps)
    distance_to_dead_end[s] = 0
    
    while queue:
        node, steps = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if current node is a dead end
        if node in dead_ends:
            if steps % 2 == 1:  # Odd number of steps (Petya's turn to make the last move)
                # Petya wins
                # Now we need to reconstruct the path
                path = []
                current = node
                temp_steps = steps
                # We need to reconstruct the path from s to current
                # We'll do this in reverse
                temp_distance = {}
                queue2 = deque([(s, 0)])
                temp_distance[s] = 0
                parent = {s: None}
                
                while queue2:
                    node2, steps2 = queue2.popleft()
                    if node2 in graph:
                        for neighbor in graph[node2]:
                            if neighbor not in temp_distance:
                                temp_distance[neighbor] = steps2 + 1
                                parent[neighbor] = node2
                                queue2.append((neighbor, steps2 + 1))
                
                # Reconstruct path
                path = []
                current = node
                while current is not None:
                    path.append(current)
                    current = parent[current]
                path.reverse()
                
                print("Win")
                print(*path)
                return
        
        # Explore neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in distance_to_dead_end:
                distance_to_dead_end[neighbor] = steps + 1
                queue.append((neighbor, steps + 1))
    
    # Check if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    # We can check if any vertex can reach itself or loop
    reachable = set()
    queue = deque([s])
    reachable.add(s)
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in reachable:
                reachable.add(neighbor)
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Check if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    # For each vertex in reachable, check if it can reach itself
    visited = {}
    path_to = {}
    
    def dfs_cycle(start):
        visited[start] = True
        stack = [start]
        path = []
        
        while stack:
            node = stack.pop()
            if node in path:
                # Found cycle
                cycle_idx = path.index(node)
                return True, path[cycle_idx:]
            
            if node not in visited and node in graph:
                path.append(node)
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if neighbor not in visited:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        stack.append(neighbor)
        
        return False, []
    
    # Simpler: any vertex that has a path to a vertex that can reach itself has a cycle
    # We can check if any vertex in reachable has a path to another vertex in reachable that forms a cycle
    
    # Actually, if we can reach a vertex that has an edge to a vertex that is already in our reachable set, it might form a cycle
    # But easier: if any vertex has a self-loop or can reach itself
    
    # Check for self-loops
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if i in reachable and i in graph[i]:
            # Self-loop - cycle exists
            print("Draw")
            return
    
    # Check for cycles between different vertices
    # More systematically: for any vertex in reachable, check if it can reach another vertex in reachable
    # If it can reach a vertex that has a path back to it, it's a cycle
    
    # Use the visited approach with path tracking
    cycle_found = False
    
    # Let's do a BFS from each reachable vertex to detect cycles
    for start in reachable:
        visited = set()

        visited.add(start)
        
        while queue:

            for neighbor in graph[node]:

                    # But we don't have parent tracking
                    # Instead, check if neighbor is in reachable and we can form a cycle
                    if neighbor in reachable:
                        # This could be part of a cycle, but we need to check
                        # Actually, if any vertex can reach another vertex that can reach it, it's a cycle

                        # and that vertex has a path back to this one, it's a cycle
                        # But with simple BFS, we can't easily detect that
                        
                        # Alternative: if we can reach any vertex that has a path back to start, it's a cycle
                        # Actually, we can just check if there's any cycle in the reachable subgraph
                        
                        # Instead, let's assume that if there's any self-loop or edge in a cycle, we have draw
                        # We already checked self-loops
                        pass
                else:
                    visited.add(neighbor)
                    queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # If no dead end is reachable in odd steps, and no cycle exists, then Petya loses
    # But we need to check for cycles more reliably
    
    # Correct approach: use DFS with recursion stack to detect cycles
    def detect_cycle_with_path(node, path, visited, in_stack):
        if node in in_stack:
            return True
        if node in visited:
            return False
            
        visited.add(node)
        in_stack.add(node)
        path.append(node)
        
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if detect_cycle_with_path(neighbor, path, visited, in_stack):
                return True
                
        in_stack.remove(node)
        path.pop()
        return False
    
    # We only care about cycles reachable from s
    visited = set()
    in_stack = set()
    path = []
    
    # Check if there's a cycle in the component reachable from s
    def has_reachable_cycle():
        stack = [s]
        visited = set()
        parent = {}
        
        while stack:
            node = stack.pop()
            if node in visited:
                continue
            visited.add(node)
            
            for neighbor in graph[node]:
                if neighbor in visited:
                    # Check if neighbor is on path to node or if it's a cycle
                    # We need to check if there's a cycle in the entire reachable component
                    # But simpler: if any vertex has a self-loop or can reach itself
                    # Actually, we already check self-loops
                    pass
                else:
                    stack.append(neighbor)
        
        # Actually, after all this, if we haven't found a dead end in odd steps and no cycle, then lose
        # But we need to detect cycles properly
        
        # Final check: if there's any vertex in reachable that can reach itself (through any path)
        # We can check for cycles using the reachability graph
        
        # For each vertex in reachable, check if it can reach any other vertex in reachable
        # Then check if that vertex can reach back to the original one
        
        # Actually, if we can reach any other vertex from start and that vertex can reach start, then cycle exists
        # But that would be a cycle
        
        # Simpler: if any vertex in reachable has an edge to another vertex in reachable, it might be part of a cycle
        # But not necessarily
        
        # I'm overcomplicating. The key insight is:
        # - If there's a path from s to a dead end with odd length -> Win
        # - If there's a path from s to a dead end with even length -> Vasya wins (Petya loses)
        # - If there's a cycle in the reachable component (any cycle that can be reached from s) -> Draw
        
        # So we need to check for cycles in the reachable component
        
        # Use BFS to check if any vertex can reach itself
        visited = set()
        for start_node in reachable:
            if start_node in visited:
                continue
            visited.add(start_node)
            # Check if starting from start_node, we can reach any vertex that can reach start_node
            # Do BFS from start_node to check if any vertex reaches back to start_node
            queue = deque([start_node])
            discovered = set()
            discovered.add(start_node)
            
            while queue:
                node = queue.popleft()
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if neighbor in discovered:
                        # If we find a cycle in the component
                        # But we need to check if this forms a cycle with start_node
                        # Actually, if we can reach a vertex that can reach start_node, it's a cycle
                        # But the cycle might not involve start_node
                        
                        # Actually, if we can reach any vertex that has a path to start_node, it's a cycle
                        # So we need to check reachability from neighbor to start_node
                        
                        # Do BFS from neighbor to see if it can reach start_node
                        queue2 = deque([neighbor])
                        visited2 = set()
                        visited2.add(neighbor)
                        
                        found = False
                        while queue2:
                            n2 = queue2.popleft()
                            if n2 == start_node:
                                found = True
                                break
                            for n3 in graph[n2]:
                                if n3 not in visited2:
                                    visited2.add(n3)
                                    queue2.append(n3)
                        
                        if found:
                            return True
                    else:
                        discovered.add(neighbor)
                        queue.append(neighbor)
        
        return False
    
    # Actually, the simplest and most reliable way:
    # 1. BFS to find shortest paths to dead ends
    # 2. If any dead end is reachable in odd distance -> win
    # 3. Else, check if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    
    # We already have the BFS code above. Let me finish it
    
    # If we found a path to dead end with odd steps, we already printed "Win"
    
    # If we didn't find any, now check for cycles
    # Use DFS with recursion stack
    
    visited = set()
    stack = []
    path = []
    
    def detect_cycle_in_component():
        visited = set()
        for node in reachable:
            if node in visited:
                continue
            # DFS from this node to find cycles
            stack = [node]
            visited.add(node)
            path = []
            
            while stack:
                current = stack.pop()
                path.append(current)
                
                for neighbor in graph[current]:
                    if neighbor in path and path.index(neighbor) < path.index(current):
                        # Found a cycle
                        return True
                    if neighbor not in visited:
                        visited.add(neighbor)
                        stack.append(neighbor)
        return False
    
    # But this might not be efficient and might miss cycles
    
    # Final decision:
    # 1. Do BFS from start to find if any dead end is reachable in odd steps
    # 2. If yes, output the path to that dead end
    
    # 3. If no, then check if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    
    # Let's restart with clean BFS for distance to dead ends
    
    # Reset and do clean BFS
    distance_to_dead_end = {}
    queue = deque([(s, 0)])
    distance_to_dead_end[s] = 0
    
    # We'll also keep parent information to reconstruct path
    parent = {}
    
    found_odd_dead_end = False
    path_to_odd_dead_end = None
    
    while queue:
        node, steps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If this is a dead end, check if steps is odd
        if node in dead_ends:
            if steps % 2 == 1:
                found_odd_dead_end = True
                path_to_odd_dead_end = []
                # Reconstruct path using parent
                current = node
                while current is not None:
                    path_to_odd_dead_end.append(current)
                    current = parent.get(current)
                path_to_odd_dead_end.reverse()
                print("Win")
                print(*path_to_odd_dead_end)
                return
        
        # Explore neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in distance_to_dead_end:
                distance_to_dead_end[neighbor] = steps + 1
                parent[neighbor] = node
                queue.append((neighbor, steps + 1))
    
    # If no odd-length path to dead end found
    # Now check for cycles in the reachable component
    
    # Use a simple cycle detection: if any vertex has a self-loop, or if there's a path from a vertex back to itself
    
    # First, find all vertices reachable from s
    reachable = set()
    queue_reachable = deque([s])
    reachable.add(s)
    
    while queue_reachable:
        node = queue_reachable.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in reachable:
                reachable.add(neighbor)
                queue_reachable.append(neighbor)
    
    # Check if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    # We can do this by checking if we can reach a vertex from multiple paths
    # Or check for back edges in DFS
    
    # Simple: if any vertex has a self-loop
    for node in reachable:
        if node in graph[node]:
            print("Draw")
            return
    
    # Check for cycles between different vertices
    # For each pair of vertices, check if a->b and b->a
    # But this is expensive
    
    # We can use DFS to detect cycles
    visited = set()
    stack = set()
    
    def has_cycle_dfs(node):
        if node in stack:
            return True
        if node in visited:
            return False
            
        visited.add(node)
        stack.add(node)
        
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor in stack or has_cycle_dfs(neighbor):
                return True
                
        stack.remove(node)
        return False
    
    # Check if there's a cycle in reachable component
    for node in reachable:
        if has_cycle_dfs(node):
            print("Draw")
            return
    
    # If we reach here, no odd path to dead end and no cycle
    print("Lose")
```

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            graph[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Mark vertices with no outgoing edges (dead ends)
    dead_ends = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            dead_ends.add(i)
    
    # BFS to find the shortest path to a dead end with odd length
    parent = {}
    distance = {}
    queue = deque([s])
    distance[s] = 0
    
    found_odd_dead_end = False
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        
        # If current node is a dead end and we reached it in odd steps, Petya wins
        if node in dead_ends and distance[node] % 2 == 1:
            found_odd_dead_end = True
            # Reconstruct the path
            path = []
            current = node
            while current is not None:
                path.append(current)
                current = parent.get(current)
            path.reverse()
            print("Win")
            print(*path)
            return
        
        # Explore all outgoing edges
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in distance:
                distance[neighbor] = distance[node] + 1
                parent[neighbor] = node
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # If we couldn't reach a dead end in odd steps, check for a cycle
    # Find all vertices reachable from s
    reachable = set()
    queue_reachable = deque([s])
    reachable.add(s)
    
    while queue_reachable:
        node = queue_reachable.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if neighbor not in reachable:
                reachable.add(neighbor)
                queue_reachable.append(neighbor)
    
    # Check if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    # If any vertex has a self-loop, or can reach itself through a path
    for start in reachable:
        # BFS to check if start can reach itself through a path
        visited = set()
        queue_bfs = deque([start])
        visited.add(start)
        
        found_cycle = False
        while queue_bfs:
            node = queue_bfs.popleft()
            for neighbor in graph[node]:
                if neighbor == start:
                    found_cycle = True
                    break
                if neighbor not in visited:
                    visited.add(neighbor)
                    queue_bfs.append(neighbor)
            if found_cycle:
                break
                
        if found_cycle:
            print("Draw")
            return
    
    # If no path to dead end with odd length and no cycle, Petya loses
    print("Lose")
```

            dead_ends.add(i)

    parent = {}
    distance = {}
    queue = deque([s])
    distance[s] = 0
