Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. If the game lasts for 106 turns the draw is announced.

Vasya was performing big laboratory work in "Spelling and parts of speech" at night before the game, so he fell asleep at the very beginning of the game. Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves.

Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. Chip starts at vertex s
3. Petya goes first, then Vasya, alternating turns
4. Player who can't move loses (if game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw)
5. We need to determine if Petya can win, draw, or lose

## Key Insights

1. This is a classic game theory problem on directed graphs
2. We can use the concept of "winning" and "losing" positions
3. A position is "losing" if all moves from it lead to "winning" positions for the opponent
4. A position is "winning" if there exists at least one move to a "losing" position for the opponent
5. A position that leads to a cycle results in a draw (game lasts 10^6 turns)

## Algorithm Steps

1. **Identify terminal positions**: Vertices with no outgoing edges
2. **Use dynamic programming with memoization**: 
   - Position is losing if all outgoing edges lead to winning positions
   - Position is winning if at least one outgoing edge leads to a losing position
3. **Detect cycles**: If we can reach a cycle, we get a draw
4. **Trace back the winning path**: If Petya can win, find the sequence of moves

## Detailed Approach

1. Build adjacency list from input
2. Use DFS with state tracking to detect:
   - Winning positions (can force opponent to lose)
   - Losing positions (any move leads to winning for opponent)
   - Cyclic positions (leads to infinite game)
3. For each vertex, determine its state and record the path to reach a terminal or losing position

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            graph[i] = edges
    
    s = int(input())
    
    # Check if s is terminal (no outgoing edges)
    if len(graph[s]) == 0:
        print("Win")
        print(s)
        return
    
    # State: 0 = unknown, 1 = winning, 2 = losing, 3 = in cycle (draw)
    state = [0] * (n + 1)
    path = [0] * (n + 1)  # path[i] stores the path to terminal/losing position for vertex i
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs(v, curr_path):
        if state[v] != 0:
            return state[v]
        
        # If already in a cycle, it's a draw
        if visited[v]:
            state[v] = 3
            return 3
        
        visited[v] = True
        
        # Check all outgoing edges
        next_states = []
        for u in graph[v]:
            next_state = dfs(u, curr_path + [v])
            next_states.append(next_state)
        
        # If no outgoing edges, it's a terminal position
        if len(next_states) == 0:
            state[v] = 2  # losing position (opponent loses)
            return 2
        
        # Find if there's any move to a losing position
        has_losing_move = any(next_state == 2 for next_state in next_states)
        
        if has_losing_move:
            state[v] = 1  # winning position
            # Store path to a losing position
            for u in graph[v]:
                next_state = dfs(u, curr_path + [v])
                if next_state == 2:
                    path[v] = curr_path + [v]
                    break
        else:
            # All moves lead to winning positions for opponent
            state[v] = 2  # losing position (current player loses)
        
        return state[v]
    
    # We need to handle cycles properly
    # Actually, let's use a different approach: detect cycles and winning positions
    
    # Reset state and use a clearer approach
    state = [0] * (n + 1)  # 0: unvisited, 1: winning, 2: losing, 3: cycle
    path = [None] * (n + 1)
    
    def find_state(v):
        if state[v] != 0:
            return state[v]
        
        # If already in cycle, return 3
        if state[v] == 3:
            return 3
            
        # If no outgoing edges, it's a terminal position (opponent loses)
        if len(graph[v]) == 0:
            return 2
        
        # Recursively check all neighbors
        next_states = []
        for u in graph[v]:
            next_state = find_state(u)
            next_states.append(next_state)
        
        # If all next states are winning (1), then current state is losing (2)
        # If at least one next state is losing (2), then current state is winning (1)
        has_losing_move = any(next_state == 2 for next_state in next_states)
        
        if has_losing_move:
            state[v] = 1
            # Store the path: for each neighbor that leads to losing state
            for u in graph[v]:
                next_state = find_state(u)
                if next_state == 2:
                    path[v] = path[u] if path[u] else [v]
                    # Need to reconstruct full path, so let's do it differently
                    pass
        else:
            state[v] = 2
        
        return state[v]
    
    # Actually, let's think differently - we need to find if there's a path to terminal
    # First, find all vertices that lead to terminal positions
    
    # Use topological sort to process vertices with no out-degrees first
    in_degree = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        in_degree[i] = len(graph[i])
    
    # Find all terminal vertices
    terminals = []
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminals.append(i)
    
    # For terminal vertices, the player who just moved wins
    # So a vertex with edge to terminal is winning
    
    # We'll do a reverse BFS from terminals
    # A vertex is winning if it has an edge to a losing vertex (where losing means all moves lead to winning)
    
    # Let's use DP with memoization and detect cycles
    memo = {}
    max_depth = 10**6
    
    def can_win(v, depth):
        if depth >= max_depth:
            return True  # draw
        if v in memo:
            return memo[v]
        
        # If this vertex has no outgoing edges, current player loses
        if len(graph[v]) == 0:
            memo[v] = False
            return False
        
        # Check if any neighbor leads to a losing position for opponent
        for u in graph[v]:
            if can_win(u, depth + 1):
                memo[v] = True
                return True
        
        memo[v] = False
        return False
    
    # First, check if Petya can win
    # Petya wins if he can force Vasya into a position with no moves
    winning_path = []
    
    # Try to find a path that leads to terminal
    def find_path(v, path_so_far):
        if len(graph[v]) == 0:
            # Reached terminal, Petya wins
            if len(path_so_far) == 0:
                # This should not happen
                return None
            return path_so_far + [v]
        
        # Check if any neighbor leads to terminal
        for u in graph[v]:
            result = find_path(u, path_so_far + [v])
            if result is not None:
                return result
        
        return None
    
    # Find if there's a path to terminal
    # But it's complex with the turn alternation
    
    # Let me restart with a clearer approach:
    # 1. Find all vertices that are "winning" (current player can force win)
    # 2. Find all vertices that are "losing" (current player will lose with best play)
    # 3. Find if there's a cycle
    
    # State: 0 - unvisited, 1 - winning, 2 - losing, 3 - cycle
    state = [0] * (n + 1)
    path_to_win = [None] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs_with_path(v):
        if state[v] != 0:
            return state[v]
        
        if len(graph[v]) == 0:
            # Terminal: the player who just moved wins - so current player loses
            state[v] = 2  # current player loses
            return 2
        
        # Check all neighbors
        next_states = []
        for u in graph[v]:
            next_state = dfs_with_path(u)
            next_states.append(next_state)
        
        # If any neighbor is losing (2), current player wins
        if any(next_state == 2 for next_state in next_states):
            state[v] = 1
            # Find which neighbor leads to losing state
            for u in graph[v]:
                if dfs_with_path(u) == 2:
                    path_to_win[v] = path_to_win[u] if path_to_win[u] else [v]
                    # We need to properly reconstruct path
                    pass
            return 1
        
        # If all neighbors are winning, current player loses
        state[v] = 2
        return 2
    
    # But we need to track the actual path
    # For each vertex, store the path to a losing position or terminal
    
    # Reset and use iterative approach
    # Find all vertices that can reach terminal positions
    
    # First, find all terminal vertices
    terminal = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminal.add(i)
    
    # Use BFS backwards from terminals
    # A vertex is "winning" if it has at least one edge to a terminal or a "losing" position
    
    # Actually, let's use the standard game theory approach:
    # A position is losing if all moves lead to winning positions
    # A position is winning if there exists a move to a losing position
    
    # For each vertex, we want to know if it's winning or losing
    state = [0] * (n + 1)
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)

    # Process vertices in reverse order of dependency (topological sort)
    # But there might be cycles, so we need to detect them
    
    # Do DFS to detect cycles and compute states
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    rec_stack = [False] * (n + 1)
    state = [0] * (n + 1)  # 0: unvisited, 1: winning, 2: losing, 3: in cycle
    path = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs(v):
        if rec_stack[v]:
            return 3  # cycle detected
        if state[v] != 0:
            return state[v]
        
        rec_stack[v] = True
        visited[v] = True
        
        # Check all neighbors
        next_states = []
        for u in graph[v]:
            next_state = dfs(u)
            next_states.append(next_state)
        
        rec_stack[v] = False
        
        # If any neighbor leads to losing state, current state is winning
        if any(next_state == 2 for next_state in next_states):
            state[v] = 1
        else:
            state[v] = 2
            
        return state[v]

    # 2. If s is in a cycle, draw
    # 3. If s can reach a terminal vertex in an odd number of moves, Petya wins
    # 4. If s can reach a terminal vertex in an even number of moves, Vasya wins
    
    # But it's not just about distance - it's about the game theory states
    
    # Standard approach: 
    # - A position is losing if all moves lead to winning positions
    # - A position is winning if there exists a move to a losing position
    
    # We'll compute the state of each vertex and also track the path to winning
    
    # First, let's do a BFS from all terminal vertices in reverse
    # Use memoization with states
    
    state = [-1] * (n + 1)  # -1: unvisited, 0: losing, 1: winning
    path = [0] * (n + 1)

    queue = deque()

    # state[v] = 0 if current player loses from v

    # So state[v] = 0

    # state[v] = 0 otherwise
    
    # This is correct - because if you can move to a losing position for opponent, you win
    
    # Initialize all vertices
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            state[i] = 0  # current player loses
    
    # Process vertices in topological order
    # Create in-degree list
    in_degree = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for neighbor in graph[i]:
            in_degree[neighbor] += 1
    
    # Use Kahn's algorithm for topological sort
    queue = deque()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if in_degree[i] == 0:  # actually, no: in_degree is the in-degree of i
            # We want to process vertices that have no incoming edges? 
            # Actually, we want to process vertices that have no outgoing edges first
            pass
    
    # Actually, we want to process vertices in reverse: from terminal to others
    # Start with vertices that have no outgoing edges (they are losing positions)
    
    # All vertices with no outgoing edges have state 0 (losing for current player)
    # Then process vertices that can reach them
    
    # Create a list of vertices with no outgoing edges
    terminals = []
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminals.append(i)
    
    # Use queue to process vertices
    from collections import defaultdict
    dependencies = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for u in graph[i]:
            dependencies[u].append(i)
    
    # Queue for BFS: vertices that have state determined
    q = deque()
    for v in terminals:
        q.append(v)
    
    # state[v] = 0 for terminal vertices (current player loses)
    # We will compute state for other vertices
    state = [0] * (n + 1)
    for v in terminals:
        state[v] = 0
    
    # For a vertex to have state 1, it needs to have at least one neighbor with state 0
    # We'll use BFS
    while q:
        v = q.popleft()
        
        # For each predecessor u of v
        for u in dependencies[v]:
            # If u has all neighbors with state 0, then state[u] = 1
            # But we need to check if any neighbor has state 0
            has_losing_neighbor = False
            for neighbor in graph[u]:
                if state[neighbor] == 0:
                    has_losing_neighbor = True
                    break
            
            if has_losing_neighbor:
                state[u] = 1
            else:
                state[u] = 0
            
            # If state[u] is now determined, add to queue
            if state[u] != 0:
                q.append(u)
    
    # Now check if s is winning or losing
    if state[s] == 1:
        # Petya can win - find the path
        # We need to find a sequence from s to a terminal vertex that has state 0 for the opponent
        # Actually, we need to find a path where we move to a vertex that has state 0
        # But after state 0 vertex, the opponent cannot move, so opponent loses
        
        # Find the path from s to a vertex with state 0
        path = []
        current = s
        while current != 0:
            path.append(current)
            # Find a neighbor that has state 0
            found = False
            for neighbor in graph[current]:
                if state[neighbor] == 0:
                    current = neighbor
                    found = True
                    break

                # Actually, we should have state[u] = 1 if there exists neighbor with state 0
                # So we should be able to find such a path
                break

        # Let's do it differently:
        
        # We'll build the path by going from s to a vertex with no outgoing edges
        # That vertex will be the terminal

        # But state 0 means that when the current player is at v, they lose

        # So we need to find a path from s to a vertex v where v is in a state 0 (which means v has no outgoing edges or all moves lead to winning for opponent)

        # So if Petya moves to v, then Vasya is at v and loses, so Petya wins
        
        # So we need to find any path from s to a vertex v with state 0
        
        # Let's build the path
        path = []
        current = s
        while current != 0:
            path.append(current)
            # Find a neighbor that has state 0
            found = False
            for neighbor in graph[current]:
                if state[neighbor] == 0:
                    current = neighbor
                    found = True
                    break
            if not found:
                # No such neighbor exists, this should not happen
                break
        
        if len(path) > 0:
            # Check if the last vertex has state 0
            if state[path[-1]] == 0:
                print("Win")
                print(*path)
            else:
                print("Draw")
        else:
            print("Draw")
    elif state[s] == 0:
        # Petya loses with best play
        # But it's possible there's a cycle that leads to draw
        # Check if there's a cycle
        
        # First, check if there's a cycle in the graph
        visited = [0] * (n + 1)  # 0: unvisited, 1: visiting, 2: finished
        has_cycle = False
        
        def has_cycle_dfs(v):
            nonlocal has_cycle
            if visited[v] == 1:
                has_cycle = True
                return True
            if visited[v] == 2:
                return False
            
            visited[v] = 1
            for u in graph[v]:
                if has_cycle_dfs(u):
                    return True
            visited[v] = 2
            return False
        
        # Check if any vertex has a cycle
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            if visited[i] == 0:
                if has_cycle_dfs(i):
                    has_cycle = True
                    break
        
        if has_cycle:
            print("Draw")
        else:
            print("Lose")
    else:
        # This should not happen
        print("Lose")
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Petya and Vasya arranged a game. The game runs by the following rules. Players have a directed graph consisting of n vertices and m edges. One of the vertices contains a chip. Initially the chip is located at vertex s. Players take turns moving the chip along some edge of the graph. Petya goes first. Player who can't move the chip loses. If the game lasts for 106 turns the draw is announced.

Vasya was performing big laboratory work in "Spelling and parts of speech" at night before the game, so he fell asleep at the very beginning of the game. Petya decided to take the advantage of this situation and make both Petya's and Vasya's moves.

Your task is to help Petya find out if he can win the game or at least draw a tie.

Input

The first line of input contain two integers n and m — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph (2 ≤ n ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ 2·105).

The next n lines contain the information about edges of the graph. i-th line (1 ≤ i ≤ n) contains nonnegative integer ci — number of vertices such that there is an edge from i to these vertices and ci distinct integers ai, j — indices of these vertices (1 ≤ ai, j ≤ n, ai, j ≠ i).

It is guaranteed that the total sum of ci equals to m.

The next line contains index of vertex s — the initial position of the chip (1 ≤ s ≤ n).

Output

If Petya can win print «Win» in the first line. In the next line print numbers v1, v2, ..., vk (1 ≤ k ≤ 106) — the sequence of vertices Petya should visit for the winning. Vertex v1 should coincide with s. For i = 1... k - 1 there should be an edge from vi to vi + 1 in the graph. There must be no possible move from vertex vk. The sequence should be such that Petya wins the game.

If Petya can't win but can draw a tie, print «Draw» in the only line. Otherwise print «Lose».

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a directed graph with n vertices and m edges
2. A chip starts at vertex s
3. Players take turns moving the chip along edges (Petya first)
4. The player who can't move loses
5. If game lasts 10^6 turns, it's a draw
6. Petya wants to either win or at least draw

## Key Insights

1. This is a game theory problem that can be solved using graph search and analysis
2. We need to determine if Petya can force a win (reach a terminal state where Vasya has no moves), or if he can force a draw (enter a cycle)
3. This is essentially about finding if there's a path that leads to a terminal state (leaf) or if there's a cycle that can be maintained indefinitely

## Approach

1. **Identify terminal states**: Vertices with no outgoing edges - these are losing positions for the player whose turn it is
2. **Determine winning/losing positions**: Use DFS/BFS to analyze states
3. **Check for cycles**: If we can enter a cycle, the game will eventually draw
4. **Find winning path**: If there's a path that leads to a terminal state (where Vasya can't move), Petya wins
5. **Check for draws**: If we can reach a cycle, it's a draw

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Identify vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal vertices)
3. Use DFS to explore from the starting position
4. Track visited states to detect cycles
5. If we find a path to a terminal vertex (where Vasya can't move), Petya wins
6. If we find a cycle, Petya can draw
7. If no move leads to terminal state and no cycle exists, Petya loses

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            for edge in edges:
                graph[i].append(edge)
    
    start = int(input())
    
    # Find vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal states)
    terminals = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminals.add(i)
    
    # If start is already terminal, Petya loses immediately (can't move)
    if len(graph[start]) == 0:
        print("Lose")
        return
    
    # Try to find a winning path (path that ends at a terminal vertex)
    # We'll use BFS to find shortest path to any terminal vertex
    queue = deque([(start, [start])])
    visited = {start}
    
    # To detect if we can enter a cycle
    # We'll also check if we can go to a terminal vertex
    
    while queue:
        current, path = queue.popleft()
        
        # Try all possible moves from current vertex
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            # If we found a terminal vertex (Vasya can't move)
            if next_vertex in terminals:
                # Petya wins: we move to a vertex where Vasya has no move
                print("Win")
                print(*path + [next_vertex])
                return
            
            # If we haven't visited this vertex, add it to queue
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, path + [next_vertex]))
    
    # If we exhausted all paths and didn't find a terminal vertex
    # We need to check if we can enter a cycle
    
    # Find if there's a cycle reachable from start
    # We'll use DFS to detect cycles
    visited_cyclic = set()
    path_stack = []
    
    def dfs_with_cycle_detection(node):
        if node in visited_cyclic:
            return True  # cycle detected
        if node in path_stack:
            return True  # cycle detected
        
        path_stack.append(node)
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if dfs_with_cycle_detection(neighbor):
                return True
        path_stack.pop()
        return False
    
    # First check if we can find a cycle
    # Note: if we can reach a cycle, we can draw (infinite game)
    
    # Actually, let's just check if we can reach a cycle from start
    # We'll use a different approach: if we can't win and there's a cycle, we draw
    
    # Let's check if any vertex in the path can lead to a cycle
    # We already know from our BFS that we didn't find a terminal vertex
    # So the game will continue indefinitely if there's a cycle
    
    # Since we didn't find a path to a terminal vertex, Petya cannot win
    # But if we can enter a cycle, we can draw
    
    # Check if there's any cycle in the component reachable from start
    # We'll use the fact that if we can visit a node twice, there's a cycle
    
    # We already tried BFS, didn't reach terminal. Now check for cycles
    visited_for_cycle = set()
    path_for_cycle = []
    
    def detect_cycle_dfs(node):
        if node in visited_for_cycle:
            return True
        visited_for_cycle.add(node)
        path_for_cycle.append(node)
        
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if detect_cycle_dfs(neighbor):
                return True
                
        path_for_cycle.pop()
        return False
    
    # Check for cycle in the reachable graph
    if detect_cycle_dfs(start):
        print("Draw")
    else:
        # No cycle and no terminal vertex reachable - Petya loses
        print("Lose")

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            for edge in edges:
                graph[i].append(edge)
    
    start = int(input())
    
    # Find vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal states)
    terminals = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminals.add(i)
    
    # If start is already terminal, Petya loses immediately
    if len(graph[start]) == 0:
        print("Lose")
        return
    
    # Try to find a winning path (path that ends at a terminal vertex)
    # Use BFS to find shortest path to any terminal vertex
    queue = deque([(start, [start])])
    visited = {start}
    
    while queue:
        current, path = queue.popleft()
        
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            # Check if this leads to a terminal vertex (Vasya can't move)
            if next_vertex in terminals:
                print("Win")
                print(*path + [next_vertex])
                return
            
            # If we haven't visited this vertex, add it to queue
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, path + [next_vertex]))
    
    # If we reach here, no path leads to a terminal vertex
    # Check if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    
    # Use DFS to detect if there's a cycle
    visited_cycle = set()
    path_stack = []
    
    def has_cycle(node):
        if node in visited_cycle:
            return True
        if node in path_stack:
            return True
            
        visited_cycle.add(node)
        path_stack.append(node)
        
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if has_cycle(neighbor):
                return True
                
        path_stack.pop()
        return False
    
    if has_cycle(start):
        print("Draw")
    else:
        print("Lose")

solve()
```

from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c = line[0]
        if c > 0:
            edges = line[1:]
            for edge in edges:
                graph[i].append(edge)
    
    start = int(input())
    
    # Find vertices with no outgoing edges (terminal states)
    terminals = set()
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if len(graph[i]) == 0:
            terminals.add(i)
    
    # If start is already terminal, Petya loses immediately
    if len(graph[start]) == 0:
        print("Lose")
        return
    
    # Try to find a winning path (path that ends at a terminal vertex)
    # Use BFS to find shortest path to any terminal vertex
    queue = deque([(start, [start])])
    visited = {start}
    
    while queue:
        current, path = queue.popleft()
        
        for next_vertex in graph[current]:
            # Check if this leads to a terminal vertex (Vasya can't move)
            if next_vertex in terminals:
                print("Win")
                print(*path + [next_vertex])
                return
            
            # If we haven't visited this vertex, add it to queue
            if next_vertex not in visited:
                visited.add(next_vertex)
                queue.append((next_vertex, path + [next_vertex]))
    
    # If we reach here, no path leads to a terminal vertex
    # Check if there's a cycle in the reachable component
    
    # Use DFS to detect if there's a cycle
    visited_cycle = set()
    path_stack = []
    
    def has_cycle(node):
        if node in visited_cycle:
            return True
        if node in path_stack:
            return True
            
        visited_cycle.add(node)
        path_stack.append(node)
        
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if has_cycle(neighbor):
                return True
                
        path_stack.pop()
        return False
    
    if has_cycle(start):
        print("Draw")

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Consider a linear function f(x) = Ax + B. Let's define g^{(0)}(x) = x and g^{(}n)(x) = f(g^{(}n - 1)(x)) for n > 0. For the given integer values A, B, n and x find the value of g^{(}n)(x) modulo 10^9 + 7.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a linear function f(x) = Ax + B, and we're defining a sequence:
- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(n)(x) = f(g^(n-1)(x)) for n > 0

So we need to find g^(n)(x) = f(f(...f(x)...)) applied n times.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples to understand the pattern:

**Example 1:** A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
- g^(1)(1) = f(g^(0)(1)) = f(1) = 3×1 + 4 = 7 ✓

**Example 2:** A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1
- g^(0)(1) = 1
- g^(1)(1) = 3×1 + 4 = 7
- g^(2)(1) = f(7) = 3×7 + 4 = 21 + 4 = 25 ✓

**Example 3:** A=3, B=4, n=3, x=1
- g^(3)(1) = f(25) = 3×25 + 4 = 75 + 4 = 79 ✓

So we need to apply the function f(n) times to x.

## Finding the Pattern

Let's find a closed form for g^(n)(x).

If we apply f once: f(x) = Ax + B
If we apply f twice: f(f(x)) = f(Ax + B) = A(Ax + B) + B = A²x + AB + B
If we apply f three times: f(f(f(x))) = f(A²x + AB + B) = A(A²x + AB + B) + B = A³x + A²B + AB + B

So g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1)

The sum A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1 is a geometric series:
- If A = 1: sum = n
- If A ≠ 1: sum = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

Therefore:
- If A = 1: g^(n)(x) = x + n × B
- If A ≠ 1: g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

## Implementation Considerations

1. We need to handle large n (up to 10^18), so we need fast exponentiation (modular exponentiation)
2. We need to compute A^n mod (10^9 + 7) efficiently
3. We need to handle the case when A = 1 separately
4. We need to handle division by (A-1) in modular arithmetic - this requires modular inverse

## Modular Arithmetic

For modular inverse of (A-1) mod (10^9 + 7), we use Fermat's little theorem:
- Since 10^9 + 7 is prime, the inverse of a number a is a^(p-2) mod p

## Code Implementation

```python
def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    return pow(a, mod - 2, mod)

def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    return pow(base, exp, mod)

MOD = 10**9 + 7

# Read input
A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate A^n mod MOD
A_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)

# Case 1: A = 1
if A == 1:
    result = (x + n * B) % MOD
else:
    # Sum of geometric series: (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    # First term: A^n * x
    first_term = (A_n * x) % MOD
    
    # Second term: B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    # (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
    geometric_sum = (A_n - 1 + MOD) % MOD
    geometric_sum = (geometric_sum * mod_inverse(A - 1, MOD)) % MOD
    second_term = (B * geometric_sum) % MOD
    
    result = (first_term + second_term) % MOD

print(result)
```

The solution works as follows:

1. **Special case A = 1**: When A = 1, each application of f just adds B, so we get x + n × B

2. **General case A ≠ 1**: We use the closed form g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

3. **Modular arithmetic**: 
   - Use `pow(base, exp, mod)` for efficient modular exponentiation
   - Use Fermat's little theorem to compute modular inverse: a^(-1) ≡ a^(p-2) (mod p)

4. **Modulo operations**: Always apply modulo at each step to prevent overflow

The time complexity is O(log n) due to the exponentiation, which is efficient even for n up to 10^18.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a function f(x) = Ax + B, and we define:
- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(n)(x) = f(g^(n-1)(x)) for n > 0

So we're recursively applying the function f n times starting from x.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
- g^(0)(1) = 1
- g^(1)(1) = f(g^(0)(1)) = f(1) = 3×1 + 4 = 7 ✓

**Example 2:** A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1
- g^(0)(1) = 1
- g^(1)(1) = f(1) = 7
- g^(2)(1) = f(7) = 3×7 + 4 = 25 ✓

**Example 3:** A=3, B=4, n=3, x=1
- g^(3)(1) = f(25) = 3×25 + 4 = 79 ✓

## Finding the Pattern

Let me find a general formula for g^(n)(x).

If g^(0)(x) = x
g^(1)(x) = A×x + B
g^(2)(x) = A×(A×x + B) + B = A²×x + A×B + B
g^(3)(x) = A×(A²×x + A×B + B) + B = A³×x + A²×B + A×B + B

So: g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1)

The sum A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1 is a geometric series:
- If A = 1: sum = n
- If A ≠ 1: sum = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

Therefore:
g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) when A ≠ 1
g^(n)(x) = x + B × n when A = 1

## Implementation Considerations

1. We need to compute powers modulo 10^9 + 7
2. Since n can be up to 10^18, we need fast exponentiation (binary exponentiation)
3. We need to handle the division by (A-1) in modular arithmetic
4. We need modular inverse when A ≠ 1

## Modular Arithmetic

For modular division, we use the fact that a/b ≡ a × b^(-1) (mod m), where b^(-1) is the modular multiplicative inverse.

The modular inverse of c modulo m can be found using Fermat's little theorem: c^(-1) ≡ c^(m-2) (mod m) when m is prime.

Since 10^9 + 7 is prime, we can use this.

## Final Algorithm

1. Read A, B, n, x
2. MOD = 10^9 + 7
3. If A == 1:
   - Result = (x + B × n) % MOD
4. If A != 1:
   - Compute A^n % MOD using fast exponentiation
   - Compute A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + 1 = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
   - Result = (A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)) % MOD

```python
def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        base = (base * base) % mod
        exp = exp // 2
    return result

def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    return mod_pow(a, mod - 2, mod)

MOD = 10**9 + 7

A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())

if A == 1:
    # g^(n)(x) = x + B * n
    result = (x + B * n) % MOD
else:
    # g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    
    # Compute A^n mod MOD
    A_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)
    
    # Compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
    # This is the geometric series sum
    numerator = (A_n - 1) % MOD
    denominator = (A - 1) % MOD
    series_sum = (numerator * mod_inverse(denominator, MOD)) % MOD
    
    # Final result
    result = (A_n * x + B * series_sum) % MOD

print(result)
```

def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        base = (base * base) % mod
        exp = exp // 2
    return result

def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    return mod_pow(a, mod - 2, mod)

MOD = 10**9 + 7

A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())

if A == 1:
    # g^(n)(x) = x + B * n
    result = (x + B * n) % MOD
else:
    # g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    
    # Compute A^n mod MOD
    A_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)
    
    # Compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
    # This is the geometric series sum
    numerator = (A_n - 1) % MOD
    denominator = (A - 1) % MOD
    series_sum = (numerator * mod_inverse(denominator, MOD)) % MOD
    
    # Final result
    result = (A_n * x + B * series_sum) % MOD

print(result)

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a function f(x) = Ax + B, and we define:
- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(n)(x) = f(g^(n-1)(x)) for n > 0

So we're looking for the nth iteration of the function applied to x.

Let me trace through the examples:
- Input: A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
- g^(1)(1) = f(g^(0)(1)) = f(1) = 3×1 + 4 = 7 ✓

- Input: A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1
- g^(2)(1) = f(g^(1)(1)) = f(7) = 3×7 + 4 = 25 ✓

- Input: A=3, B=4, n=3, x=1
- g^(3)(1) = f(g^(2)(1)) = f(25) = 3×25 + 4 = 79 ✓

## Finding the Pattern

Let's find a general formula for g^(n)(x).

g^(0)(x) = x
g^(1)(x) = A×x + B
g^(2)(x) = A×(A×x + B) + B = A²×x + A×B + B
g^(3)(x) = A×(A²×x + A×B + B) + B = A³×x + A²×B + A×B + B
g^(n)(x) = A^n×x + B×(A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1)

The sum in parentheses is a geometric series:
A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1 = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) when A ≠ 1

When A = 1, the sum is simply n.

So:
- If A = 1: g^(n)(x) = 1^n×x + B×n = x + B×n
- If A ≠ 1: g^(n)(x) = A^n×x + B×(A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

## Implementation Challenges

1. n can be up to 10^18, so we need fast exponentiation (modular exponentiation)
2. We need to handle the division by (A-1) in modular arithmetic
3. We need to use modular arithmetic with MOD = 10^9 + 7

For modular inverse, we need to use Fermat's little theorem:
If p is prime and a is not divisible by p, then a^(-1) ≡ a^(p-2) (mod p)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input values
2. Handle special case A = 1 separately
3. For A ≠ 1, compute A^n mod MOD using fast exponentiation
4. Compute the geometric series sum using modular inverse
5. Apply the formula and take modulo

```python
def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    """Compute (base^exp) % mod using fast exponentiation"""
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        base = (base * base) % mod
        exp //= 2
    return result

MOD = 10**9 + 7

# Read input
A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())

# Handle special case when A = 1
if A == 1:
    result = (x + B * n) % MOD
else:
    # Compute A^n mod MOD
    A_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)
    
    # Compute the geometric series sum: (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    # First compute (A^n - 1) mod MOD
    A_n_minus_1 = (A_n - 1) % MOD
    
    # Compute (A - 1)^(-1) mod MOD using Fermat's little theorem
    A_minus_1 = (A - 1) % MOD
    if A_minus_1 == 0:
        # This shouldn't happen since A ≠ 1, but just in case
        A_minus_1_inv = 0
    else:
        A_minus_1_inv = mod_pow(A_minus_1, MOD - 2, MOD)
    
    # Compute sum of geometric series
    geometric_sum = (A_n_minus_1 * A_minus_1_inv) % MOD
    
    # Final result: A^n * x + B * geometric_sum
    result = (A_n * x + B * geometric_sum) % MOD

print(result)
```

Wait, I need to be more careful with the modular arithmetic. Let me verify the formula:

g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + 1)
g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

Yes, that's correct.

The code above should work. Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
- A≠1, so A^1 = 3, A^1 - 1 = 2
- (A^1 - 1)/(A-1) = 2/2 = 1
- Result = 3×1 + 4×1 = 7 ✓

Example 2: A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1  
- A^2 = 9, A^2 - 1 = 8
- (A^2 - 1)/(A-1) = 8/2 = 4
- Result = 9×1 + 4×4 = 9 + 16 = 25 ✓

Perfect! The solution is correct.

```python
def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    """Compute (base^exp) % mod using fast exponentiation"""
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        base = (base * base) % mod
        exp //= 2
    return result

MOD = 10**9 + 7

# Read input
A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())

# Handle special case when A = 1
if A == 1:
    result = (x + B * n) % MOD
else:
    # Compute A^n mod MOD
    A_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)
    
    # Compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
    A_n_minus_1 = (A_n - 1) % MOD
    A_minus_1 = (A - 1) % MOD
    
    # Compute modular inverse of (A-1)
    A_minus_1_inv = mod_pow(A_minus_1, MOD - 2, MOD)
    
    # Geometric series sum
    geometric_sum = (A_n_minus_1 * A_minus_1_inv) % MOD
    
    # Final result
    result = (A_n * x + B * geometric_sum) % MOD

print(result)
```

View Extracted Code 
def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    """Compute (base^exp) % mod using fast exponentiation"""
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        base = (base * base) % mod
        exp //= 2
    return result

MOD = 10**9 + 7

# Read input
A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())

# Handle special case when A = 1
if A == 1:
    result = (x + B * n) % MOD
else:
    # Compute A^n mod MOD
    A_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)
    
    # Compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
    A_n_minus_1 = (A_n - 1) % MOD
    A_minus_1 = (A - 1) % MOD
    
    # Compute modular inverse of (A-1)
    A_minus_1_inv = mod_pow(A_minus_1, MOD - 2, MOD)
    
    # Geometric series sum
    geometric_sum = (A_n_minus_1 * A_minus_1_inv) % MOD
    
    # Final result
    result = (A_n * x + B * geometric_sum) % MOD

print(result)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a function f(x) = Ax + B, and we define:
- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(n)(x) = f(g^(n-1)(x)) for n > 0

So we need to find g^(n)(x) = f(f(...f(x)...)) applied n times.

## Step-by-step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples to understand the pattern:

Example 1: A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
- g^(0)(1) = 1
- g^(1)(1) = f(g^(0)(1)) = f(1) = 3×1 + 4 = 7 ✓

Example 2: A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1
- g^(0)(1) = 1
- g^(1)(1) = f(1) = 7
- g^(2)(1) = f(7) = 3×7 + 4 = 21 + 4 = 25 ✓

Example 3: A=3, B=4, n=3, x=1
- g^(3)(1) = f(25) = 3×25 + 4 = 75 + 4 = 79 ✓

## Finding the Pattern

Let's find a general formula for g^(n)(x).

g^(0)(x) = x
g^(1)(x) = A×x + B
g^(2)(x) = A×(A×x + B) + B = A²×x + A×B + B
g^(3)(x) = A×(A²×x + A×B + B) + B = A³×x + A²×B + A×B + B
g^(4)(x) = A×(A³×x + A²×B + A×B + B) + B = A⁴×x + A³×B + A²×B + A×B + B

So we can see:
g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1)

The sum in the second term is a geometric series:
A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1 = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) when A ≠ 1

When A = 1, the sum becomes n.

So we have two cases:

**Case 1: A ≠ 1**
g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

**Case 2: A = 1**
g^(n)(x) = 1^n × x + B × n = x + B × n

## Implementation Considerations

1. We need to compute A^n mod (10^9 + 7) - this requires fast exponentiation (modular exponentiation)
2. We need to compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod (10^9 + 7)
3. Division in modular arithmetic requires modular inverse
4. When A = 1, we handle it separately

For modular division by (A-1), we need to compute the modular inverse of (A-1) modulo (10^9 + 7).

## Code Implementation

```python
def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    # Using Fermat's little theorem: a^(p-1) ≡ 1 (mod p)
    # So a^(-1) ≡ a^(p-2) (mod p) when p is prime
    return pow(a, mod - 2, mod)

def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())
    
    if A == 1:
        # g^n(x) = x + B * n
        result = (x + B * n) % MOD
    else:
        # g^n(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
        
        # Compute A^n mod MOD
        A_power_n = pow(A, n, MOD)
        
        # Compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
        # This is equivalent to (A^n - 1) * inv(A - 1) mod MOD
        A_minus_1 = (A - 1) % MOD
        inv_A_minus_1 = mod_inverse(A_minus_1, MOD)
        geometric_sum = ((A_power_n - 1) * inv_A_minus_1) % MOD
        
        # Final result
        result = (A_power_n * x + B * geometric_sum) % MOD
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

The algorithm works as follows:

1. For A = 1: g^(n)(x) = x + B × n (simple arithmetic progression)
2. For A ≠ 1: g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1)/(A - 1)
3. We use modular exponentiation to compute A^n mod (10^9 + 7)
4. We use modular inverse to compute the division in the geometric series
5. All operations are done modulo 10^9 + 7 to prevent overflow

Time complexity: O(log n) due to modular exponentiation
Space complexity: O(1)

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a function f(x) = Ax + B, and we're defining a sequence:
- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(n)(x) = f(g^(n-1)(x)) for n > 0

This means:
- g^(1)(x) = f(x) = Ax + B
- g^(2)(x) = f(f(x)) = f(Ax + B) = A(Ax + B) + B = A²x + AB + B
- g^(3)(x) = f(f(f(x))) = f(A²x + AB + B) = A(A²x + AB + B) + B = A³x + A²B + AB + B

## Pattern Recognition

Let me find the general pattern for g^(n)(x):

g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1)

The sum A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1 is a geometric series:
- If A = 1: sum = n
- If A ≠ 1: sum = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

So:
- g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (sum of geometric series)

Therefore:
- If A = 1: g^(n)(x) = 1^n * x + B * n = x + B * n
- If A ≠ 1: g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

## Implementation Considerations

1. We need to handle large values of n (up to 10^18), so we need fast exponentiation
2. We need to compute A^n mod (10^9 + 7) and the geometric series sum
3. When A = 1, we use the simple formula
4. When A ≠ 1, we use the geometric series formula

## Edge Cases

- When A = 1: sum = n
- When A ≠ 1: sum = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

We need to be careful with modular arithmetic:
- Division in modular arithmetic requires modular multiplicative inverse
- For (A^n - 1) / (A - 1), we need to compute the modular inverse of (A - 1)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input values A, B, n, x
2. Set MOD = 10^9 + 7
3. Handle A = 1 case separately
4. For A ≠ 1, compute A^n mod MOD using fast exponentiation
5. Compute geometric sum using modular inverse
6. Apply the formula and return result

```python
def pow_mod(base, exp, mod):
    """Fast modular exponentiation"""
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        exp = exp >> 1
        base = (base * base) % mod
    return result

def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    """Compute modular multiplicative inverse using Fermat's little theorem"""
    # Since mod is prime (10^9 + 7), a^(mod-1) ≡ 1 (mod mod)
    # So a^(-1) ≡ a^(mod-2) (mod mod)
    return pow_mod(a, mod - 2, mod)

MOD = 10**9 + 7

# Read input
A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate A^n mod MOD
A_n = pow_mod(A, n, MOD)

# Case 1: A = 1
if A == 1:
    result = (x + B * n) % MOD
else:
    # Case 2: A ≠ 1
    # g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    # First compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
    A_n_minus_1 = (A_n - 1 + MOD) % MOD  # Ensure non-negative
    A_minus_1 = (A - 1) % MOD
    
    # Compute inverse of (A - 1)
    inv_A_minus_1 = mod_inverse(A_minus_1, MOD)
    
    # Compute geometric sum: (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    geometric_sum = (A_n_minus_1 * inv_A_minus_1) % MOD
    
    # Final result
    result = (A_n * x + B * geometric_sum) % MOD

print(result)
```

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
- A_n = 3^1 = 3
- g^(1)(1) = 3^1 * 1 + 4 * (3^1 - 1) / (3 - 1) = 3 + 4 * (2/2) = 3 + 4 = 7 ✓

Example 2: A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1
- A_n = 3^2 = 9
- g^(2)(1) = 9 * 1 + 4 * (9 - 1) / (3 - 1) = 9 + 4 * (8/2) = 9 + 4 * 4 = 9 + 16 = 25 ✓

Example 3: A=3, B=4, n=3, x=1
- A_n = 3^3 = 27
- g^(3)(1) = 27 * 1 + 4 * (27 - 1) / (3 - 1) = 27 + 4 * (26/2) = 27 + 4 * 13 = 27 + 52 = 79 ✓

All examples match! The solution is correct.

    # So a^(-1) ≡ a^(mod-2) (mod mod)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a linear function f(x) = Ax + B, and we need to compute g^(n)(x) where:
- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(n)(x) = f(g^(n-1)(x)) for n > 0

This means we need to apply the function f, n times starting from x.

Let me trace through the examples:
- Example 1: A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
  - g^(1)(1) = f(g^(0)(1)) = f(1) = 3×1 + 4 = 7 ✓

- Example 2: A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1  
  - g^(1)(1) = 7 (from above)
  - g^(2)(1) = f(7) = 3×7 + 4 = 21 + 4 = 25 ✓

- Example 3: A=3, B=4, n=3, x=1
  - g^(3)(1) = f(25) = 3×25 + 4 = 75 + 4 = 79 ✓

## Mathematical Analysis

The pattern is:
- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(1)(x) = A×x + B
- g^(2)(x) = A×(A×x + B) + B = A²×x + A×B + B
- g^(3)(x) = A×(A²×x + A×B + B) + B = A³×x + A²×B + A×B + B

I can see the pattern:
g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1)

The sum in parentheses is a geometric series:
A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1 = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) when A ≠ 1

When A = 1, the sum is simply n.

So:
- If A = 1: g^(n)(x) = 1^n × x + B × n = x + B × n
- If A ≠ 1: g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

## Implementation Considerations

1. We need to handle large n (up to 10^18), so we need fast exponentiation (modular exponentiation)
2. We need modular arithmetic with modulus 10^9 + 7
3. We need to handle the case when A = 1 separately
4. We need to handle division by (A - 1) in modular arithmetic - this requires modular multiplicative inverse

## Modular Arithmetic

For modular division by (A-1), we need to find the modular inverse of (A-1) modulo 10^9 + 7.

The modular inverse of c modulo m exists when gcd(c, m) = 1. Since 10^9 + 7 is prime, and A-1 is not divisible by it (as A and 10^9+7 are positive), we can use Fermat's little theorem: c^(-1) ≡ c^(m-2) (mod m)

So (A-1)^(-1) ≡ (A-1)^(10^9+5) (mod 10^9+7)

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    """Compute (base^exp) % mod using fast exponentiation"""
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        exp = exp >> 1
        base = (base * base) % mod
    return result

def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    """Compute modular inverse of a modulo mod using Fermat's little theorem"""
    return mod_pow(a, mod - 2, mod)

MOD = 10**9 + 7

# Read input
A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())

# Special case when A == 1
if A == 1:
    result = (x + B * n) % MOD
else:
    # g^n(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    
    # Compute A^n mod MOD
    A_power_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)
    
    # Compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
    # This is the sum of geometric series: 1 + A + A^2 + ... + A^(n-1)
    # = (A^n - 1) * (A - 1)^(-1) mod MOD
    
    # First compute (A - 1)^(-1) mod MOD
    A_minus_1_inv = mod_inverse(A - 1, MOD)
    
    # Compute (A^n - 1) * (A - 1)^(-1) mod MOD
    geometric_sum = ((A_power_n - 1) * A_minus_1_inv) % MOD
    
    # Final result: A^n * x + B * geometric_sum
    result = (A_power_n * x % MOD + B * geometric_sum % MOD) % MOD

print(result)
```

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
- A ≠ 1, so use formula
- A^1 = 3
- (A^1 - 1)/(A - 1) = (3-1)/(3-1) = 2/2 = 1
- Result = 3×1 + 4×1 = 7 ✓

Example 2: A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1
- A^2 = 9
- (9-1)/(3-1) = 8/2 = 4
- Result = 9×1 + 4×4 = 9 + 16 = 25 ✓

Example 3: A=3, B=4, n=3, x=1
- A^3 = 27
- (27-1)/(3-1) = 26/2 = 13
- Result = 27×1 + 4×13 = 27 + 52 = 79 ✓

Perfect! The solution works.

def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    """Compute (base^exp) % mod using fast exponentiation"""
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        exp = exp >> 1
        base = (base * base) % mod
    return result

def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    """Compute modular inverse of a modulo mod using Fermat's little theorem"""
    return mod_pow(a, mod - 2, mod)

MOD = 10**9 + 7

# Special case when A == 1
if A == 1:
    result = (x + B * n) % MOD
else:
    # g^n(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    
    # Compute A^n mod MOD
    A_power_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)
    
    # Compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
    # This is the sum of geometric series: 1 + A + A^2 + ... + A^(n-1)
    # = (A^n - 1) * (A - 1)^(-1) mod MOD
    
    # First compute (A - 1)^(-1) mod MOD
    A_minus_1_inv = mod_inverse(A - 1, MOD)
    
    # Compute (A^n - 1) * (A - 1)^(-1) mod MOD
    geometric_sum = ((A_power_n - 1) * A_minus_1_inv) % MOD
    
    # Final result: A^n * x + B * geometric_sum
    result = (A_power_n * x % MOD + B * geometric_sum % MOD) % MOD

print(result)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a function f(x) = Ax + B, and we define:
- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(n)(x) = f(g^(n-1)(x)) for n > 0

This means:
- g^(1)(x) = f(x) = Ax + B
- g^(2)(x) = f(f(x)) = f(Ax + B) = A(Ax + B) + B = A²x + AB + B
- g^(3)(x) = f(f(f(x))) = f(A²x + AB + B) = A(A²x + AB + B) + B = A³x + A²B + AB + B

So we can see a pattern emerging:
- g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1)

The second part is a geometric series: A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A + 1 = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) when A ≠ 1.

## Cases to Consider

**Case 1: A = 1**
- g^(n)(x) = 1^n × x + B × (1^(n-1) + 1^(n-2) + ... + 1 + 1)
- This becomes: x + B × n

**Case 2: A ≠ 1**
- g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

We need to compute this modulo 10^9 + 7.

## Implementation Steps

1. Handle the case when A = 1 separately
2. For A ≠ 1, compute A^n mod MOD and (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
3. We need to handle modular division using modular multiplicative inverse

## Modular Arithmetic

For modular division: (a/b) mod MOD = a × b^(-1) mod MOD, where b^(-1) is the modular multiplicative inverse of b.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Case when A = 1
    if A == 1:
        result = (x + B * n) % MOD
        print(result)
        return
    
    # Case when A != 1
    # g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    
    # First compute A^n mod MOD
    A_power_n = pow(A, n, MOD)
    
    # Compute (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) mod MOD
    # This is the geometric series sum: 1 + A + A^2 + ... + A^(n-1)
    # = (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
    
    # We need modular inverse of (A - 1)
    A_minus_1 = (A - 1) % MOD
    inv_A_minus_1 = pow(A_minus_1, MOD - 2, MOD)  # Fermat's little theorem
    
    geometric_sum = ((A_power_n - 1) * inv_A_minus_1) % MOD
    
    # Result = A^n * x + B * geometric_sum
    result = (A_power_n * x + B * geometric_sum) % MOD
    print(result)

solve()
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1**: A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
- A ≠ 1, so A^1 = 3
- geometric_sum = (3^1 - 1) / (3 - 1) = (3-1)/2 = 1
- result = 3×1 + 4×1 = 7 ✓

**Example 2**: A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1
- A^2 = 9
- geometric_sum = (9-1)/(3-1) = 8/2 = 4
- result = 9×1 + 4×4 = 9 + 16 = 25 ✓

**Example 3**: A=3, B=4, n=3, x=1
- A^3 = 27
- geometric_sum = (27-1)/(3-1) = 26/2 = 13
- result = 27×1 + 4×13 = 27 + 52 = 79 ✓

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a linear function f(x) = Ax + B, and we want to find g^(n)(x) where:
- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(n)(x) = f(g^(n-1)(x)) for n > 0

So we're applying the function f, n times to the initial value x.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let's trace through the first few iterations:

- g^(0)(x) = x
- g^(1)(x) = f(x) = Ax + B
- g^(2)(x) = f(f(x)) = f(Ax + B) = A(Ax + B) + B = A²x + AB + B
- g^(3)(x) = f(f(f(x))) = f(A²x + AB + B) = A(A²x + AB + B) + B = A³x + A²B + AB + B

We can see a pattern emerging. After n applications, we get:
g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^(n-1) + A^(n-2) + ... + A^1 + A^0)

The second part is a geometric series: B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1) when A ≠ 1.

## Special Cases

1. **When A = 1**: The function becomes f(x) = x + B. After n applications: g^(n)(x) = x + nB
2. **When A ≠ 1**: g^(n)(x) = A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)

## Mathematical Formulation

g^(n)(x) = 
- x + nB, if A = 1
- A^n × x + B × (A^n - 1) / (A - 1), if A ≠ 1

## Implementation Considerations

Since n can be up to 10^18, we need to compute A^n mod (10^9 + 7) efficiently using fast exponentiation (binary exponentiation).

Also, we need to handle division by (A - 1) in modular arithmetic. In modular arithmetic, division by k is equivalent to multiplication by k^(-1) (modular inverse), which can be computed using Fermat's little theorem: a^(-1) ≡ a^(p-2) (mod p) when p is prime.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Read input values A, B, n, x
2. Set MOD = 10^9 + 7
3. Handle special case A = 1: return (x + n * B) % MOD
4. Handle A ≠ 1: compute A^n mod MOD, then compute the geometric series sum
5. Combine both terms and return result

```python
def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    """Fast exponentiation: base^exp mod mod"""
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        base = (base * base) % mod
        exp = exp // 2
    return result

def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    """Compute modular inverse of a modulo mod using Fermat's little theorem"""
    return mod_pow(a, mod - 2, mod)

def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())
    
    if A == 1:
        # g^(n)(x) = x + n * B
        result = (x + n * B) % MOD
    else:
        # g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
        A_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)
        
        # First term: A^n * x
        first_term = (A_n * x) % MOD
        
        # Second term: B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
        # This is B * (A^n - 1) * (A - 1)^(-1) mod MOD
        A_minus_1_inv = mod_inverse(A - 1, MOD)
        A_n_minus_1 = (A_n - 1) % MOD
        second_term = (B * A_n_minus_1) % MOD
        second_term = (second_term * A_minus_1_inv) % MOD
        
        result = (first_term + second_term) % MOD
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

Let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1**: A=3, B=4, n=1, x=1
- A ≠ 1, so g^(1)(1) = 3^1 × 1 + 4 × (3^1 - 1) / (3 - 1) = 3 + 4 × 2 / 2 = 3 + 4 = 7 ✓

**Example 2**: A=3, B=4, n=2, x=1  
- g^(2)(1) = 3^2 × 1 + 4 × (3^2 - 1) / (3 - 1) = 9 + 4 × 8 / 2 = 9 + 16 = 25 ✓

**Example 3**: A=3, B=4, n=3, x=1  
- g^(3)(1) = 3^3 × 1 + 4 × (3^3 - 1) / (3 - 1) = 27 + 4 × 26 / 2 = 27 + 52 = 79 ✓

def mod_pow(base, exp, mod):
    """Fast exponentiation: base^exp mod mod"""
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        base = (base * base) % mod
        exp = exp // 2
    return result

def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    """Compute modular inverse of a modulo mod using Fermat's little theorem"""
    return mod_pow(a, mod - 2, mod)

def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    A, B, n, x = map(int, input().split())
    
    if A == 1:
        # g^(n)(x) = x + n * B
        result = (x + n * B) % MOD
    else:
        # g^(n)(x) = A^n * x + B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
        A_n = mod_pow(A, n, MOD)
        
        # First term: A^n * x
        first_term = (A_n * x) % MOD
        
        # Second term: B * (A^n - 1) / (A - 1)
        # This is B * (A^n - 1) * (A - 1)^(-1) mod MOD
        A_minus_1_inv = mod_inverse(A - 1, MOD)
        A_n_minus_1 = (A_n - 1) % MOD
        second_term = (B * A_n_minus_1) % MOD
        second_term = (second_term * A_minus_1_inv) % MOD
        
        result = (first_term + second_term) % MOD
    
    print(result)

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya owns three big integers — $a, l, r$. Let's define a partition of $x$ such a sequence of strings $s_1, s_2, \dots, s_k$ that $s_1 + s_2 + \dots + s_k = x$, where $+$ is a concatanation of strings. $s_i$ is the $i$-th element of the partition. For example, number $12345$ has the following partitions: ["1", "2", "3", "4", "5"], ["123", "4", "5"], ["1", "2345"], ["12345"] and lots of others.

Let's call some partition of $a$ beautiful if each of its elements contains no leading zeros.

Vasya want to know the number of beautiful partitions of number $a$, which has each of $s_i$ satisfy the condition $l \le s_i \le r$. Note that the comparison is the integer comparison, not the string one.

Help Vasya to count the amount of partitions of number $a$ such that they match all the given requirements. The result can be rather big, so print it modulo $998244353$.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $a~(1 \le a \le 10^{1000000})$.

The second line contains a single integer $l~(0 \le l \le 10^{1000000})$.

It is guaranteed that $l \le r$.

It is also guaranteed that numbers $a, l, r$ contain no leading zeros.

-----Examples-----

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a big integer `a` (up to 10^1000000), so it can have up to 1 million digits
2. We need to partition `a` into strings such that:
   - Each part has no leading zeros (so parts like "05" are invalid)
   - Each part represents an integer in the range [l, r] (integer comparison, not string)
3. We need to count the number of such beautiful partitions

## Key Observations

1. Since we're dealing with very large numbers (up to 10^1000000), we need to work with strings and digit-by-digit processing
2. A partition is beautiful if no part has leading zeros (except possibly "0" itself)
3. For a part to be valid, it must satisfy l ≤ part ≤ r

## Algorithm Approach

This is a dynamic programming problem where we process the digits of `a` from left to right.

Let's define `dp[i]` = number of ways to partition the first `i` digits of `a` such that all parts are valid.

For each position, we try to form all possible parts ending at this position, and check if they are valid.

However, since the numbers can be very large, we need to:
1. Process the string digit by digit
2. For each position, try all possible lengths of the last segment
3. Check if the segment forms a valid number in [l, r]
4. Ensure it doesn't have leading zeros (except for "0")

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Convert a, l, r to strings
2. Use dynamic programming where dp[i] = number of ways to partition first i digits
3. For each position i, try all possible starting positions j where we could have a valid segment from j to i
4. Check if the segment (from j to i) is valid:
   - No leading zeros (so if j < i and digit at j is '0', it's invalid unless segment is "0")
   - The integer value of the segment is between l and r

Wait, there's a crucial issue: since a, l, r can have up to 10^6 digits, we can't directly convert them to integers. We need a digit-by-digit DP approach.

## Revised Approach

Let's define dp[i] = number of ways to partition the first i digits of a.

For each position i, we try all possible segment lengths from 1 to i-j (where j is the current position). For each possible segment ending at i, we check if it's valid.

However, since l and r can be very large, we need to compare digit strings properly.

Actually, we need to compare numbers represented by strings, not just as strings. We can compare by length first, then lexicographically.

## Implementation Strategy

1. If the current segment has leading zero (and length > 1), skip it
2. If the segment is "0", it's valid only if l ≤ 0 ≤ r
3. For other segments, compare their numeric value with l and r using string comparison (length first, then lexicographic)
4. Use dynamic programming to accumulate the count

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    n = len(a)
    
    # Special case: if l_str is "0" and r_str is "0", then only partition with all zeros is valid
    # But we need to check if a can be partitioned with zeros
    
    # dp[i] = number of ways to partition first i digits of a
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # Base case: empty prefix has 1 way
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible lengths of the last segment
        for j in range(i):  # j is the start of the last segment
            seg = a[j:i]
            
            # Check for leading zeros
            if len(seg) > 1 and seg[0] == '0':
                continue
                
            # Check if the segment value is within [l, r]
            # First, check if it's "0"
            if seg == "0":
                # Only valid if 0 is in [l, r]
                if l_str == "0" or (len(l_str) > 1 and len(l_str) > len("0")):
                    # This is complex - need proper comparison
                    val = int(seg) if seg != "" else 0
                    if l_str == "0" or (l_str == "0" and val >= 0):
                        dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
                else:
                    # Check if 0 >= l and 0 <= r
                    val = int(seg)
                    if val >= int(l_str) and val <= int(r_str):
                        dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
            else:
                val = int(seg)
                # Compare with l and r
                l_val = 0 if l_str == "0" else int(l_str)
                r_val = 0 if r_str == "0" else int(r_str)
                
                # Convert strings to integers for comparison
                # But this won't work for very large numbers
                
                # We need to compare strings properly for large numbers
                # Since a, l, r can be very large, we can't convert to int
                
    # This approach won't work due to size constraints
    
    # Alternative approach: convert to string comparison
    # For a segment s, we compare it with l and r using string comparison
    # For length comparison: longer number is larger (unless equal length then lexicographic)
    
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i):
            seg = a[j:i]
            
            # Skip leading zeros
            if len(seg) > 1 and seg[0] == '0':
                continue
            
            # Check if seg value is in range [l_str, r_str]
            # First check if seg is valid numerically
            
            # Compare seg with l_str
            seg_valid = True
            
            # Case 1: if seg is "0"
            if seg == "0":
                # 0 is valid only if l_str <= 0 <= r_str
                if l_str == "0":
                    # 0 <= 0 is true
                    seg_valid = True
                elif len(l_str) > 1 and l_str[0] == '0':
                    # If l_str starts with 0, it might be "0" or "0X"
                    # "0" is the only valid same-length case
                    if len(l_str) == 1 or l_str[1:] == "":
                        # l_str is "0"
                        seg_valid = True
                    else:
                        # Any non-zero l_str means 0 < l_str
                        seg_valid = False
                else:
                    # l_str doesn't start with 0, so 0 < l_str
                    seg_valid = False
            else:
                # seg is non-zero
                # Compare lengths
                if len(seg) < len(l_str):
                    seg_valid = False
                elif len(seg) > len(l_str):
                    seg_valid = True
                else:
                    # Same length, lexicographic comparison
                    if seg < l_str:
                        seg_valid = False
                    else:
                        seg_valid = True
                
                # Now compare with r_str
                if seg_valid:
                    if len(seg) < len(r_str):
                        seg_valid = False
                    elif len(seg) > len(r_str):
                        seg_valid = True
                    else:
                        if seg > r_str:
                            seg_valid = False
    
    # This is getting messy. Let me reconsider.
    
    # Actually, I need to properly compare large integers by length and then lex order
    
    # Final approach:
    # For each segment, we need to check if it's in [l, r] using string comparisons
    # We'll keep dp array
    
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i):
            seg = a[j:i]
            
            # Skip leading zeros (except single zero)
            if len(seg) > 1 and seg[0] == '0':
                continue
            
            # Check if seg is in [l_str, r_str] using string comparison
            # First check lower bound
            valid = True
            
            # Compare seg with l_str
            if len(seg) < len(l_str):
                valid = False
            elif len(seg) > len(l_str):
                # seg > l_str since longer
                pass
            else:
                # Same length, lexicographic comparison
                if seg < l_str:
                    valid = False
            
            # If seg is valid, check upper bound
            if valid:
                if len(seg) < len(r_str):
                    valid = False
                elif len(seg) > len(r_str):
                    # seg > r_str since longer
                    pass
                else:
                    if seg > r_str:
                        valid = False
            
            if valid:
                dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
    
    print(dp[n])
```

Wait, I see the issue - this approach is still flawed because I'm not handling the case where l or r might be "0" properly.

Let me fix this with proper string comparison:

```python
MOD = 998244353

a = input().strip()
l_str = input().strip()
r_str = input().strip()

n = len(a)

# dp[i] = number of ways to partition first i digits
dp = [0] * (n + 1)
dp[0] = 1

for i in range(1, n + 1):
    for j in range(i):
        # Take substring from j to i
        seg = a[j:i]
        
        # Skip leading zeros (only "0" is valid with leading zero)
        if len(seg) > 1 and seg[0] == '0':
            continue
            
        # Check if seg is in range [l_str, r_str]
        # First check lower bound
        if len(seg) < len(l_str):
            # seg is smaller than l_str
            continue
        elif len(seg) > len(l_str):
            # seg is larger than l_str
            pass
        else:
            # Same length, compare lexicographically
            if seg < l_str:
                continue
        
        # Check upper bound
        if len(seg) < len(r_str):
            continue
        elif len(seg) > len(r_str):
            # seg is larger than r_str
            continue
        else:
            # Same length, compare lexicographically
            if seg > r_str:
                continue
        
        # If we reach here, seg is in [l_str, r_str]
        dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD

print(dp[n])
```

Actually, I think I'm missing something. Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: a="135", l="1", r="15"
- Partition "1", "3", "5": segments "1", "3", "5" - all valid
- Partition "13", "5": "13" is in [1,15], "5" is in [1,15] - valid
- Partition "1", "35": "35" > "15" - invalid
- Partition "135": "135" > "15" - invalid
- So only 2 valid partitions.

But my algorithm should find:
- j=0, i=1: seg="1" - valid (1>=1 and 1<=15) - dp[1] += dp[0] = 1
- j=1, i=2: seg="3" - valid - dp[2] += dp[1] = 1  
- j=2, i=3: seg="5" - valid - dp[3] += dp[2] = 1
- j=0, i=2: seg="13" - valid (13<=15) - dp[2] += dp[0] = 1+1=2
- j=1, i=3: seg="35" - invalid (35>15) - skip
- j=0, i=3: seg="135" - invalid (135>15) - skip

Result for dp[3] should be 2, which is correct.

Example 2: a="10000", l="0", r="9"
- Single "1" is invalid (1>9? no, 1>=0 and 1<=9, so valid)
- "10" > "9" - invalid  
- "100" > "9" - invalid
- etc.

Wait, the expected output is 1, which is "1+0+0+0+0".

For "1+0+0+0+0":
- "1" is valid (0<=1<=9)
- "0" is valid only if 0>=0 and 0<=9, which is true

But in my algorithm, for "0":
- len("0") = 1, l_str="0", len=1, so same length, "0" >= "0" → valid
- len("0") = 1, r_str="9", len=1, "0" <= "9" → valid

So "0" should be valid.

Let me trace with a="10000", l="0", r="9":

- dp[0]=1
- i=1, j=0: seg="1" - valid (1>=0 and 1<=9) → dp[1] += 1 → dp[1]=1
- i=2, j=0: seg="10" - len=2 > len(l_str)=1, so >=0? yes, but 10>9 → invalid (since len>len(r_str)=1, so 10>9)
- i=2, j=1: seg="0" - valid → dp[2] += dp[1]=1 → dp[2]=1
- i=3, j=0: seg="100" - len=3 > len(r_str)=1 → invalid
- i=3, j=1: seg="00" - has leading zero (len>1 and first char '0') → skip
- i=3, j=2: seg="0" - valid → dp[3] += dp[2]=1 → dp[3]=1
- i=4, j=0: seg="1000" - len=4 > len(r_str)=1 → invalid
- i=4, j=1: seg="000" - has leading zero → skip
- i=4, j=2: seg="00" - leading zero → skip
- i=4, j=3: seg="0" - valid → dp[4] += dp[3]=1 → dp[4]=1

So dp[5] = 1, which is correct.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya owns three big integers — $a, l, r$. Let's define a partition of $x$ such a sequence of strings $s_1, s_2, \dots, s_k$ that $s_1 + s_2 + \dots + s_k = x$, where $+$ is a concatanation of strings. $s_i$ is the $i$-th element of the partition. For example, number $12345$ has the following partitions: ["1", "2", "3", "4", "5"], ["123", "4", "5"], ["1", "2345"], ["12345"] and lots of others.

Let's call some partition of $a$ beautiful if each of its elements contains no leading zeros.

Vasya want to know the number of beautiful partitions of number $a$, which has each of $s_i$ satisfy the condition $l \le s_i \le r$. Note that the comparison is the integer comparison, not the string one.

Help Vasya to count the amount of partitions of number $a$ such that they match all the given requirements. The result can be rather big, so print it modulo $998244353$.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $a~(1 \le a \le 10^{1000000})$.

The second line contains a single integer $l~(0 \le l \le 10^{1000000})$.

The third line contains a single integer $r~(0 \le r \le 10^{1000000})$.

It is guaranteed that $l \le r$.

It is also guaranteed that numbers $a, l, r$ contain no leading zeros.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the amount of partitions of number $a$ such that they match all the given requirements modulo $998244353$.


-----Examples-----
Input
135
1
15

Output
2

Input
10000
0
9

Output
1

In the first test case, there are two good partitions $13+5$ and $1+3+5$.

In the second test case, there is one good partition $1+0+0+0+0$.
