Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a big integer `a` (up to 10^1000000 digits), so we need to handle it as a string
2. We need to partition `a` into strings `s_1, s_2, ..., s_k` such that:
   - All elements have no leading zeros (so "01" is invalid, "1" is valid)
   - Each element `s_i` represents an integer in the range `[l, r]` (integer comparison, not string)
3. We need to count the number of such beautiful partitions

## Key Insights

1. Since `a` can be extremely large (up to 10^1000000), we can't convert it to integers directly
2. We need to process the digits of `a` from left to right
3. For each position, we need to consider all possible cuts that would form valid parts
4. A digit string is invalid if it has leading zeros (unless it's just "0")
5. We need to check if a substring represents a number in [l, r]

## Algorithm Approach

This is a dynamic programming problem on strings:

1. Let `dp[i]` = number of valid partitions for the prefix `a[0:i]`
2. For each position `i`, we try all possible ending positions `j` where `j < i` and check if `a[j:i]` is a valid number in `[l, r]`
3. We only allow cuts that don't create leading zeros (so no "0" at the start of a part unless the part is "0")

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Convert the input strings to actual string representations
2. For each position, try all possible substrings ending at that position
3. For each substring, check:
   - No leading zeros (unless the substring is "0")
   - The integer value is in range [l, r]
4. Use dynamic programming to accumulate counts

However, since the numbers are huge (up to 10^1000000), we need to be careful about the range checking.

## Range Checking Optimization

For a substring `s` of `a`:
- If `s` has leading zeros (and length > 1), it's invalid
- Otherwise, we need to check if `int(s)` is in `[l, r]`

But `int(s)` could be extremely large. We can compare strings with appropriate length handling.

## Implementation Strategy

1. Use DP where `dp[i]` = number of ways to partition `a[0:i]`
2. Initialize `dp[0] = 1` (empty prefix has one way)
3. For each position `i` from 1 to len(a):
   - For each starting position `j` from 0 to i-1:
     - Extract substring `s = a[j:i]`
     - Check if `s` has leading zeros (i.e., if `s[0] == '0' and len(s) > 1`)
     - If yes, skip
     - Otherwise, check if `s` represents a number in `[l, r]` using string comparison
     - If valid, add `dp[j]` to `dp[i]`

For the range check, we can compare by length first, then lexicographically.

```python
def solve():
    a = input().strip()
    l = input().strip()
    r = input().strip()
    
    MOD = 998244353
    
    # Convert strings to lists for easier manipulation
    n = len(a)
    
    # dp[i] = number of ways to partition a[0:i]
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # empty prefix has one way
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i):
            # substring from j to i-1
            substr = a[j:i]
            
            # Check leading zeros - invalid if has leading zero and length > 1
            if len(substr) > 1 and substr[0] == '0':
                continue
            
            # Check if substr represents a number in [l, r]
            # First check length
            if len(substr) < len(l):
                # If length is less than l, it's smaller than l
                continue
            elif len(substr) > len(r):
                # If length is greater than r, it's larger than r
                continue
            elif len(substr) == len(l):
                # Same length - compare lexicographically
                if substr < l:
                    continue
                elif substr > r:
                    continue
            elif len(substr) == len(r):
                # Same length - compare lexicographically  
                if substr < l:
                    continue
                elif substr > r:
                    continue
            else:  # len(substr) is between len(l) and len(r)
                # Check if it's >= l and <= r
                if len(substr) < len(l) or len(substr) > len(r):
                    continue
                # Compare lexicographically
                if substr < l or substr > r:
                    continue
            
            # If we reach here, the substring is valid
            dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
    
    return dp[n]

print(solve())
```

```python
            # Check leading zeros - invalid if has leading zero and length > 1
            if len(substr) > 1 and substr[0] == '0':
                continue
            
            # Check if substr represents a number in [l, r]
            # Compare with l and r
            # First by length
            if len(substr) < len(l):
                # shorter than l, so smaller than l
                continue
            elif len(substr) > len(r):
                # longer than r, so larger than r
                continue
            elif len(substr) == len(l):

                if substr < l:

                # Actually, if len(substr) is between len(l) and len(r), 
                # we need to check bounds
                if len(substr) > len(r):

                if substr < l or substr > r:

            dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a big integer `a` (up to 10^1000000), which means it can have up to 1 million digits
2. We need to partition `a` into strings such that:
   - Each string has no leading zeros (except for "0" itself)
   - Each string represents an integer in the range [l, r] (integer comparison)
3. We need to count the number of such beautiful partitions

## Key Insights

1. Since `a` can be very large (up to 10^1000000), we can't convert it to integers - we need to work with string representations
2. For any partition, each substring must:
   - Have no leading zeros (so if the substring starts with '0', it can only be "0")
   - Be between `l` and `r` in integer value
3. We can use dynamic programming on the string positions

## Algorithm Design

1. Convert `a` to a string `s` and work with its digits
2. Use DP where `dp[i]` = number of ways to partition the first `i` digits of `a`
3. For each position `i`, try all possible ways to form the last segment ending at position `i`
4. For each possible segment length from 1 to `i`, check if the substring from `i-length` to `i` is valid:
   - No leading zeros (unless it's "0")
   - Its integer value is between `l` and `r`

## Implementation Details

1. We need to handle very large numbers, so we'll use string comparison and conversion
2. For checking if a substring value is in [l, r], we need to:
   - Skip leading zeros (except for "0")
   - Compare the numeric values using string comparison for large numbers
3. We can precompute the numeric values and compare them

## Step-by-step solution

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    # If l or r is "0", we need special handling for zero
    # For zero: only "0" is allowed, and it can only appear as a segment
    
    # DP: dp[i] = number of ways to partition first i digits
    n = len(a)
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # empty prefix has 1 way
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible lengths of the last segment
        for j in range(1, i + 1):
            # Segment from i-j to i-1 (0-indexed)
            segment_str = a[i-j:i]
            
            # Check if this segment has leading zeros (except for "0")
            if segment_str[0] == '0' and len(segment_str) > 1:
                continue  # leading zeros - invalid
                
            # Convert to integer for comparison (but we need to handle very large numbers)
            # Since strings can be very long, we can compare string-wise
            # But we need to actually convert to integer or compare properly
            
            # Actually, since the numbers can be very large, we'll need to compare strings
            # For positive numbers, lexicographic order matches numeric order
            # For zero, we handle separately
            
            # Check if the segment represents a valid number in [l, r]
            try:
                segment_val = int(segment_str)
            except:
                continue
                
            # Now compare with l and r
            l_val = 0
            if l_str == "0":
                l_val = 0
            else:
                try:
                    l_val = int(l_str)
                except:
                    l_val = 0
                    
            r_val = 0
            if r_str == "0":
                r_val = 0
            else:
                try:
                    r_val = int(r_str)
                except:
                    r_val = 0
                    
            # For very large numbers, the string comparison works when both are positive
            # But we need to handle cases where L or R is 0
            if l_val == 0 and r_val == 0:
                # Only valid if segment is 0
                if segment_val == 0:
                    dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
            elif l_val == 0:
                # segment >= 0, need segment <= r_val and segment >= 0
                if segment_val <= r_val:
                    dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
            elif r_val == 0:
                # segment <= 0, so segment must be 0
                if segment_val == 0:
                    dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
            else:
                # normal case: l_val > 0, r_val > 0
                if l_val <= segment_val <= r_val:
                    dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
    
    return dp[n]

# However, the above approach fails for very large numbers (like 10^1000000)
# We need to handle string comparison for very large numbers

def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    n = len(a)
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible lengths of the last segment
        for j in range(1, i + 1):
            # Segment from i-j to i-1
            segment_str = a[i-j:i]
            
            # Check leading zeros
            if segment_str[0] == '0' and len(segment_str) > 1:
                continue
                
            # Compare with l and r using string comparison
            # Since all numbers are non-negative, string comparison works
            # for the same number of digits: shorter string is smaller
            # for same length: lexicographic order
            
            # Special case: zero
            if segment_str == "0":
                # Zero is valid only if l <= 0 <= r
                if l_str == "0" or (l_str != "0" and len(l_str) == 1 and l_str[0] == '0'):
                    # l <= 0
                    if r_str == "0" or (r_str != "0" and len(r_str) == 1 and r_str[0] == '0'):
                        dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
                else:
                    # l > 0, so 0 < l, invalid
                    continue
            else:
                # Non-zero segment
                # Need to check if segment is in [l, r]
                # Convert to strings for comparison
                
                # Check if segment >= l
                l_valid = True
                r_valid = True
                
                # Compare segment_str with l_str
                if l_str == "0":
                    l_valid = True
                elif segment_str == l_str:
                    l_valid = True
                elif len(segment_str) > len(l_str):
                    l_valid = True
                elif len(segment_str) < len(l_str):
                    l_valid = False
                else:
                    # Same length, compare lexicographically
                    l_valid = (segment_str >= l_str)
                
                # Compare segment_str with r_str
                if r_str == "0":
                    r_valid = (segment_str == "0")
                elif segment_str == r_str:
                    r_valid = True
                elif len(segment_str) > len(r_str):
                    r_valid = False
                elif len(segment_str) < len(r_str):
                    r_valid = True
                else:
                    # Same length, compare lexicographically
                    r_valid = (segment_str <= r_str)
                
                if l_valid and r_valid:
                    dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
    
    return dp[n]

# The above still has issues with the zero comparison logic
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the comparison logic. For large numbers, string comparison doesn't work directly because "1" < "10" but numerically 1 < 10. The lexicographic comparison works for numbers of same length, but for different lengths, the longer one is larger.

Actually, for non-negative integers, string comparison (lexicographic) gives the correct numeric ordering:
- If one string is a prefix of another, the shorter one is smaller
- If they have same length, lexicographic order works
- If different lengths, the longer one is larger

But "1" vs "10": "1" is a prefix of "10", so "1" < "10" correctly

So string comparison should work for non-negative integers.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya owns three big integers — $a, l, r$. Let's define a partition of $x$ such a sequence of strings $s_1, s_2, \dots, s_k$ that $s_1 + s_2 + \dots + s_k = x$, where $+$ is a concatanation of strings. $s_i$ is the $i$-th element of the partition. For example, number $12345$ has the following partitions: ["1", "2", "3", "4", "5"], ["123", "4", "5"], ["1", "2345"], ["12345"] and lots of others.

Let's call some partition of $a$ beautiful if each of its elements contains no leading zeros.

Vasya want to know the number of beautiful partitions of number $a$, which has each of $s_i$ satisfy the condition $l \le s_i \le r$. Note that the comparison is the integer comparison, not the string one.

Help Vasya to count the amount of partitions of number $a$ such that they match all the given requirements. The result can be rather big, so print it modulo $998244353$.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $a~(1 \le a \le 10^{1000000})$.

The second line contains a single integer $l~(0 \le l \le 10^{1000000})$.

The third line contains a single integer $r~(0 \le r \le 10^{1000000})$.

It is guaranteed that $l \le r$.

It is also guaranteed that numbers $a, l, r$ contain no leading zeros.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the amount of partitions of number $a$ such that they match all the given requirements modulo $998244353$.


-----Examples-----
Input
135
1
15

Output
2

Input
10000
0
9

Output
1

In the first test case, there are two good partitions $13+5$ and $1+3+5$.

In the second test case, there is one good partition $1+0+0+0+0$.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a big integer `a` (up to 10^1000000 digits), so we need to handle it as a string
2. We need to partition `a` into substrings where each substring represents an integer
3. Each substring must:
   - Not have leading zeros (except for "0" itself)
   - Have value between `l` and `r` (inclusive)
4. We need to count the number of beautiful partitions

## Key Observations

1. Since `a` can have up to 1000000 digits, we can't convert it to regular integers
2. We need to use dynamic programming on the string representation
3. For each position in `a`, we consider all possible lengths of the next substring
4. A substring can't have leading zeros - if it starts with '0', it can only be "0"
5. We need to check if the numeric value of each substring is in [l, r]

## Algorithm Steps

1. Convert `a` to string for digit-by-digit processing
2. Use dynamic programming where `dp[i]` = number of valid partitions for the prefix `a[0:i]`
3. For each position `i`, try all possible lengths `j` for the last substring (from position `i-j` to `i`)
4. Check if the substring `a[i-j:i]`:
   - Has no leading zeros (is "0" or starts with non-zero digit)
   - Has value between `l` and `r`
5. If valid, add `dp[i-j]` to `dp[i]`

## Challenges

1. Comparing large numbers (up to 10^1000000) - need to compare strings lexicographically for the same length
2. Leading zeros - any substring starting with '0' must be exactly "0"
3. The numbers `l` and `r` are also very large

## Special Cases

- If a substring starts with '0', it can only be "0" (if 0 is in [l, r])
- We need to compare the numeric value of substrings with l and r

## Implementation Strategy

For comparing two numbers represented as strings:
- If one string is shorter, it's smaller (unless both are "0")
- If same length, lexicographical comparison works

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    n = len(a)
    
    # Handle edge cases
    if n == 0:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Convert to string comparisons
    # For comparison of string numbers: compare length first, then lexicographically
    def compare_nums(num_str, target_str):
        # Compare two string representations of numbers
        len1, len2 = len(num_str), len(target_str)
        
        # If one is empty, it's smaller
        if len1 == 0:
            return -1
        if len2 == 0:
            return 1
            
        # If different lengths, shorter is smaller (unless both are 0)
        if len1 != len2:
            return -1 if len1 < len2 else 1
            
        # Same length, lexicographic comparison
        return -1 if num_str < target_str else (1 if num_str > target_str else 0)
    
    def compare_with_target(num_str, target_str):
        # Compare num_str with target_str
        if len(num_str) < len(target_str):
            return -1
        elif len(num_str) > len(target_str):
            return 1
        else:
            return -1 if num_str < target_str else (1 if num_str > target_str else 0)
    
    # DP: dp[i] = number of valid partitions for a[0:i]
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # Empty prefix has one partition (empty)
    
    # Process each position
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible lengths of the last substring
        for j in range(1, i + 1):
            # Substring from i-j to i-1 (0-indexed)
            substring = a[i-j:i]
            
            # Check leading zeros
            if substring[0] == '0' and len(substring) > 1:
                # Leading zero - invalid
                continue
            
            # Check if substring is "0" and 0 is in [l, r]
            if substring == "0":
                # Check if 0 is valid
                if is_in_range("0", l_str, r_str):
                    dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
                continue
            
            # For non-zero substrings, check if value is in [l, r]
            if is_in_range(substring, l_str, r_str):
                dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
    
    print(dp[n])

def is_in_range(num_str, l_str, r_str):
    # Check if num_str is in [l_str, r_str]
    # First, check if num_str is greater than r_str
    if len(num_str) > len(r_str):
        return False
    elif len(num_str) < len(r_str):
        return True
    else:
        # Same length - lexicographic comparison
        if num_str > r_str:
            return False
        if num_str < l_str:
            return False
        return True

solve()
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    a = input().strip()
    l = input().strip()
    r = input().strip()
    
    n = len(a)
    
    # Check if a number string is in range [l, r]
    def in_range(num_str, low_str, high_str):
        # First, compare lengths
        len_num = len(num_str)
        len_low = len(low_str)
        len_high = len(high_str)
        
        # If num is longer than high, it's too big
        if len_num > len_high:
            return False
        # If num is shorter than low, it's too small (unless low is "0")
        if len_num < len_low:
            # But if low is "0", then any positive number is >= 0
            if low_str == "0":
                return True
            else:
                return False
        
        # Same length - lexicographic comparison
        if len_num == len_high:
            # Check if <= high
            if num_str > high_str:
                return False
            # Check if >= low
            if num_str < low_str:
                return False
        elif len_num == len_low:
            # Check if >= low
            if num_str < low_str:
                return False
            # Check if <= high
            if num_str > high_str:
                return False
        
        # All conditions satisfied
        return True
    
    # DP: dp[i] = number of valid partitions for prefix a[0:i]
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # Empty prefix
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible lengths for the last substring
        for j in range(1, i + 1):
            # Substring from i-j to i-1
            substr = a[i-j:i]
            
            # Check leading zeros - substring with leading zero must be "0"
            if substr[0] == '0' and len(substr) > 1:
                continue
            
            # Check if this substring is valid (in range)
            if substr == "0":
                # 0 is valid only if 0 is in [l, r]
                if in_range("0", l, r):
                    dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
            else:
                # Normal positive number
                if in_range(substr, l, r):
                    dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
    
    print(dp[n])

solve()
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a large integer `a` (up to 10^1000000), so we need to handle it as a string
2. We need to partition `a` into segments such that:
   - Each segment has no leading zeros (so "01" is invalid, "0" is valid if the segment is "0")
   - Each segment's integer value is between `l` and `r` (inclusive)
3. We need to count the number of such beautiful partitions

## Key Insights

1. Since `a` can be extremely large (up to 10^1000000), we must process it character by character
2. We need dynamic programming where `dp[i]` represents the number of ways to partition the first `i` digits of `a`
3. For each position, we try all possible segment endings and check if the segment value is valid
4. We need to handle leading zeros carefully - a segment starting with '0' is only valid if it's exactly "0"

## Algorithm Steps

1. Convert `a`, `l`, `r` to strings for digit-by-digit processing
2. Use dynamic programming where `dp[i]` = number of ways to partition first `i` digits
3. For each position `i`, try all possible segment lengths `j` from 1 to `i`
4. Extract the substring `a[i-j:i]` and check if it forms a valid segment:
   - If the segment starts with '0' and has length > 1, it's invalid (leading zero)
   - If the segment starts with '0' and has length 1, it's "0", which is valid
   - Convert the segment to integer and check if it's in [l, r]
5. Apply modulo 998244353

## Edge Cases

- `l = 0`: segment "0" is valid
- Numbers with leading zeros: only "0" is valid
- Very large numbers that exceed r: invalid segments

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    # Convert to lists of digits for easier manipulation
    a_digits = [int(c) for c in a]
    l_digits = [int(c) for c in l_str]
    r_digits = [int(c) for c in r_str]
    
    n = len(a)
    
    # Handle the case where l = 0 or r = 0
    # We need to check if the value is within range
    
    # dp[i] = number of ways to partition first i digits
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # Empty partition
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible segment lengths ending at position i
        for j in range(1, i + 1):
            # Segment from position i-j to i-1 (0-indexed)
            start = i - j
            segment_str = ''.join(str(a_digits[pos]) for pos in range(start, i))
            
            # Check for leading zero - only valid if it's exactly "0" or starts with non-zero
            if segment_str[0] == '0' and len(segment_str) > 1:
                continue  # leading zero invalid
            
            # Convert to integer - need to handle very large numbers
            # For this, we'll compare as strings with length constraints
            segment_val = int(segment_str)
            
            # Check if the segment value is in [l, r]
            # First, check if it's valid to start with zero
            if segment_str[0] == '0':
                # Only valid if the segment is "0"
                if not segment_str or segment_str == "0":
                    if l_digits and segment_val < int(''.join(map(str, l_digits))) or \
                       r_digits and segment_val > int(''.join(map(str, r_digits))):
                        continue
                else:
                    continue
            
            # For the case where segment starts with non-zero, we can safely compare
            # But we need to be careful about number size
            
            # Since a, l, r can be extremely large, we need to compare string values
            # with same length, or by length difference
            
            # First, check if segment_val is within bounds
            # We need to compare as integers, but they might be huge
            
            # Since we're dealing with digit strings, we can compare by converting
            # But the numbers can be huge (10^1000000), so we can't convert to int
            
            # Alternative: compare as strings with lexicographic ordering
            # For same length: lex order matches numerical order
            # For different lengths: shorter numbers are smaller
            
            # Check bounds using string comparison
            if l_str == "0":
                # l is 0, so any non-negative number is >= l
                # We need to check if segment_val <= r
                # Compare segment_str with r_str lexicographically
                if len(r_str) == 0 or segment_str == "0":
                    # Handle edge case
                    pass
                # Since l is 0, we only need to check upper bound
                if len(segment_str) > len(r_str):
                    continue
                elif len(segment_str) == len(r_str):
                    if segment_str > r_str:
                        continue
                else:
                    # len(segment_str) < len(r_str)
                    # So segment_val < r_val since shorter number is smaller
                    pass
            else:
                # l > 0, so segment must be at least l
                # Both l and segment have positive values
                if len(l_str) > len(segment_str):
                    continue
                elif len(l_str) == len(segment_str):
                    if l_str > segment_str:
                        continue
                else:
                    # len(l_str) < len(segment_str)
                    # So l_val < segment_val since longer number is larger
                    pass
            
            # Check upper bound
            if len(r_str) == 0 or segment_str == "0":
                # r is 0, only "0" is valid
                if segment_str != "0":
                    continue
            else:
                if len(segment_str) > len(r_str):
                    continue
                elif len(segment_str) == len(r_str):
                    if segment_str > r_str:
                        continue
                else:
                    # len(segment_str) < len(r_str)
                    # So segment_val < r_val since shorter number is smaller
                    pass
            
            # If we made it here, the segment is valid
            dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[start]) % MOD
    
    print(dp[n])

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    n = len(a)
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(1, i + 1):
            # Segment from i-j to i-1 (0-indexed)
            segment = a[i-j:i]
            
            # Check leading zeros
            if segment[0] == '0' and len(segment) > 1:
                continue
                
            # Check bounds using string comparison
            # First, check if segment value >= l
            if l_str == "0":
                # Any non-negative number is >= 0, so we only need upper bound
                # Check if segment value <= r
                if len(r_str) < len(segment):
                    continue
                elif len(r_str) == len(segment):
                    if segment > r_str:
                        continue
                # If len(r_str) > len(segment), segment < r
                # So it's valid
            else:
                # l > 0, so segment must be >= l
                if len(l_str) > len(segment):
                    continue
                elif len(l_str) == len(segment):
                    if l_str > segment:
                        continue
                # If len(l_str) < len(segment), segment > l (since longer number is larger)
                # So we need to check upper bound
                
                # Check if segment value <= r
                if len(r_str) < len(segment):
                    continue
                elif len(r_str) == len(segment):
                    if segment > r_str:
                        continue
                # If len(r_str) > len(segment), segment < r
                
            # If we passed all checks, add to dp
            dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[i-j]) % MOD
    
    print(dp[n])

solve()
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya owns three big integers — $a, l, r$. Let's define a partition of $x$ such a sequence of strings $s_1, s_2, \dots, s_k$ that $s_1 + s_2 + \dots + s_k = x$, where $+$ is a concatanation of strings. $s_i$ is the $i$-th element of the partition. For example, number $12345$ has the following partitions: ["1", "2", "3", "4", "5"], ["123", "4", "5"], ["1", "2345"], ["12345"] and lots of others.

Let's call some partition of $a$ beautiful if each of its elements contains no leading zeros.

Vasya want to know the number of beautiful partitions of number $a$, which has each of $s_i$ satisfy the condition $l \le s_i \le r$. Note that the comparison is the integer comparison, not the string one.

Help Vasya to count the amount of partitions of number $a$ such that they match all the given requirements. The result can be rather big, so print it modulo $998244353$.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $a~(1 \le a \le 10^{1000000})$.

The second line contains a single integer $l~(0 \le l \le 10^{1000000})$.

The third line contains a single integer $r~(0 \le r \le 10^{1000000})$.

It is guaranteed that $l \le r$.

It is also guaranteed that numbers $a, l, r$ contain no leading zeros.


-----Output-----

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a big integer `a` and two bounds `l` and `r`
2. We need to count the number of ways to partition `a` into substrings (digits) such that:
   - Each substring represents a valid integer (no leading zeros)
   - Each substring's integer value is between `l` and `r` (inclusive)
3. The result should be modulo 998244353

## Key Observations

1. Since `a` can have up to 1,000,000 digits, we can't convert it to a regular integer
2. We need to process the digits of `a` from left to right
3. For each position, we need to consider all possible splits (substrings) that satisfy the constraints
4. A substring has leading zeros only if it starts with '0' and has length > 1

## Algorithm Steps

1. Convert the big integers to strings
2. Use dynamic programming where `dp[i]` represents the number of beautiful partitions for the first `i` digits of `a`
3. For each position `i`, try all possible substrings ending at position `i`
4. For each substring from `j` to `i`, check if:
   - It doesn't have leading zeros (either length 1, or starts with '1'-'9')
   - The integer value is between `l` and `r`
5. Add the valid partitions to `dp[i]`

## Special Cases

- If `l == 0`, then substrings starting with '0' are allowed (but they must be single digit '0')
- If a substring starts with '0' and has length > 1, it has leading zeros (invalid)
- We need to compare the integer values of substrings with `l` and `r`

## Implementation Strategy

Since the numbers are huge, we need to:
1. Process strings directly
2. For each possible substring, convert to integer form (by comparing digit-by-digit)
3. Use memoization or iterative DP

The key insight is that we can process the digits from left to right and at each position, try all possible substring lengths ending at current position.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    # Convert to strings for digit manipulation
    # We need to compare integers, so we'll compare digit by digit
    
    n = len(a)
    
    # Special case: if l and r are 0, then we need to handle zeros carefully
    
    # Convert l and r strings to comparable integers
    def string_to_int(s):
        if s == "":
            return 0
        return [int(c) for c in s]
    
    def int_compare(s1, s2):
        # Compare two digit strings lexicographically (which gives correct integer comparison)
        len1, len2 = len(s1), len(s2)
        if len1 != len2:
            return len1 - len2
        for i in range(len1):
            if s1[i] != s2[i]:
                return ord(s1[i]) - ord(s2[i])
        return 0
    
    # For a substring from i to j, we need to check if it represents a valid integer in [l, r]
    def is_valid_range(substr, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(substr) > 1 and substr[0] == '0':
            return False
        
        # Compare the integer value with l and r
        # Compare digit by digit
        l_val = string_to_int(l_str)
        r_val = string_to_int(r_str)
        
        if len(substr) > len(l_str):
            return False  # too long (greater than r if r has shorter length)
        
        if len(substr) < len(l_str):
            return True  # smaller than l if l has more digits, but we need to check
            
        # Same length
        result = int_compare(substr, l_str)
        if result < 0:
            return False
        if result > 0:
            return True
            
        # Equal in length, now compare digit by digit
        for i in range(len(substr)):
            if substr[i] != l_str[i]:
                return substr[i] > l_str[i]
                
        return True
    
    # Actually, simpler: compare length first, then lex
    def in_range(substr, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(substr) > 1 and substr[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        # Compare lengths first
        len_substr = len(substr)
        len_l = len(l_str)
        len_r = len(r_str)
        
        # If substring length is less than l's length, it's smaller than l
        if len_substr < len_l:
            return False
            
        # If substring length is greater than r's length, it's greater than r
        if len_substr > len_r:
            return False
            
        # Same length, compare lexicographically
        if len_substr == len_l:
            # Less than l
            if substr < l_str:
                return False
            # Greater than r  
            if substr > r_str:
                return False
            # Equal to l or r (we need to check if in range)
            return True
            
        if len_substr == len_r:
            # Less than l
            if substr < l_str:
                return False
            # Greater than r  
            if substr > r_str:
                return False
            # Equal to l or r
            return True
            
        # This should not happen with proper bounds
        return False
    
    # Actually, we can simplify - we need to check if the integer value is in [l, r]
    def valid_substring(substr, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(substr) > 1 and substr[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        # Compare to l and r
        len_substr = len(substr)
        len_l = len(l_str)
        len_r = len(r_str)
        
        # If len_substr < len_l, then value < l (since l has more digits)
        if len_substr < len_l:
            return False
            
        # If len_substr > len_r, then value > r
        if len_substr > len_r:
            return False
            
        # If equal in length, compare lexicographically
        if len_substr == len_l:
            if substr < l_str:
                return False
            if substr > r_str:
                return False
        elif len_substr == len_r:
            if substr < l_str:
                return False
            if substr > r_str:
                return False
                
        return True
    
    # DP: dp[i] = number of beautiful partitions for first i digits
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # empty partition
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible starting positions j for the last substring
        for j in range(i):
            substr = a[j:i]
            if valid_substring(substr, l_str, r_str):
                dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
    
    print(dp[n])
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a big integer `a` (up to 10^1000000 digits), and we need to partition it into strings such that:
   - Each partition element has no leading zeros (so "01" is invalid)
   - Each element `s_i` must satisfy `l ≤ s_i ≤ r` (integer comparison)
   - We need to count the number of such beautiful partitions

2. The key constraints:
   - No leading zeros means if a number starts with '0', it must be exactly "0"
   - The comparison is integer comparison, not string comparison

## Analysis

Let me think about the constraints:

1. **Leading zeros**: If we have a multi-digit number in our partition, it cannot start with '0'. So we can only form numbers like "12", "345", etc., not "01", "002", etc.

2. **Integer comparison**: We need to check if the integer value of each partition element is between l and r (inclusive).

3. **Partitioning**: We're splitting the string representation of `a` into consecutive substrings.

## Key Insights

1. Since we're dealing with very large numbers (up to 10^1000000), we can't convert them to integers in regular Python (they'd be too big).

2. We need to work with the string representation of `a`.

3. For each possible cut position in the string, we need to check if the resulting substring forms a valid number (no leading zeros) and if its integer value is between l and r.

4. This is a dynamic programming problem where we can define `dp[i]` = number of beautiful partitions for the first `i` digits of `a`.

5. For each position `i`, we try all possible cuts `j` where `j < i`, and check if the substring `a[j:i]` is valid.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Convert `a` to string (already given)
2. For each position, try all possible substrings ending at that position
3. For each substring:
   - Check if it has leading zeros (except "0" itself)
   - Convert to integer (but we need to be careful with very large numbers)
4. Use dynamic programming to count valid partitions

But the numbers can be extremely large (up to 10^1000000), so we can't convert them to integers directly.

## Alternative Approach

Since we're comparing with `l` and `r`, we need to compare the numerical values of substrings with these bounds.

We can:
1. Preprocess the string of `a`
2. For each possible substring of `a`, determine if its integer value is in [l, r]
3. Use DP where `dp[i]` = number of valid partitions for first `i` digits

## Implementation Plan

1. Convert all inputs to strings
2. Use dynamic programming: `dp[i]` = number of beautiful partitions for first `i` characters of `a`
3. Base case: `dp[0] = 1` (empty partition)
4. For each position `i` from 1 to len(a):
   - For each possible start position `j` from 0 to `i-1`:
     - Extract substring `a[j:i]`
     - Check if it has leading zeros (unless it's "0")
     - Check if integer value is in [l, r]
     - If valid, add `dp[j]` to `dp[i]`

But we still need to compare large numbers. We can compare strings lexicographically with proper handling of lengths.

## String Comparison Rules

- If `len(x) < len(y)`, then `x < y` if x is not "0"
- If `len(x) > len(y)`, then `x > y` 
- If same length, compare lexicographically

However, we need to handle the case where one number is "0" and others are positive.

## Detailed Solution

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    # If l or r is "0", we need special handling
    # But we can handle it with string comparison
    
    # DP: dp[i] = number of beautiful partitions for first i digits
    n = len(a)
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # Empty partition
    
    # Preprocess: determine if a substring s is valid (no leading zeros and value in [l, r])
    def is_valid(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
        
        # Compare with l and r
        # First check length
        if len(s) < len(l_str):
            return False
        elif len(s) > len(r_str):
            return False
        elif len(s) == len(l_str):
            # Same length - compare lexicographically
            if s < l_str:
                return False
            if s > r_str:
                return False
            return True
        else:  # len(s) > len(l_str) and len(s) <= len(r_str)
            # If length is greater than l_str, it's at least as large as l_str
            if len(s) > len(r_str):
                return False
            if len(s) == len(r_str):
                if s > r_str:
                    return False
                if s < l_str:
                    return False
                return True
            # len(s) > len(l_str) and len(s) < len(r_str) - so it's in range
            return True
    
    # Actually, the comparison logic is wrong. Let me fix:
    def compare(s, target):
        # Compare string s with target (both strings)
        # Return true if s <= target, false otherwise
        slen, tlen = len(s), len(target)
        if slen < tlen:
            return True
        elif slen > tlen:
            return False
        else:
            return s <= target
    
    def is_valid(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        # Check bounds
        if compare(s, l_str) == False:  # s < l
            return False
        if compare(s, r_str) == False:  # s > r
            return False
            
        return True
    
    # Actually, I think I have the logic backwards. Let me fix:
    # If s < l_str, return false
    # If s > r_str, return false
    
    def is_valid(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        # Compare with l_str
        slen, l_len, r_len = len(s), len(l_str), len(r_str)
        
        # Check if s is less than l_str
        if slen < l_len:
            return False
        elif slen > l_len:
            # s is longer, so it's larger than any number with fewer digits
            if slen > r_len:
                return False
            # slen == l_len, so compare lexicographically
            elif slen == l_len:
                if s < l_str:
                    return False
                if s > r_str:
                    return False
                return True
            # slen > l_len and slen <= r_len - so it's in range
            if slen > r_len:
                return False
            # slen <= r_len
            # Since slen > l_len, we need to check if it's within r
            if slen == r_len:
                if s > r_str:
                    return False
                return True
            # slen < r_len - in range
            return True  # Actually this is wrong
        # slen == l_len
        if s < l_str:
            return False
        if s > r_str:
            return False
        return True
    
    # Let me simplify: the correct comparison is:
    def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        # Compare lengths first
        slen = len(s)
        l_len, r_len = len(l_str), len(r_str)
        
        # If s is shorter than l, it's too small
        if slen < l_len:
            return False
        # If s is longer than r, it's too big
        if slen > r_len:
            return False
        # If same length, compare lexicographically
        if slen == l_len:
            # s must be >= l_str and <= r_str
            if s < l_str or s > r_str:
                return False
            return True
        # slen > l_len and slen <= r_len
        # Since slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, s is larger than l (because longer number)
        # and s is at most as large as r (since slen <= r_len)
        # But we need to check if it's <= r_str
        if slen == r_len:
            if s > r_str:
                return False
            return True
        # slen < r_len - definitely in range
        return True
    
    # Even better: since we know l <= r, we can compare
    def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        slen = len(s)
        l_len, r_len = len(l_str), len(r_str)
        
        # If s is shorter than l, too small
        if slen < l_len:
            return False
        # If s is longer than r, too big
        if slen > r_len:
            return False
        # If same length, compare lexicographically
        if slen == l_len:
            if s < l_str or s > r_str:
                return False
            return True
        # If slen > l_len and slen <= r_len
        # s has more digits than l, so it's larger than l
        # s has fewer or equal digits than r
        if slen == r_len:
            return s <= r_str
        # slen < r_len - so it's less than r
        return True
    
    # Actually, if slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, then s is > l (since longer number) and s <= r (since slen <= r_len)
    # So it's automatically in range when slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, unless r_len < slen
    def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        slen = len(s)
        l_len, r_len = len(l_str), len(r_str)
        
        # If s is shorter than l, too small
        if slen < l_len:
            return False
        # If s is longer than r, too big
        if slen > r_len:
            return False
        # If same length, compare lexicographically
        if slen == l_len:
            if s < l_str or s > r_str:
                return False
            return True
        # If slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, then s > l and s <= r (since longer than l, shorter or equal than r)
        return True
    
    # But this is wrong: what if r_str is "10" and s is "100"? s has 3 digits, r_str has 2 digits, so s > r_str
    # So we have to compare numerically when lengths differ
    
    # Correct comparison: if slen > r_len, then s > r_str
    # if slen < r_len, then s < r_str (if slen < r_len)
    # if slen == r_len, then compare lexicographically
    
    def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        slen = len(s)
        l_len, r_len = len(l_str), len(r_str)
        
        # If s is shorter than l, it's too small
        if slen < l_len:
            return False
        # If s is longer than r, it's too big
        if slen > r_len:
            return False
        # If same length, compare lexicographically
        if slen == l_len:
            return s >= l_str and s <= r_str
        # If slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, it's automatically > l and <= r
        return True
    
    # Actually, this is still wrong in the middle case. Let me think again.
    # For example: l_str = "10", r_str = "20", s = "100" - slen = 3, l_len = 2, r_len = 2
    # slen > r_len, so it should return false.
    # So the condition slen <= r_len is correct.
    
    # But if slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, then s is definitely greater than l (because longer digits),
    # and if slen <= r_len, s might be less than r_str or greater than r_str.
    
    # Example: l_str = "1", r_str = "9", s = "10" - slen = 2 > 1 = l_len, slen = 2 > 1 = r_len, so it should be false.
    # But "10" > "9", so it should be false.
    
    # So if slen > r_len, then s > r_str, so it's too big.
    # If slen < l_len, then s < l_str, so it's too small.
    # If slen == l_len, compare lexicographically.
    # If slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, then s > l_str and s could be > r_str.
    
    # But if slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, and r_str has the same length, then we need to compare.
    # If slen > r_len, then s > r_str
    
    # Actually: if slen > r_len, then s > r_str -> too big
    # if slen < l_len, then s < l_str -> too small
    # if slen == l_len and slen == r_len, compare lexicographically
    # if slen > l_len and slen <= r_len:
    #   - if slen > r_len: already handled
    #   - if slen == r_len: compare lexicographically
    #   - if slen < r_len: then s is longer than l_str but shorter than r_str, so s > l_str and s < r_str (since have fewer digits than r_str)
    
    def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        slen = len(s)
        l_len, r_len = len(l_str), len(r_str)
        
        # If s has fewer digits than l, it's smaller
        if slen < l_len:
            return False
        # If s has more digits than r, it's larger
        if slen > r_len:
            return False
        # If same number of digits
        if slen == l_len:
            return s >= l_str and s <= r_str
        # If s has more digits than l and same or fewer than r
        # When we have more digits than l, it's automatically larger than l
        # When we have more digits than r, it's larger than r
        # So if slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, it's in range
        return True
    
    # But this is wrong: s = "10", l_str = "9", r_str = "99"
    # l_len = 1, r_len = 2, slen = 2 > 1, slen <= 2, so return True
    # "10" > "9", so it's > l_str, but is it <= r_str? "10" <= "99" is true, so it's in range.
    # This seems correct.
    
    # Another case: s = "100", l_str = "9", r_str = "99"
    # slen = 3 > 1 = l_len, slen = 3 > 2 = r_len, so return False - correct, since 100 > 99.
    
    # Another case: s = "10", l_str = "10", r_str = "15"
    # slen = 2 = l_len, so check if "10" >= "10" and "10" <= "15" - true.
    
    # Another case: s = "12", l_str = "10", r_str = "11"
    # slen = 2 = l_len, "12" >= "10" true, "12" <= "11" false -> false.
    
    # So the logic seems correct.
    
    # Now implement the DP:
    n = len(a)
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i):
            substring = a[j:i]
            if is_in_range(substring, l_str, r_str):
                dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
    
    print(dp[n] % MOD)

solve()
```

Wait, I need to handle the case where l or r is "0". Let me reconsider the leading zeros part.

If l_str is "0", then we need to check if the substring can be "0".

For a substring to be "0", it must be exactly "0".

For any other substring, it can't have leading zeros.

So when checking is_in_range, I need to handle the "0" case.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    # Special case: if l_str is "0"
    # For substring "0", it's valid
    # For any other substring, can't have leading zeros
    # But "0" is only valid if it's exactly "0"
    
    # DP: dp[i] = number of beautiful partitions for first i digits
    n = len(a)
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # empty partition
    
    def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros: if length > 1 and first char is '0', invalid
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        # If s is "0", check if 0 is in [l, r]
        if s == "0":
            # Check if 0 >= l and 0 <= r
            # This happens when l <= 0 <= r
            # But if l_str is "0", then 0 >= 0 is true
            # If l_str is "1", then 0 >= 1 is false
            # If r_str is "0", then 0 <= 0 is true
            # So we need to compare with l_str and r_str
            # Actually, if s == "0", then value is 0
            # 0 >= l_val, 0 <= r_val
            # But we don't have l_val, we have l_str and r_str
            # So we need to check if 0 >= l and 0 <= r
            # 0 >= l iff l <= 0
            # 0 <= r iff r >= 0
            if l_str == "0":
                # l >= 0, so 0 >= l is true only if l <= 0
                # But l_str is "0", so l == 0
                # So 0 >= 0 is true
                pass
            # We can do this by string comparison:
            # 0 >= l_str iff l_str <= "0"
            # 0 <= r_str iff r_str >= "0"
            # But we need to be careful with strings
            
            # Compare "0" with l_str: "0" >= l_str iff l_str <= "0"
            # "0" >= l_str means 0 >= value of l_str
            # This is true if l_str is "0" or l_str has more digits or l_str < "0"
            # But since l_str >= "0" by the problem, and we're comparing strings
            # If l_str has more digits than "0", then l_str > "0"
            # If l_str has fewer digits than "0", then l_str < "0"
            # If same digits, compare lex
            
            # Actually, 0 >= l_str means:
            # - if l_str has fewer digits than "0" (1 digit), then l_str < "0" -> true
            # - if l_str has more digits than "0", then l_str > "0" -> false
            # - if same digits, then l_str <= "0"
            
            # But l_str has at least one digit, and "0" has one digit
            # So if len(l_str) < 1: impossible
            # if len(l_str) > 1: then l_str has more digits -> value > 0 -> 0 >= l_str is false
            # if len(l_str) == 1: then compare l_str and "0"
            
            # Actually, 0 >= l_str is true only if:
            # - len(l_str) < 1 (impossible) 
            # - len(l_str) > 1 (l_str has more digits, so > 0) -> false
            # - len(l_str) == 1 and l_str <= "0"
            
            # But "0" <= "0" is true, and if l_str is "1", "1" > "0", so false
            # So 0 >= l_str iff (len(l_str) == 1 and l_str <= "0") or (len(l_str) < 1) 
            # Since len(l_str) >= 1, this means len(l_str) == 1 and l_str <= "0"
            
            # Actually, if l_str == "0", then true
            # if l_str > "0", then false
            # So 0 >= l_str iff l_str <= "0"
            
            # Similarly, 0 <= r_str iff r_str >= "0"
            # 0 <= r_str iff r_str >= "0" (i.e., r_str has fewer digits or (same digits and r_str >= "0"))
            
            # But r_str >= "0" is always true if r_str has same length or less? 
            # No: r_str = "1" has one digit, 1 > 0, so 0 <= r_str is true
            # r_str = "0" -> true
            # r_str with more digits -> value > 0, so true
            # r_str with fewer digits -> value < 0? impossible since no negative numbers
            # So actually, any non-empty r_str has value >= 0, since no leading zeros
            
            # So 0 <= r_str is always true for non-empty r_str
            
            # Therefore, 0 is in range iff 0 >= l_str (i.e., l_str <= "0")
            return (l_str <= "0" and r_str >= "0")
            
        # For non-zero substrings
        slen = len(s)
        l_len, r_len = len(l_str), len(r_str)
        
        # If s has fewer digits than l, it's smaller than l
        if slen < l_len:
            return False
        # If s has more digits than r, it's larger than r
        if slen > r_len:
            return False
        # If same number of digits
        if slen == l_len:
            return s >= l_str and s <= r_str
        # If longer than l and shorter or equal to r
        # Since slen > l_len and slen <= r_len, and values are larger than those with fewer digits
        # and since we know slen <= r_len, it's automatically less than or equal to r_str if slen < r_len,
        # and if slen == r_len we need to compare
        # Actually, if slen > l_len and slen <= r_len:
        #   - if slen > r_len: already handled
        #   - if slen == r_len: compare lexicographically
        #   - if slen < r_len: then s has more digits than l_str but fewer digits than r_str, so s > l_str and s < r_str
        return True
    
    # But the above for s=="0" is complicated. Let me simplify.
    # Actually, if s is "0", then we need to check if l <= 0 <= r
    # This is true if l <= 0 and 0 <= r
    
    # For non-zero substrings: we can use the length-based comparison
    
    # Let me define a simpler function:
    
    def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check leading zeros
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        # Handle the case where s is "0"
        if s == "0":
            # Check if 0 is in [l, r]
            # 0 >= l_value and 0 <= r_value
            # l_value >= 0 only if l_str >= "0" in value, which is always when len(l_str) > 1 or (len(l_str)==1 and l_str > "0")
            # But 0 >= l_value means l_value <= 0
            # Since all numbers are non-negative, l_value <= 0 only if l_value == 0
            # Similarly, r_value >= 0 always
            # So 0 >= l_value iff l_value == 0
            # So we need l_str == "0"
            return l_str == "0"
        
        # For non-zero substrings
        slen = len(s)
        l_len, r_len = len(l_str), len(r_str)
        
        # If s has fewer digits than l, it's smaller
        if slen < l_len:
            return False
        # If s has more digits than r, it's larger
        if slen > r_len:
            return False
        # Same digits, compare lexicographically
        if slen == l_len:
            return s >= l_str and s <= r_str
        # More digits than l, fewer or equal to r
        # This means s > l_str (since more digits) and s <= r_str (since if slen > r_len it would have been caught, and if slen == r_len we handle)
        # But we need to check if s <= r_str
        if slen == r_len:
            return s <= r_str
        # slen < r_len - then s < r_str (since fewer digits)
        return True
    
    # But this has a bug: if slen > r_len, we return false
    # if slen > l_len and slen < r_len, we return true
    # if slen > l_len and slen == r_len, we return s <= r_str
    
    # But what if s = "10", l_str = "1", r_str = "9"?
    # slen = 2, l_len = 1, r_len = 1
    # slen > l_len is true, slen > r_len is true -> return false
    # But "10" > "9", so should be false - correct.
    
    # What if s = "10", l_str = "1", r_str = "99"?
    # slen = 2, l_len = 1, r_len = 2
    # slen > l_len -> true, slen > r_len -> false, slen == r_len -> true, so check s <= r_str: "10" <= "99" -> true
    # Correct.
    
    # What if s = "10", l_str = "1", r_str = "19"?
    # "10" <= "19" -> true, correct.
    
    # What if s = "10", l_str = "1", r_str = "9"?
    # We already handled - slen > r_len, so false.
    
    # What if s = "10", l_str = "1", r_str = "0"? Impossible since l <= r.
    
    # But we have l_str <= r_str, so this won't happen.
    
    # However, what if l_str = "10", r_str = "9"? This can't happen by input guarantee.
    
    # So the function seems to work.
    
    # Actually, in the case s = "10", l_str = "1", r_str = "99", it works.
    # But what if s = "100", l_str = "1", r_str = "99"?
    # slen = 3, l_len = 1, r_len = 2
    # slen > r_len -> true, so return false - correct.
    
    # One more: s = "0", l_str = "0", r_str = "5"
    # returns l_str == "0" -> true
    # Correct.
    
    # s = "0", l_str = "1", r_str = "5"
    # returns l_str == "0" -> false
    # Correct.
    
    # So let's implement this.
    
    n = len(a)
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i):
            substring = a[j:i]
            if is_in_range(substring, l_str, r_str):
                dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
    
    print(dp[n] % MOD)

```

But I think there's a mistake in the "0" handling. Let me think again.

For s = "0", we need to check if 0 is in [l, r] as integers.

0 >= l iff l <= 0
0 <= r iff r >= 0

Since the input guarantees l <= r and no leading zeros, and numbers are non-negative, r >= 0 always.

So 0 <= r is always true.

For 0 >= l: when is l <= 0?
Since l is a non-negative number (given 0 <= l <= r), l <= 0 means l = 0.

So 0 >= l iff l = 0.

Therefore, s = "0" is valid only if l = 0.

So the function should be:

```python
def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
    # Check leading zeros
    if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
        return False
        
    # Handle the case where s is "0"
    if s == "0":
        # 0 is valid only if l <= 0, which means l == 0 (since l >= 0)
        return l_str == "0"
    
    # For non-zero substrings
    slen = len(s)
    l_len, r_len = len(l_str), len(r_str)
    
    # If s has fewer digits than l, it's smaller
    if slen < l_len:
        return False
    # If s has more digits than r, it's larger
    if slen > r_len:
        return False
    # Same digits, compare lexicographically
    if slen == l_len:
        return s >= l_str and s <= r_str
    # More digits than l, fewer or equal to r
    # Since more digits than l, s > l (numerically)
    # Since we don't have more digits than r (slen <= r_len), s <= r (numerically)
    # So it's in range
    return True
```

This seems correct.

Let me test with the examples:

Example 1: a = "135", l = "1", r = "15"
Partitions:
- "135": slen = 3, l_len = 1, r_len = 2 -> slen > r_len -> false
- "13"+"5": "13" has len 2, l_len = 1, r_len = 2 -> same length? 2 == 2, so check "13" >= "1" and "13" <= "15" -> 13 >= 1 and 13 <= 15 -> true. "5" has len 1, l_len = 1, r_len = 2 -> same length: "5" >= "1" and "5" <= "15" -> true. So this partition is valid.
- "1"+"35": "1" has len 1, l_len = 1, r_len = 2 -> "1" >= "1" and "1" <= "15" -> true. "35" has len 2, l_len = 1, r_len = 2 -> "35" >= "1" and "35" <= "15"? 35 <= 15 is false -> false.
- "1"+"3"+"5": "1" valid, "3" valid, "5" valid -> valid.
- So valid partitions: "13+5", "1+3+5" -> count = 2.

In our DP:
- dp[0] = 1
- i=1: substring "1" -> valid (non-zero), len=1, l_len=1, r_len=2 -> same length: "1" >= "1" and "1" <= "15" -> true. dp[1] = dp[0] = 1
- i=2: substring "13" -> valid, len=2, l_len=1, r_len=2 -> same length: "13" >= "1" and "13" <= "15" -> true. So dp[2] += dp[0] = 1
           substring "3" -> valid, len=1, l_len=1, r_len=2 -> "3" >= "1" and "3" <= "15" -> true. dp[2] += dp[1] = 1. So dp[2] = 2
- i=3: substring "135" -> len=3 > 2 -> false
           substring "13"+"5": "13" at j=0, "5" at j=1 -> j=0: substring "13" valid, j=1: substring "5" valid. So dp[3] += dp[0] = 1 (from j=0)
           substring "1"+"35": "1" at j=0, "35" at j=1 -> "35" has len=2, "35" <= "15"? false, so not valid. dp[3] += 0
           substring "1"+"3"+"5": j=0: "1" valid, j=1: "3" valid, j=2: "5" valid. So dp[3] += dp[1] = 1
           So total dp[3] = 1 + 0 + 1 = 2

Correct.

Example 2: a = "10000", l = "0", r = "9"
- "1" -> valid (len=1, l_len=1, r_len=1: "1" >= "0" and "1" <= "9" -> true)
- "0" -> valid only if l_str == "0" -> true
- "10" -> len=2 > r_len=1 -> false
- "100" -> false
- "1000" -> false
- "10000" -> false

Partitions:
- "1+0+0+0+0": each part valid
- Any other? "10+0+0" -> "10" invalid (len 2 > 1)
- "1+0+00" -> "00" has leading zeros -> invalid
- So only one valid partition.

In DP:
- dp[0] = 1
- i=1: substring "1" -> valid, dp[1] = 1
- i=2: 
  - "10": len=2 > 1 -> false
  - "0": s="0", l_str="0", so valid -> dp[2] += dp[1] = 1
- i=3:
  - "100": len=3 > 1 -> false
  - "10": len=2 > 1 -> false
  - "00": has leading zeros -> false
  - "0": at j=2: substring "0" -> valid, dp[3] += dp[2] = 1
- i=4: similarly, only the "0" at end works, dp[4] += dp[3] = 1
- i=5: only "0" at end, dp[5] += dp[4] = 1

So dp[5] = 1. Correct.

Therefore, the solution is correct.

```python
MOD = 998244353

a = input().strip()
l_str = input().strip()
r_str = input().strip()

n = len(a)
dp = [0] * (n + 1)
dp[0] = 1

def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
    # Check leading zeros
    if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
        return False
        
    # Handle the case where s is "0"
    if s == "0":
        # 0 is valid only if l <= 0, which means l == 0 (since l >= 0)
        return l_str == "0"
    
    # For non-zero substrings
    slen = len(s)
    l_len, r_len = len(l_str), len(r_str)
    
    # If s has fewer digits than l, it's smaller
    if slen < l_len:
        return False
    # If s has more digits than r, it's larger
    if slen > r_len:
        return False
    # Same digits, compare lexicographically
    if slen == l_len:
        return s >= l_str and s <= r_str
    # More digits than l, fewer or equal to r
    # Since more digits than l, s > l (numerically)
    # Since we don't have more digits than r (slen <= r_len), s <= r (numerically)
    # So it's in range
    return True

for i in range(1, n + 1):
    for j in range(i):
        substring = a[j:i]
        if is_in_range(substring, l_str, r_str):
            dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD

print(dp[n] % MOD)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya owns three big integers — $a, l, r$. Let's define a partition of $x$ such a sequence of strings $s_1, s_2, \dots, s_k$ that $s_1 + s_2 + \dots + s_k = x$, where $+$ is a concatanation of strings. $s_i$ is the $i$-th element of the partition. For example, number $12345$ has the following partitions: ["1", "2", "3", "4", "5"], ["123", "4", "5"], ["1", "2345"], ["12345"] and lots of others.

Let's call some partition of $a$ beautiful if each of its elements contains no leading zeros.

Vasya want to know the number of beautiful partitions of number $a$, which has each of $s_i$ satisfy the condition $l \le s_i \le r$. Note that the comparison is the integer comparison, not the string one.

Help Vasya to count the amount of partitions of number $a$ such that they match all the given requirements. The result can be rather big, so print it modulo $998244353$.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $a~(1 \le a \le 10^{1000000})$.

The second line contains a single integer $l~(0 \le l \le 10^{1000000})$.

The third line contains a single integer $r~(0 \le r \le 10^{1000000})$.

It is guaranteed that $l \le r$.

It is also guaranteed that numbers $a, l, r$ contain no leading zeros.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the amount of partitions of number $a$ such that they match all the given requirements modulo $998244353$.


-----Examples-----
Input
135
1
15

Output
2

Input
10000
0
9

Output
1



-----Note-----

In the first test case, there are two good partitions $13+5$ and $1+3+5$.

In the second test case, there is one good partition $1+0+0+0+0$.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a big integer `a` and bounds `l` and `r`
2. We need to count the number of partitions of `a` (splitting its digits) such that:
   - Each partition element has no leading zeros
   - Each element is between `l` and `r` (integer comparison)
3. The result should be modulo 998244353

## Key Observations

1. Since `a` can be very large (up to 10^1000000), we need to work with strings and digit-by-digit processing
2. For each position in `a`, we need to consider all possible splits
3. Each split creates a substring that must be within range `[l, r]`
4. We need to avoid leading zeros - if a substring starts with '0' and has length > 1, it's invalid

## Algorithm Approach

This is a dynamic programming problem on strings. For each position in `a`, we'll track the number of valid ways to partition the prefix.

Let's define `dp[i]` = number of beautiful partitions for the first `i` digits of `a`.

For each position `i`, we consider all possible last segments ending at position `i` (from position `j` to `i` where `j < i`), and check if:
1. The substring `a[j:i]` has no leading zeros (if length > 1 and starts with '0', invalid)
2. The integer value of `a[j:i]` is within `[l, r]`

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Convert the input numbers to strings
2. Use dynamic programming with memoization
3. For each position, try all possible previous starting positions
4. Check validity of each segment (no leading zeros, within bounds)
5. Accumulate the count

## Special Cases

- If `l == 0`, then segments starting with '0' are allowed (but only as single digit "0")
- If a segment has length > 1 and starts with '0', it's invalid due to leading zero rule
- We need to compare numbers with potentially different lengths

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    # Handle edge case where l = 0 and we need to check for "0"
    # But note: leading zeros are not allowed for multi-digit numbers
    
    n = len(a)
    
    # Convert l and r to integers for comparison when possible
    # But since they can be very large, we need string comparison logic
    
    def str_to_int(s):
        if s == "":
            return 0
        return int(s) if len(s) <= 100 else None  # For very large numbers, we use string comparison logic
    
    # For comparing two strings representing numbers, we can compare lexicographically
    # But with different lengths, we need to be careful
    
    def compare_str(s1, s2):
        # Compare two string numbers
        if len(s1) < len(s2):
            return -1
        elif len(s1) > len(s2):
            return 1
        else:
            return -1 if s1 < s2 else (1 if s1 > s2 else 0)
    
    def is_in_range(s, l_str, r_str):
        # Check if string s is in range [l, r]
        if s == "":
            return False
            
        # Check for leading zero - only single digit "0" is valid
        if len(s) > 1 and s[0] == '0':
            return False
            
        # Compare with l and r
        comp_l = compare_str(s, l_str)
        comp_r = compare_str(s, r_str)
        
        return comp_l >= 0 and comp_r <= 0
    
    # dp[i] = number of beautiful partitions for first i digits
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # empty prefix has one way (base case)
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible starting positions for the last segment
        for j in range(i):
            # Last segment is from position j to i-1 (0-indexed)
            segment = a[j:i]
            
            # Check if this segment has leading zeros
            if len(segment) > 1 and segment[0] == '0':
                continue
                
            # Check if segment value is in [l, r]
            if not is_in_range(segment, l_str, r_str):
                continue
                
            dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
    
    return dp[n]

print(solve())
```

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    a = input().strip()
    l_str = input().strip()
    r_str = input().strip()
    
    n = len(a)
    
    # Function to compare two string numbers (lexicographically for same length, longer number is larger)
    def compare_strings(s1, s2):
        if len(s1) < len(s2):
            return -1
        elif len(s1) > len(s2):
            return 1
        else:
            return -1 if s1 < s2 else (1 if s1 > s2 else 0)
    
    # dp[i] = number of beautiful partitions for first i digits
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1  # base case: empty prefix
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible starting positions for the last segment
        for j in range(i):
            segment = a[j:i]
            
            # Check leading zeros - no leading zeros allowed except for single "0"
            if len(segment) > 1 and segment[0] == '0':
                continue
                
            # Check if segment value is in [l, r]
            # Convert to string comparison
            comp_l = compare_strings(segment, l_str)
            comp_r = compare_strings(segment, r_str)
            
            # segment should be >= l and <= r
            if comp_l >= 0 and comp_r <= 0:
                dp[i] = (dp[i] + dp[j]) % MOD
    
    return dp[n]

print(solve())
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

There are N islands lining up from west to east, connected by N-1 bridges.
The i-th bridge connects the i-th island from the west and the (i+1)-th island from the west.
One day, disputes took place between some islands, and there were M requests from the inhabitants of the islands:
Request i: A dispute took place between the a_i-th island from the west and the b_i-th island from the west. Please make traveling between these islands with bridges impossible.
You decided to remove some bridges to meet all these M requests.
Find the minimum number of bridges that must be removed.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 2 \leq N \leq 10^5
 - 1 \leq M \leq 10^5
 - 1 \leq a_i < b_i \leq N
 - All pairs (a_i, b_i) are distinct.

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
a_1 b_1
a_2 b_2
:
a_M b_M

-----Output-----
Print the minimum number of bridges that must be removed.

-----Sample Input-----
5 2
1 4
2 5

-----Sample Output-----
1

The requests can be met by removing the bridge connecting the second and third islands from the west.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs).
