I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have N islands in a line, connected by N-1 bridges (each bridge connects consecutive islands). We have M requests, each asking to make travel between two islands impossible. We need to find the minimum number of bridges to remove to satisfy all requests.

## Key Insight

This is essentially a problem about finding the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect certain pairs of nodes in a path graph.

The key insight is that we want to find the minimum number of bridges to remove such that for each request (a_i, b_i), there's no path between island a_i and island b_i.

Since the islands are in a line, we can think of this as finding "gaps" that need to be created. If we remove a bridge between island i and i+1, then islands 1 to i are disconnected from islands i+1 to N.

## Approach

The optimal strategy is to consider the requests and find the minimum number of bridge removals that can satisfy all constraints.

For each request (a_i, b_i), we need to ensure that islands a_i and b_i are in different connected components. This means we need to remove at least one bridge somewhere between them.

The key observation: we can satisfy multiple requests with a single bridge removal if the requests overlap in their required "cut points".

Let me think about this differently:

1. If we remove a bridge between island i and i+1, then all islands ≤ i are disconnected from all islands > i.
2. For a request (a, b), we need to remove a bridge somewhere between a and b (inclusive of a, exclusive of b).
3. We want to minimize the total number of bridge removals.

This becomes a problem of finding the minimum number of "cut points" such that every requested interval (a_i, b_i) contains at least one cut point.

In other words, we need to cover all the intervals with minimum number of points (where each point corresponds to removing a bridge).

This is the classic "minimum points to cover intervals" problem, which can be solved greedily:

- Sort the intervals by their right endpoint
- For each interval, if it doesn't contain any existing cut point, add a cut point at its right endpoint

## Algorithm Steps

1. Read the input
2. Store all requests as intervals (a_i, b_i)
3. Sort the intervals by b_i (right endpoint)
4. Initialize a variable to track number of cuts
5. For each interval in sorted order:
   - If the current interval doesn't contain any existing cut point, add a cut at position b_i - 1 (which means removing bridge between b_i-1 and b_i)
6. Return the number of cuts

Wait, let me reconsider. If we have a request (a, b), we need to ensure no path exists between a and b. This means we need to remove a bridge between some position i and i+1 where a ≤ i < b.

So for request (a, b), we need a cut at position i where a ≤ i < b.

The greedy algorithm for covering intervals: sort by right endpoint, and place cuts at the right endpoint of each uncovered interval.

For each request (a, b), we need a cut at some position i where a ≤ i < b.

So we need to cover all intervals (a_i, b_i) with points in [a_i, b_i-1].

The minimum number of points to cover all intervals is found by sorting intervals by b_i and placing a point at b_i for each interval that doesn't contain a previously placed point.

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their ending position b
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

cuts = 0
last_cut_position = 0  # last position where we made a cut (cut at position last_cut_position means removing bridge between last_cut_position and last_cut_position+1)

for a, b in requests:
    # Check if this request is already covered by a previous cut
    # A previous cut at position p covers request (a, b) if a <= p < b
    # Since we have p >= last_cut_position and p < b, we just need to check if last_cut_position >= a
    
    # If last_cut_position >= a, then the cut at last_cut_position is in [a, b), so it covers (a, b)
    if last_cut_position >= a:
        # This request is already covered
        continue
    else:
        # We need to add a new cut. Place it at position b-1 (remove bridge between b-1 and b)
        cuts += 1
        last_cut_position = b - 1

print(cuts)
```

Let me trace through the sample:
- N=5, M=2
- Requests: (1,4), (2,5)

Sorted by b: (1,4), (2,5)

1. Process (1,4): last_cut_position = 0, 0 >= 1? No, so we add a cut at position 4-1 = 3
   - cuts = 1, last_cut_position = 3
2. Process (2,5): last_cut_position = 3, 3 >= 2? Yes, so this request is covered.

Answer: 1 ✓

This matches the expected output.

```python
        # The cut at last_cut_position is already in the range [a, b)
        continue
    else:
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have N islands in a line connected by N-1 bridges (each bridge connects consecutive islands). We have M requests, each asking to make travel between two specific islands impossible. We need to find the minimum number of bridges to remove to satisfy all requests.

## Key Insight

This is essentially a problem of finding the minimum number of edges to remove to break all requested connections. 

For each request (a_i, b_i), we want to make it impossible to travel from island a_i to island b_i. This means we need to break the path between them.

If we think about it, to block travel between a_i and b_i, we need to remove at least one bridge in the range [a_i, b_i-1] (since bridges connect consecutive islands).

The key insight is that we want to find the minimum number of bridges to remove such that for every request (a_i, b_i), at least one bridge in the range [a_i, b_i-1] is removed.

This becomes a "minimum hitting set" problem where each request defines a range, and we want to find the minimum number of points that intersect all ranges.

## Strategy

This is actually a classic interval covering problem. We can use a greedy approach:

1. Sort the requests by their right endpoint (b_i)
2. For each request, if the current bridge we're considering doesn't cover the request, we need to add a bridge at the right end of the request
3. This works because by processing requests in order of their right endpoint, we ensure we don't over-remove bridges

Actually, let me reconsider. This is equivalent to finding the minimum number of bridges such that every request (a_i, b_i) has at least one bridge in its range [a_i, b_i-1] removed.

The optimal strategy is to use a greedy approach where we process intervals by their right end, and whenever we find an interval that doesn't contain any previously chosen bridge, we choose the bridge at its right end.

## Algorithm

1. Store all requests as intervals (a_i, b_i)
2. Sort intervals by their right endpoint b_i
3. Initialize removed_bridges = 0
4. For each interval in sorted order:
   - If this interval doesn't contain any previously removed bridge, remove the bridge at position (b_i - 1)
   - Increment removed_bridges
5. Return removed_bridges

Wait, let me verify with the example:
- N=5, M=2
- Requests: (1,4) → range [1,3], (2,5) → range [2,4]
- Sort by right endpoint: (1,4), (2,5)
- For (1,4): no previous bridges, remove bridge at position 3 (since b_i-1 = 3)
- For (2,5): check if it contains bridge 3. Since 2 ≤ 3 ≤ 4, it does contain bridge 3.
- So we only need to remove 1 bridge.

This matches the expected output.

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their right endpoint (b)
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

removed_bridges = 0
# Keep track of the last removed bridge position
last_removed = -1

for a, b in requests:
    # The bridge we need to remove is at position (b-1)
    # We need to remove it if no bridge in [a, b-1] has been removed
    bridge_to_remove = b - 1
    
    # If we haven't removed any bridge that covers this request
    # Check if any previously removed bridge is in [a, b-1]
    if bridge_to_remove < a or bridge_to_remove < last_removed:
        # This check is wrong - let me fix it
        pass

# Let me restart with correct logic
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

removed_bridges = 0
last_removed_position = -1

for a, b in requests:
    # We need to make sure that bridge at position (b-1) is removed
    # or some bridge in the range [a, b-1]
    # But we want to minimize total removals
    
    # If the rightmost removed bridge is not in the range [a, b-1], we need to remove a new bridge
    if last_removed_position < a:
        # We need to remove a bridge in [a, b-1]
        # Since we want to minimize, we remove the rightmost bridge in this range
        # which is at position (b-1)
        last_removed_position = b - 1
        removed_bridges += 1

print(removed_bridges)
```

Wait, let me think again. The condition should be: if the last removed bridge is not in the range [a, b-1], then we need to remove a new bridge at position (b-1).

The last removed bridge at position `last_removed_position` will block all requests that have a ≤ last_removed_position < b.

So for a new request (a, b), if a ≤ last_removed_position < b, then the request is already blocked.

If a > last_removed_position, then we need to remove a new bridge.

For the example:
- requests: (1,4), (2,5)
- Sort by b: (1,4), (2,5)
- For (1,4): last_removed_position = -1 < 1, so remove bridge at position 3, last_removed_position = 3, removed_bridges = 1

Actually: we need to check if last_removed_position >= a AND last_removed_position < b?
No, we have a request (a,b) that needs a bridge in [a, b-1]. The last_removed_position is at b-1 from previous request.

For the second request (2,5): we need a bridge in [2,4]. Since last_removed_position = 3, and 2 ≤ 3 ≤ 4, this bridge is in the range, so the request is satisfied.

So the condition should be: if last_removed_position < a, then we need to remove a bridge.

```python

    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort by right endpoint (b)
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

removed_bridges = 0
last_removed_position = -1  # the position of the rightmost bridge we've removed

for a, b in requests:
    # We need a bridge in the range [a, b-1]
    # If the last removed bridge is at position p, then if p >= a and p >= a, and p < b, it's in the range
    # But since we're removing at position b-1, we want to check if b-1 >= a and b-1 < b
    # Actually, we want to check if the last removed bridge is in [a, b-1]
    if last_removed_position < a:
        # This request requires a bridge in [a, b-1]
        # We remove the bridge at position (b-1) to block this request
        last_removed_position = b - 1
        removed_bridges += 1

print(removed_bridges)
```

This should work. Let me verify:
- For (1,4): a=1, b=4, last_removed_position=-1 < 1 → remove bridge at 3, removed_bridges=1, last_removed_position=3
- For (2,5): a=2, b=5, last_removed_position=3 ≥ 2 → condition fails, so no removal
- Answer: 1 ✓

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their right endpoint (b)
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

removed_bridges = 0
last_removed_position = -1  # position of the last bridge we removed (0-indexed)

for a, b in requests:
    # If the last removed bridge is not in the range [a, b-1], we need to add one
    # The last removed bridge is at position 'last_removed_position'
    # It will be in range [a, b-1] if a <= last_removed_position < b
    # But if last_removed_position < a, then it's not in the range
    if last_removed_position < a:
        # We need to remove a bridge in [a, b-1]
        # Remove the rightmost bridge in this range: at position (b-1)
        last_removed_position = b - 1
        removed_bridges += 1

print(removed_bridges)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have N islands in a line connected by N-1 bridges (each bridge connects consecutive islands). We have M requests, each asking to make travel between two islands impossible. We need to find the minimum number of bridges to remove to satisfy all requests.

## Key Insight

This is essentially a problem about finding the minimum number of bridges to remove so that certain pairs of islands become disconnected.

The key insight is that if we want to disconnect islands a and b, we need to remove at least one bridge between them. Specifically, we need to remove a bridge between some position i and i+1 where a ≤ i < b.

So for each request (a, b), we need to ensure there's no path from island a to island b. This means we must remove at least one bridge in the range [a, b-1].

## Strategy

This becomes a problem of finding the minimum number of "cut points" (bridge removals) such that for each request (a, b), at least one bridge in the range [a, b-1] is removed.

This is equivalent to finding the minimum number of intervals to cover all the required ranges. We can solve this using a greedy approach:

1. For each request (a, b), we need to "cover" the interval [a, b-1]
2. We want to minimize the total number of bridge removals
3. We can use a greedy strategy: always choose the rightmost possible cut point that covers the current request

However, there's a simpler insight: this is equivalent to finding the minimum number of bridges such that every requested pair has at least one bridge removed between them.

Actually, let me think of it differently. We can think of this as a covering problem. Each bridge removal at position i (between island i and i+1) can "cover" all requests where a ≤ i < b.

So we want to select bridge positions such that every request [a, b] has at least one selected bridge in [a, b-1].

The optimal strategy is to process requests from left to right and use a greedy algorithm:

- Sort requests by their right endpoint
- For each request, if the current position doesn't cover it, place a cut at the rightmost possible position (which is b-1)

Wait, let me reconsider. Looking at the sample:
- N=5, M=2
- Requests: (1,4) and (2,5)
- If we remove bridge between 2 and 3 (position 2), then:
  - Between 1 and 4: path 1-2-3-4 is broken at 2-3, so impossible
  - Between 2 and 5: path 2-3-4-5 is broken at 2-3, so impossible
- So only 1 bridge needed.

This suggests we can use a greedy algorithm that processes requests by their right endpoint and places cuts optimally.

## Greedy Algorithm

1. Sort all requests by their right endpoint (b)
2. Initialize a variable to track the current position where we've placed cuts
3. For each request (a, b) in sorted order:
   - If we haven't already blocked the path from a to b, we need to place a cut at position b-1
   - This cut will block all requests that have a ≤ b-1 < b

Actually, since we want to minimize the number of cuts, we should place a cut at position b-1 for each request that isn't already blocked.

But we need to ensure that if a request (a, b) isn't blocked by any previous cut, we place a cut at position b-1.

The key insight: place a cut at position b-1 whenever a request (a, b) exists such that a > current_position.

Wait, let me trace through:

- Process requests sorted by b
- For each request (a, b), if a > current_max_cut_position, then we need to place a cut at b-1
- This cut will block all requests with a ≤ b-1 < b

But actually, if we place a cut at position x, it blocks all requests where a ≤ x < b.

So the greedy strategy: sort by b, and for each request, if a > current_max_cut (the rightmost cut position we've placed), place a cut at position b-1.

Let me verify with sample:
- Requests: (1,4), (2,5)
- Sorted by b: (1,4), (2,5)
- Initially current_max_cut = 0
- First request (1,4): a=1, current_max_cut=0, since 1 > 0, place cut at 3 (b-1=3)
- Second request (2,5): a=2, current_max_cut=3, since 2 ≤ 3, already blocked? 

Wait, this doesn't seem right. If we place a cut at position 3 (between island 3 and 4), then:
- Request (1,4): path 1-2-3-4 has cut at 3-4, so blocked ✓
- Request (2,5): path 2-3-4-5 has cut at 3-4, so blocked ✓

So only 1 cut is needed. And we placed it at position 3.

But what if we had (1,3) and (2,4)?
- Sorted: (1,3), (2,4)
- First: a=1 > 0, place cut at 2
- Second: a=2 ≤ 2, already blocked by cut at 2? 

For request (2,4), we need to block path from 2 to 4. If we have cut at 2 (between 2 and 3), then path 2-3-4 is blocked at 2-3, so yes, blocked.

So the algorithm works.

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their right endpoint (b)
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

cuts = 0
current_max_cut = 0  # the rightmost position where we've placed a cut

for a, b in requests:
    # If the left endpoint is greater than current_max_cut, 
    # we need to place a cut at position b-1
    if a > current_max_cut:
        cuts += 1
        current_max_cut = b - 1

print(cuts)
```

Wait, let me verify the logic again. We place a cut at position b-1 when a > current_max_cut.

This means if a request starts at position a and we haven't blocked it yet (since a > current_max_cut), we place a cut at b-1. This cut will block the path from a to b (since a ≤ b-1 < b).

And because we're processing in increasing order of b, when we place a cut at b-1, it will block all future requests with a ≤ b-1 < next_b.

Yes, this seems correct.

Let me verify with another example:
- Requests: (1,3), (3,5)
- Sorted: (1,3), (3,5)
- Initially current_max_cut = 0
- (1,3): a=1 > 0, place cut at 2, cuts=1, current_max_cut=2
- (3,5): a=3 > 2, so place cut at 4, cuts=2, current_max_cut=4

With cuts at 2 and 4:
- (1,3): blocked by cut at 2 (between 2-3)
- (3,5): blocked by cut at 4 (between 4-5)

But is this minimal? Could we do with just one cut?

If we cut at position 3 (between 3-4):
- (1,3): path 1-2-3 is still connected, not blocked
- (3,5): path 3-4-5 is blocked

So we need another cut. Actually, no cut at position 3 will block (1,3) because (1,3) has b=3, so position 3 is not in [1,2].

To block (1,3), we need a cut at position 1, 2, or 2 (since b-1=2).

To block (3,5), we need a cut at position 3, 4 (since b-1=4).

The positions 2 and 4 are needed, so 2 cuts is minimal.

So the algorithm is correct.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have N islands in a line connected by N-1 bridges. We have M requests where each request says "make traveling between islands a_i and b_i impossible". We need to find the minimum number of bridges to remove to satisfy all requests.

## Key Insight

This is essentially a problem about making pairs of islands disconnected. Each request (a_i, b_i) means we want to disconnect island a_i from island b_i.

The key insight is that we want to find the minimum number of bridges to remove such that for each request (a_i, b_i), there's no path between a_i and b_i.

Consider the bridges as edges between consecutive islands. If we remove a bridge between island i and island i+1, then islands i and i+1 become disconnected from each other.

## Strategy

This problem can be transformed into finding the minimum number of "cut points" needed to disconnect all the specified pairs.

A crucial observation: if we think about the constraints, we want to ensure that for each pair (a_i, b_i), there's no path from a_i to b_i. This means that between a_i and b_i, there should be at least one bridge that is removed.

However, a bridge removal can satisfy multiple requests. For example, if we remove bridge between island 2 and 3, it disconnects:
- Any pair where one island is ≤2 and the other ≥3
- Specifically, it disconnects (1,3), (1,4), (1,5), (2,3), (2,4), (2,5) etc.

The optimal strategy is to find a set of bridge removals such that every request is satisfied, and we minimize the total number of removals.

## Algorithm

This problem is equivalent to finding the minimum number of "cut points" such that every interval [a_i, b_i] contains at least one cut point.

In other words, we have intervals [a_i, b_i] and we want to place as few points as possible such that each interval contains at least one point.

This is the classic "minimum number of points to cover all intervals" problem, which can be solved greedily by considering the rightmost endpoints.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, we want to place a cut at some bridge between island i and i+1 such that for every request (a_i, b_i), there's a cut somewhere in the range a_i to b_i-1 (since bridges are between consecutive islands).

So the problem reduces to: Given intervals [a_i, b_i] where a_i < b_i, find the minimum number of points such that each interval contains at least one point. The points correspond to bridges between islands i and i+1.

This is the classic "interval covering" or "minimum points to cover intervals" problem, which can be solved by sorting the intervals by their end points and then greedily placing points at the end of each interval.

Actually, let me think again: We want to place points (cuts) such that every interval [a_i, b_i] contains at least one point. The minimum number of points needed is equal to the maximum number of overlapping intervals that have no common point.

But actually, the standard greedy algorithm for "minimum points to cover all intervals" is:
1. Sort intervals by their end points
2. Place a point at the end of the first interval
3. For each subsequent interval that doesn't contain this point, place a new point at its end

This gives us the minimum number of points needed.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the inputs
2. Each request (a_i, b_i) represents an interval [a_i, b_i]
3. We need to find the minimum number of bridge removals such that each interval contains at least one removed bridge
4. This is equivalent to the minimum number of points to hit all intervals
5. Use greedy algorithm: sort intervals by b_i (end point), then place points greedily

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by the end point b
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

# Greedy algorithm: place points at the end of intervals
# If an interval doesn't contain the previous point, place a new point
points = 0
last_cut = 0  # the last bridge position we cut (bridge between island i and i+1)

for a, b in requests:
    # If the current interval doesn't contain the last cut, we need a new cut
    # A cut at position x means we removed bridge between x and x+1
    # This cut will disconnect any pair where a <= x and x+1 <= b
    # So we need x >= a and x+1 >= b, which means x >= b-1
    
    # If the last cut is at position < a, then we need a new cut
    # The new cut should be at position b-1 (between island b-1 and b)
    if last_cut < a:
        points += 1
        last_cut = b - 1  # cut bridge between island b-1 and b

print(points)
```

Wait, let me verify with the sample:
- N=5, M=2
- Requests: (1,4), (2,5)

After sorting by b: [(1,4), (2,5)]

- Process (1,4): last_cut = 0, since 0 < 1, we add a point, points = 1, last_cut = 4-1 = 3
- Process (2,5): check if last_cut < 2? 3 < 2 is false, so no new point

Answer: 1 ✓

This matches the expected output.

The algorithm works because:
- We sort by end point (b)
- We greedily place cuts at the end of each interval that doesn't already contain our previous cut
- Each cut at position x-1 (between x-1 and x) will disconnect any pair where a_i <= x-1 and b_i >= x
- This ensures that every interval contains at least one cut

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have N islands in a line, connected by N-1 bridges (between consecutive islands). We have M requests, each asking to make travel between two islands impossible. We need to find the minimum number of bridges to remove to satisfy all requests.

## Key Insight

This is actually a problem about finding the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect certain pairs of nodes. Since the islands are in a line (a path graph), we can think about this differently.

For each request (a_i, b_i), we want to disconnect island a_i from island b_i. In a linear arrangement, this means we need to remove a bridge somewhere between a_i and b_i.

The key insight is that if we have multiple requests that overlap in their required removal intervals, we can satisfy multiple requests with just one bridge removal.

## Strategy

1. Each request (a_i, b_i) requires that we remove at least one bridge between positions a_i and b_i-1 (since bridges connect consecutive islands).

2. We can think of each request as defining an interval [a_i, b_i] where we need to have at least one bridge removal.

3. We want to find the minimum number of bridge removals that cover all these intervals.

4. This becomes a classic "interval covering" or "minimum point coverage" problem.

5. The optimal strategy is to find the minimum number of points such that every interval contains at least one of these points.

6. This is solved by sorting the intervals and then greedily placing points at the rightmost position of each interval (or at the end of the range).

## Algorithm

1. For each request (a_i, b_i), we have an interval [a_i, b_i-1] where we need to remove a bridge.
2. Sort these intervals by their start position.
3. Greedily place removal points: start from leftmost interval, place a removal at position b_i-1 (the end of the interval), then skip all intervals that are completely covered by this removal.
4. Continue until all intervals are covered.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, we want to find the minimum number of bridge removals such that for each request (a_i, b_i), there's at least one bridge between position a_i and b_i-1 that is removed.

This is equivalent to finding the minimum number of points such that every interval [a_i, b_i-1] contains at least one of these points.

The greedy approach is:
- Sort intervals by their right endpoint (b_i-1)
- Process intervals from left to right
- For each interval that isn't covered by previously placed points, place a point at its right endpoint (b_i-1)

But actually, since we want to cover intervals, we should place points at the end positions (b_i-1) because placing at the end of an interval covers all intervals that end at or before that position.

## Correct Algorithm

1. Store all requests as intervals [a_i, b_i-1] (since the bridge between island x and x+1 has position x)
2. Sort intervals by their right endpoint (b_i-1)
3. Initialize count = 0
4. Initialize last_removed = -1 (the position of the last bridge we removed)
5. For each interval [left, right] in sorted order:
   - If left > last_removed, we need to remove a bridge at position right (this covers all intervals that end at or after right)
   - Increment count and set last_removed = right

Let me trace through the sample:
- N=5, M=2
- Requests: (1,4) → interval [1,3], (2,5) → interval [2,4]
- Sort by right endpoint: [1,3] then [2,4]
- Start: last_removed = -1
- Process [1,3]: left=1 > -1, so remove bridge at position 3, count=1, last_removed=3
- Process [2,4]: left=2 ≤ 3 (last_removed), so skip
- Answer: 1 ✓

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    # Convert to interval [a, b-1] - we need to remove a bridge between a and b
    requests.append((a, b - 1))

# Sort requests by their right endpoint
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

count = 0
last_removed = -1  # position of the last bridge we removed

for left, right in requests:
    # If the current interval starts after the last removed bridge,
    # we need to remove a new bridge
    if left > last_removed:
        count += 1
        last_removed = right

print(count)
```

    requests.append((a, b - 1))

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have N islands in a line, connected by N-1 bridges. We have M requests where each request wants to make travel between two islands (a_i, b_i) impossible. We need to find the minimum number of bridges to remove to satisfy all requests.

## Key Insight

This is essentially a problem of finding the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect certain pairs of vertices in a path graph.

The key insight is that we want to find the maximum number of requests that can be satisfied by removing a single bridge. If we remove a bridge at position i (between island i and i+1), then any request that requires disconnecting islands where one is ≤ i and the other is ≥ i+1 will be satisfied.

For a bridge at position i (connecting island i and i+1), it can satisfy all requests where:
- Either a_j ≤ i and b_j ≥ i+1, OR
- a_j ≥ i+1 and b_j ≤ i (but this can't happen since a_j < b_j)

So it can satisfy requests where the interval [a_j, b_j] contains position i.

## Approach

1. For each possible bridge position i (from 1 to N-1), count how many requests have their interval [a_j, b_j] containing position i.
2. The answer is the maximum count over all positions, because if we remove one bridge, it can satisfy all requests that contain that bridge position.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, we want to satisfy ALL requests. We need to find the minimum number of bridges to remove such that for each request (a_i, b_i), the path between a_i and b_i is broken.

A bridge at position k (connecting island k and k+1) breaks the path between a_i and b_i if and only if a_i ≤ k < b_i.

So for each bridge position k, it can satisfy all requests where a_i ≤ k < b_i.

We want to find the minimum number of bridges to remove such that every request is satisfied.

This becomes: find the minimum number of positions to select such that every request [a_i, b_i] contains at least one selected position.

This is equivalent to finding the minimum number of points such that every interval contains at least one point.

This is the "hitting set" problem for intervals, which can be solved optimally using a greedy approach: we want to cover all intervals with minimum points.

The optimal strategy is: sort intervals by their end points, and for each interval that hasn't been covered yet, place a point at its end point.

However, since we want to find the minimum number of bridges to remove, we can think of it as finding the minimum number of positions such that every request interval contains at least one of them.

Actually, let me think differently. Since the bridges are positioned between islands, and removing a bridge at position k breaks all paths that go through that bridge, we need to cover all intervals.

The minimum number of bridges to remove is equal to the minimum number of "cut points" needed such that every interval [a_i, b_i] contains at least one cut point.

To minimize the number of cut points, we should place them optimally. The optimal strategy is:
1. Sort the requests by their right endpoint (b_i)
2. For each request, if its interval is not already covered by a previously placed cut point, place a cut point at its right endpoint (b_i)

This greedy algorithm works because if we place a cut point at the end of an interval, it will cover all intervals that start before or at that point and end at or after this point.

## Algorithm

1. Read all requests as intervals [a_i, b_i]
2. Sort intervals by b_i (the right endpoint)
3. Initialize count = 0, last_cut = 0
4. For each interval [a, b] in sorted order:
   - If a > last_cut, this interval is not covered, so we need to place a cut at position b
   - Increment count, set last_cut = b
5. Return count

Wait, let me verify with the sample:

- Requests: (1,4), (2,5)
- Sort by b: (1,4), (2,5)
- For (1,4): a=1, last_cut=0, since 1>0, we place cut at 4, count=1, last_cut=4
- For (2,5): a=2, since 2 ≤ 4, this interval is already covered by the cut at 4
- Answer = 1 ✓

- Between islands 1 and 4: the path goes through bridge 3 (between 3 and 4), so removing bridge 4 (between 4 and 5) doesn't affect this path

- For [1,4]: 1 ≤ 3 < 4 ✓
- For [2,5]: 2 ≤ 3 < 5 ✓

For interval [a,b], we place a cut at position b-1 (between island b-1 and b).

The greedy algorithm: sort intervals by b, then for each interval that starts after the previous cut position, place a cut at position b-1.

Actually, since we want to cover an interval [a,b], we need a cut at position k where a ≤ k < b. The rightmost such position is k = b-1.

So if we place a cut at position b-1, it will cover all intervals that start ≤ b-1 and end ≥ b.

For the greedy approach:
1. Sort intervals by b (right endpoint)
2. Initialize last_cut = 0 (no cut placed)
3. For each interval [a,b] in sorted order:
   - If a > last_cut, we need a new cut. Place it at position b-1
   - Update last_cut = b-1
4. Return number of cuts

- Intervals: (1,4), (2,5)
- Sort by b: (1,4), (2,5)
- For (1,4): a=1, last_cut=0, since 1 > 0, place cut at 4-1 = 3, count=1, last_cut=3
- For (2,5): a=2, since 2 ≤ 3, already covered
- Answer = 1 ✓

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their right endpoint (b)
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

cut_count = 0
last_cut_position = 0  # position of last bridge we removed (0 means no cut)

for a, b in requests:
    # If the start of current interval is greater than the last cut position,
    # we need to place a new cut
    if a > last_cut_position:
        # Place cut at position b-1 (between island b-1 and b)
        cut_count += 1
        last_cut_position = b - 1

print(cut_count)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N islands in a line, connected by N-1 bridges (between consecutive islands)
2. We have M requests, each asking to make travel between islands a_i and b_i impossible
3. We need to find the minimum number of bridges to remove to satisfy all requests

## Key Insight

When we have a request (a, b), we want to make it impossible to travel from island a to island b. This means we need to break the path between them.

The key insight is that if we remove a bridge between islands i and i+1, it will break the connection between all pairs that span across that bridge.

We can think of this as a greedy problem where we want to find the minimum number of bridge removals.

## Strategy

This is essentially finding the minimum number of "cuts" needed to separate all the requested pairs.

A better approach: we can use a sweep line technique or think in terms of intervals.

Let me consider what happens when we have multiple requests:
- Each request (a, b) means we need to block the path from a to b
- The optimal solution is to find a set of bridges to remove such that every request spans at least one removed bridge

Actually, this is equivalent to finding the minimum number of bridges to remove so that for each request (a, b), there's a removed bridge between some position i and i+1 where a ≤ i < b.

This becomes a problem of finding the minimum number of cuts to "separate" all the intervals.

## Optimal Approach

We can use a greedy approach:
1. Sort the requests by their right endpoint (b_i)
2. Process requests in order of increasing b_i
3. For each request, if the current island range is already blocked, skip; otherwise, find the leftmost bridge we need to remove to block this request

Alternatively, think of it as: we want to find the minimum number of bridge removals such that every request (a,b) has at least one removed bridge in the range [a, b-1].

This is equivalent to finding the minimum number of points to cover all intervals such that each interval contains at least one point.

This is the classic "interval covering" problem, which can be solved by sorting intervals by their end points and greedily selecting points.

## Algorithm

1. Sort all requests by b_i (the end point)
2. Initialize a counter for bridge removals
3. For each request (a, b), if we haven't already blocked this interval, remove the bridge just before b (i.e., between b-1 and b)
4. This ensures that any request (a,b) will have its path broken

Wait, let me reconsider. If we remove bridge between i and i+1, it breaks all paths that cross between i and i+1.

So for request (a,b), we need to have a removed bridge at some position i where a ≤ i < b.

The greedy approach: sort requests by b_i, and for each request, if the current "gap" is not sufficient, remove the bridge just before b_i (at position b_i-1).

This ensures we minimize the number of removals.

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their end point (b)
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

# We'll use greedy: for each request, if it's not already blocked by previous removals, remove a bridge
# Remove bridge just before b (between b-1 and b)
removed = set()
count = 0

for a, b in requests:
    # Check if this request is already blocked by any previous removal
    # A request (a,b) is blocked if there's a removed bridge at position i where a <= i < b
    blocked = False
    for pos in removed:
        if a <= pos < b:
            blocked = True
            break
    
    if not blocked:
        # Remove bridge between pos and pos+1 where pos = b-1
        # This removes bridge between (b-1) and b
        removed.add(b - 1)
        count += 1

print(count)
```

Wait, this might be inefficient with O(M^2) complexity. Let me optimize.

Since we're processing in order of increasing b, and when we add a removal at position b-1, it will block all future requests that have a <= b-1 < b' (where b' is the next end point).

We can optimize by noting that when we process (a,b), we need to check if any previous removal at position p where a <= p < b exists.

But we can do better: since we're processing by increasing b, if we haven't blocked this request, we add a removal at b-1.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their end point (b)
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

# Keep track of removed bridges (positions where we removed bridge between pos and pos+1)
removed_positions = set()
count = 0

for a, b in requests:
    # Check if this interval (a,b) is already blocked by any removed bridge
    blocked = False
    for pos in removed_positions:
        if a <= pos < b:
            blocked = True
            break
    
    if not blocked:
        # Add removal at position b-1 (bridge between b-1 and b)
        removed_positions.add(b - 1)
        count += 1

print(count)
```

This is O(M^2) in worst case. Let me optimize further.

Actually, since we're processing in order of increasing b, and we only add removals at positions that are b-1, we can notice that if a request (a,b) is not blocked, we add removal at b-1.

But checking if any previous removal is in [a, b-1] still takes O(number of previous removals).

We can maintain the maximum position that has been removed so far? No, that doesn't help directly.

Actually, we can keep track of the positions and since the requests are sorted by b, we can maintain the set of removed positions and just check membership.

For worst case, if we have many requests with very close b values, this could be O(M^2). 

But given the constraints (M ≤ 10^5), O(M^2) might be too slow.

Alternative insight: this is equivalent to finding the minimum number of points such that every interval (a,b) contains at least one point.

This is the "minimum hitting set" for intervals, and it can be solved in O(M log M) by greedy:

1. Sort intervals by end point
2. For each interval, if it doesn't contain any previously selected point, select its end point

In our case, selecting point = b-1 (the bridge just before b).

But when we select a point p, it "covers" all intervals that contain p.

So algorithm:
- Sort intervals by b
- Initialize result = 0
- For each interval (a,b) in sorted order:
  - If no previous selection covers this interval (i.e., no selected point p where a ≤ p < b), then select point p = b-1 and increment result

This is correct and efficient enough since each interval is processed once, and each selection covers some intervals.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their end point (b)
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

# Keep track of the positions of removed bridges
# Actually, we don't need to store all, just use a set
removed_positions = set()
count = 0

for a, b in requests:
    # Check if any removed bridge position is in the range [a, b-1]
    blocked = False
    for pos in removed_positions:
        if a <= pos < b:
            blocked = True
            break
    
    if not blocked:
        # Remove bridge between (b-1) and b
        removed_positions.add(b - 1)
        count += 1

print(count)
```

- Sort by b: (1,4), (2,5)
- Process (1,4): no removals, so add removal at 4-1 = 3 (bridge between 3 and 4)
- Process (2,5): check if any removal in [2,4]. Position 3 is in [2,4], so already blocked.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have N islands in a line connected by N-1 bridges (each bridge connects consecutive islands). We have M requests where each request wants to make travel between two islands impossible. We need to find the minimum number of bridges to remove to satisfy all requests.

## Key Insight

This is essentially a problem about finding the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect certain pairs of nodes. 

The crucial observation is that we want to avoid removing bridges unnecessarily. Two requests (a₁,b₁) and (a₂,b₂) can potentially be satisfied by removing just one bridge if their intervals overlap in a specific way.

## Strategy

1. Each request (a,b) means we want to disconnect island a from island b, which means we need to remove at least one bridge between them.

2. If we have multiple overlapping requests, we can satisfy them with a single bridge removal.

3. The key insight is that if we have requests that cover overlapping ranges, we can satisfy multiple requests by removing one bridge.

4. This becomes a problem about finding the minimum number of "cut points" such that every request interval contains at least one cut point.

5. This is equivalent to finding the minimum number of points such that every interval contains at least one of them - this is the "minimum covering points" problem.

6. The optimal strategy is to use a greedy approach: sort the intervals by their right endpoint, and for each interval that doesn't contain any existing cut point, place a cut at its right endpoint.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, if we have a request between islands a and b, we need to remove a bridge in the range [a, b]. If two requests have overlapping ranges, we might be able to satisfy both with one bridge removal.

The optimal approach is:
1. Sort the requests by their right endpoint b_i
2. Process requests in order of increasing right endpoint
3. For each request, if there's no bridge removed between a_i and b_i, we need to remove a bridge at position b_i (to disconnect b_i from all islands on its right)

Actually, let me think again. If we have a request (a,b), removing bridge k (between island k and k+1) will disconnect any pair where one island is ≤ k and the other is > k.

A bridge at position k (connecting island k and k+1) will block all paths between islands that straddle this bridge.

So for a request (a,b), we need to remove a bridge at position k where a ≤ k < b.

The goal is to find the minimum number of bridges such that for every request (a_i,b_i), there exists some k in [a_i, b_i-1] that we've removed.

This is the "minimum points to intersect all intervals" problem.

## Algorithm

1. Sort the requests by their b_i values (right endpoint)
2. Initialize a counter for bridges to remove
3. Process each request in order of increasing b_i
4. For each request (a,b), if we haven't already removed a bridge in the range [a,b), we remove a bridge at position b-1 (the rightmost position in the range)
5. This greedy approach works because by processing in order of b_i, we ensure that if a request has a small b_i, we satisfy it early with a cut at b_i-1, and this cut might also satisfy future requests with larger b_i.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their right endpoint
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

# Track the maximum position where we've already removed a bridge
# We'll use a variable to track the last bridge we removed
# If we haven't removed any bridge in [a, b), we need to remove one
removed_positions = set()

count = 0
for a, b in requests:
    # Check if there's already a removed bridge in the range [a, b-1]
    # A bridge at position k (between island k and k+1) is in range [a, b-1] if a <= k < b
    found = False
    for pos in removed_positions:
        if a <= pos < b:
            found = True
            break
    
    if not found:
        # Remove a bridge at position b-1 (between island b-1 and b)
        removed_positions.add(b - 1)
        count += 1

print(count)
```

Wait, this has O(M²) complexity which might be too slow for the constraints (M up to 10⁵). Let me optimize.

We can use a more efficient approach:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their right endpoint
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

# Track the maximum rightmost position of previously removed bridges
# After removing a bridge at position p, it covers all intervals [a, b) where a <= p < b
# We only need to remove a bridge when the current request doesn't get covered
last_removed_bridge = -1  # last removed bridge position
count = 0

for a, b in requests:
    # If there's already a removed bridge in [a, b-1], we're good
    # A bridge at position p satisfies this if a <= p < b
    if last_removed_bridge >= a and last_removed_bridge < b:
        # This request is already covered by a previously removed bridge
        continue
    else:
        # We need to remove a bridge
        # To minimize the number of removals, we remove it at position b-1
        # This covers all future requests with b' >= b and a' <= b-1
        last_removed_bridge = b - 1
        count += 1

print(count)
```

Wait, this is still wrong. If we remove a bridge at position b-1, it only covers requests (a,b) where a ≤ b-1 < b, which is always true for any request (a,b) with a ≤ b-1.

The issue is that a bridge at position k covers all requests (a,b) where a ≤ k < b.

So if we process requests in order of increasing b, and for each request, if no existing removed bridge satisfies a ≤ k < b, we add a new bridge at position b-1.

This means we only need to check if any existing bridge satisfies the condition.

But we don't need to check all existing bridges - we just need to know if any bridge in the set {k | k ≥ a and k < b} exists.

Actually, we can maintain the maximum bridge position we've already removed.

If we have removed bridges at positions p1, p2, ..., pk, then any request (a,b) is satisfied if max(removed) ≥ a and max(removed) < b.

No - that's incorrect. A bridge at position p covers request (a,b) if a ≤ p < b. So if we have a bridge at position p, it covers (a,b) if a ≤ p < b.

But with multiple bridges, we only need one bridge in the range [a, b-1].

The key insight is: if we have already removed a bridge at position k, and k >= a and k < b, then this request is satisfied.

We can maintain the maximum of all removed bridge positions. If this maximum is >= a and < b, then the request is satisfied.

Actually, we don't need to know the exact maximum - we just need to check if any removed bridge is in [a, b-1].

But since we process by increasing b, and we remove bridges at b-1 positions, the removed bridges are at positions: b1-1, b2-1, ... where b1 ≤ b2 ≤ ...

The maximum removed position is the last one we removed.

However, this maximum might not be in the range [a, b-1] for the current request.

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we process requests in order of increasing b, when we get to a request (a,b), any bridge we've removed at position p where p >= a and p < b would satisfy this request.

But since we remove bridges at positions b_i-1, and we process in increasing order of b_i, the removed bridges are at positions: b₁-1, b₂-1, ..., b_k-1 with b₁ ≤ b₂ ≤ ...

For the current request (a,b), if there's any removed bridge at position p where a ≤ p < b, we're good.

Since the removed positions are b_i-1 for some previous requests, we need to check if any b_j-1 satisfies a ≤ b_j-1 < b.

This is equivalent to checking if there's any j such that a ≤ b_j-1 < b, which means a+1 ≤ b_j < b+1.

But since we process in increasing order of b_j, we only need to check the last few entries.

Actually, the optimal strategy is to check if the maximum removed position is >= a and < b.

But that's not sufficient - we need to know if any removed position is in [a, b-1].

But since we only remove at b_j-1 positions, and we process in order of increasing b_j, the set of removed positions is {b_1-1, b_2-1, ..., b_k-1} with b_1 ≤ b_2 ≤ ... ≤ b_k.

For the current request (a,b), it is satisfied if max{b_j-1 | b_j < b} >= a.

Wait, no. It's satisfied if there exists j such that a ≤ b_j-1 < b, which means a+1 ≤ b_j < b+1.

For b_j < b, this means b_j ≤ b-1.

So we need to know if there's any j such that b_j > a and b_j < b.

Since the b_j are sorted, we can use a greedy approach: if the last removed bridge position (b_last-1) is >= a and < b, we're good. Otherwise, we need to remove a new bridge at b-1.

But is this sufficient? Let me think.

Actually, if we have a bridge at position p, it covers all requests (a,b) where a ≤ p < b.

So when processing a new request (a,b), if there's already a bridge at position p in the range [a, b-1], we skip.

Otherwise, we add a bridge at position b-1.

Since we process in order of increasing b, the existing bridges are at positions p_j-1 where p_j-1 < b (since we only process b in increasing order).

The maximum p_j-1 might not be in [a, b-1] - for example, if a is large and all existing bridges are at low positions.

But we don't need the maximum - we need to know if any bridge is in [a, b-1].

However, since we process in order of b, and we only remove at b-1 positions, the bridges are at positions that are less than b.

We need to know if any of the previously removed positions is in [a, b-1].

But we can maintain the maximum removed position. If max_removed >= a and max_removed < b, then we have a bridge at position max_removed which is >= a and < b, so it works.

But if max_removed < a, then no removed bridge satisfies a ≤ p < b.

So the algorithm is:

1. Sort requests by b
2. Initialize max_removed = -1
3. For each request (a,b) in sorted order:
   - If max_removed >= a and max_removed < b, skip (already covered)
   - Else, remove a bridge at position b-1, update max_removed = b-1, count += 1

But wait, if max_removed >= a and max_removed < b, then we have a bridge at position p where a <= p < b, which satisfies this request.

If max_removed < a, then the last removed bridge is not in [a, b-1], and we need to add a new bridge.

But since the bridge positions are increasing, and we're removing at b-1, we should add a bridge at b-1.

Actually, if max_removed < a, we need to add a bridge, and the new max_removed becomes b-1.

So the algorithm is correct.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
requests = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    requests.append((a, b))

# Sort requests by their right endpoint
requests.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

max_removed_position = -1
count = 0

for a, b in requests:
    # Check if any previously removed bridge satisfies a <= k < b
    if max_removed_position >= a and max_removed_position < b:
        # Already covered by existing bridge(s)
        continue
    else:
        # Need to remove a new bridge at position b-1
        max_removed_position = b - 1
        count += 1

print(count)
```
